# 05 — RED TEAM

## Summary

**Bottom line.** Two red-team rounds raised 186 objections against seven deep dives, and none was refuted. Five deep dives were killed and two were cut to part-time side lines. No surviving plan pays the founder a salary within 36 months in any price scenario. So the Phase 6 picks are side businesses, not ventures (`research/phase6/decision.json`).

### What was attacked, and by whom

Four adversarial personas attacked every deep dive independently: **VET**, a 30-year DRAM process veteran; **VC**, a semiconductor VC who lost money on Z-RAM, T-RAM, Nantero and Crossbar; **HYP**, a top-5-cloud memory procurement lead; and **PHY**, a condensed-matter physicist.

- **Round 1** covered DD1–DD5 and raised 127 objections.
- **Round 2** covered DD6–DD7 and raised 59. It was run because round 1 left no PRIMARY or MOONSHOT candidate (`03_SCORING.md` §9).

Of the 186, 11 were FATAL, 112 MAJOR and 63 MINOR. Dispositions: 0 refuted, 42 mitigated, 111 downgraded, 33 killed (Scoreboard below).

### Outcome

| DD2 Lifecycle intelligence | DOWNGRADED; now PRIMARY | $149,629 | Never (needs ≥14.7% trial conversion; modelled 10%) | RT-VC-DD2-01: the panel reaches 14.2 of the 15 contributors needed, so the index, the bundle's moat, is never published |
| DD3 NV continuity cards | KILLED | $39,890 run-rate ($2.7k recurring) | Never | RT-HYP-DD3-01: i-series controls already back up to a $20 CF card; without them and the unfound DMSMS orders, demand is ~2,591 cards/yr |

### Failure modes that recurred

1. **The buyer already had the asset for free.** Module makers hold die-source invoices and can read package top marks (DD1). MCM makers bank their own die, and Micross sells the same bundle direct (DD7). Proxmox already runs KSM (DD4). Fanuc i-series controls take a $20 CF card (DD3).
2. **A free or cheaper substitute was already in the channel.**
   - Distributors and Octopart give away the lifecycle data DD2 charges $249/month for (HYP and VC verdicts).
   - Free OpenRAM code keeps improving, and its authors are DD5's likely contractors (VC verdict; RT-HYP-DD5-04).
   - A $44.6k two-node Mac Studio cluster matches the single-stream speed of DD6's $78,708 L box, per published runs (RT-HYP-DD6-03).
3. **The models hid capacity limits.**
   - DD6's revenue never read the box's capacity (VET verdict).
   - DD2 sold index licences whatever the panel size (RT-HYP-DD2-01).
   - DD4's $750/month of 24/7 on-call buys about 4 contractor hours (RT-VET-DD4-04).
   - Part-time hours cap DD2 at ~20 trials a month (RT-VC-DD2-06). DD5 can reach only 19 new teams a month, because no more exist (RT-HYP-DD5-01).
4. **Demand inputs were estimates.**
   - DD5: 150 of the 298 projects a year (RT-VC-DD5-01).
   - DD3: two DMSMS orders nobody found, which carried $48k of the $56.2k m36 recurring ARR (RT-VC-DD3-04).
   - DD2: a 6,000-OEM market, now 3,000 and still an estimate (RT-VC-DD2-04).
   - DD1: an unmeasured 7%→10% close-rate lift (RT-VC-DD1-02).
5. **Regulated buyers will not take a part-time founder on trust.**
   - Their supplier onboarding is hard for a part-time single-member LLC to pass (DD6 revised verdict).
   - Vendor approval needs a named on-call rotation (RT-HYP-DD4-04).
   - Defense work needs NIST SP 800-171 or AS9120B, ~$35k each (DD7).
   - The day job adds an IP-assignment risk, which became DD2's K0 gate (RT-VC-DD2-05).

### What the red team got wrong or could not test

- **Evidence on both sides is snippet-grade.** Deep-dive web facts are [REPORTED] at best (`lifecycle-intel.md` header). Some objections cite a datasheet "as I recall it; verify" (RT-VET-DD3-03) or [UNVERIFIED-training] figures (RT-HYP-DD3-06).
- **It replaced estimates with smaller estimates.** Neither side has a deposit, PO or paid pilot (`decision.json`, biggest_unknown). Cutting conversion from 12% to 10%, or OEMs from 6,000 to 3,000, is more cautious, not better evidenced. The kill tests are the first real demand data.
- **Some objections overstated their case.** DD6 needs ~461 GB/s on routed-only bytes, not ~620 GB/s, though the verdict holds (RT-VET-DD6-01). DD5 recorded two factual corrections: Cadence did offer a ReRAM option in 2026 (RT-VET-DD5-05), and a 6-month slip on the Phase 4 inputs still paid the founder at m21 (RT-VC-DD5-04).
- **Zero refutations out of 186 is a warning sign.** The same study wrote the deep dives and the responses, so concessions may have come too easily. Re-running each concession in a model reduces that risk but does not remove it.
- **No persona was a buyer of either survivor.** None is an SMB-OEM purchaser (DD2) or a university tapeout team (DD5).
- **Some physics was never tested.** No one has measured DD6's throughput on the exact box, tested DD3's magnetic immunity above 8,000 A/m, or silicon-verified a 16 KB-plus sky130 macro (DD5).
- **Eleven surviving concepts were never deep-dived or red-teamed** (`SUMMARY_TABLE.md`). They include C-36, which the integrity judge calls "alive only because it was never red-teamed."

### How the outcome changed the decision

Round 1 killed DD1, DD3 and DD4 and cut DD2 and DD5 to side lines. The study then ran round 2 on the strongest untested survivors (`03_SCORING.md` §9). DD6, the best-fit hardware concept and the only one a glut helps, was the PRIMARY candidate; DD7, the strongest T2+ path, was the MOONSHOT candidate. Round 2 killed both: DD6 nets −$29,411 over 36 months and needs a $25k bridge, and DD7's Plan B returns 0.29× on $11.49M.

The three judges then reached the same picks independently (`research/phase6/judge_*.json`):

- **PRIMARY: DD2, SaaS only.** It has the cheapest decisive test (~$2,950 over 29 days) and 57% gross margin by m36, and its lifecycle module holds flat at $5,774/month in the glut. If K0, K1 or K6 fails, the PRIMARY becomes NONE.
- **FALLBACK: DD5, the wedge only, run after DD2 rather than alongside it.** Its buyers do not overlap with DD2's. If it passes its test, it is the only survivor that ends 36 months above its starting $40k in all three price scenarios.
- **MOONSHOT: none qualifies.** The killed index (C-86) is kept as a ~$0 option inside DD2's test.

The response files' "not a PRIMARY" and "not FALLBACK" lines predate round 2; the picks do not upgrade either concept.

## Method

Four adversarial reviewers attacked every deep dive independently, in two rounds (round 1: DD1–DD5, `rt_*.json`; round 2: DD6–DD7, `rt2_*.json`): **HYP** — DRAM and server procurement lead at a top-5 cloud: runs supplier qualification, ITAD/decommissioning contracts and component-compliance programs (NDAA §889, §5949 readiness), **PHY** — Condensed-matter physicist (ferroelectrics, spintronics, oxide semiconductors, memory device reliability); IEDM and Nature Electronics reviewer, **VC** — Semiconductor VC who lost money on Z-RAM, T-RAM, Nantero, Crossbar and an Optane-adjacent bet, and on picks-and-shovels SaaS that capped out at $2M ARR, **VET** — 30-year DRAM process veteran: ex-Micron/Qimonda process integration and yield engineering, qualified memory for automotive and defense customers, ran fab test floors (KGD, burn-in, AS6081, module qual). Every objection is reproduced **verbatim** below with the deep-dive owner's response, a disposition (REFUTED / ACCEPTED-MITIGATED / ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE / ACCEPTED-KILL) and the action taken. Objections are answered, never deleted. This file is generated by `research/tools/compile_redteam.py` from `research/phase5/rt_*.json` and `research/phase5/responses_*.json`.

## DD1 — Provenance & Recertified Memory (provenance-recert)

**Final status after red team: KILLED.** The three FATAL objections hold: the named core buyer (OEM-compatible module makers) already knows its die source from POs, CoCs and package top marks and may self-certify under the proposed FAR rule, and resellers may rely on that free certificate. The die-source library was sampled from the wrong population (old OEM DDR4), keyed wrongly (module P/N) and replicable, so the moat is gone; the C-85 predictive subscription is dropped. With all accepted revisions the recert 'cash engine' earns -1% per module (it needs lots at <=62% of resale for 8%), and the revised BASE reaches only $58k ARR at m36 with the founder never full-time; even if both kill tracks pass, BASE is $227k ARR with full-time at m32 and GLUT/SEVERE never pay the founder.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: Yes, with corrections. Recert is a TRL-9 trade, and CXMT die can be identified at die level (TechInsights analysed a CXMT G4 16Gb die even inside a Powev-marked package). But the module-P/N cross-reference is physically invalid (multi-sourced P/Ns, assembly-week date codes), memtest86+ burn-in cannot see ECC-corrected faults (CE logging plus a heated soak is required), X-ray die outline is only triage, and most AS6171 labs may lack CXMT reference images. · Profitable: No at post-red-team central inputs (revised after red team). Recert earns -$3/module (-1%) at buy 0.70 / B2B 0.80 / 7% fallout. CleanBOM reaches 5.3 subscribers and $58,335 ARR at m36 (same in BASE, GLUT and SEVERE), cumulative EBITDA is -$3,473, the cash low is -$5,863 at m18, and there is no breakeven at a $10k founder salary. If both kill tracks pass (lots <=0.62, a cash-paying module maker), the best case is $226,833 ARR at m36 and full-time at m32: lifestyle at best, and never full-time in GLUT/SEVERE. · Doable by this founder: Mechanically yes (T0, part-time, operations and software work the founder can do), but at central inputs there is nothing worth doing: no version puts the founder full-time inside the 36-month ceiling except the both-tracks-pass case (m32), and the federal sales motion they have never run is still the gating skill.

### RT-HYP-DD1-01 — FATAL — HYP — §3 target customer / §5 win conditions / §7 §5949 row

> The core CleanBOM buyer (AddOn, Axiom, Brute) is the one party in the chain that already knows its die source. It buys DRAM components, or finished modules from an ODM, under POs that name the DRAM maker. A CXMT package that has not been remarked carries CXMT's own marking; the DD's own router-switch example is the CXMT module part CXMR4E464R8AS-CB1C. Under proposed 52.240-XX, 'reasonable inquiry' lets that maker certify from its supplier declaration, and every downstream SEWP/GSA reseller may then rely on its certificate (Mayer Brown and Covington, cited in §7). So no party both lacks the knowledge and is barred from relying on a free upstream certificate, apart from refurb and odd-lot resellers, the smallest and most price-sensitive segment. In my program, §889-style compliance runs on contract flow-down plus supplier declarations at zero marginal cost. 01_LANDSCAPE §10.2 already classes die-level screening as an exception, and my 2026-11-01 search found no final FAR rule tightening the standard.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* In §10 track C, at least one module maker (not a reseller) pays cash and states in writing that its ODM or component supplier refuses to give a die-source declaration. Alternatively, a final FAR rule that removes reliance on supplier certification for COTS memory.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and I cannot defeat it. The named core buyers (AddOn, Axiom, Brute) buy DRAM components, or finished modules from an ODM, under POs and CoCs that name the DRAM maker. An un-remarked CXMT package carries CXMT's own marking. Under proposed 52.240-XX a reasonable inquiry may rest on that supplier declaration, and every SEWP/GSA reseller downstream may rely on the module maker's certificate at zero marginal cost. The only partial rebuttal I found is weak. TechInsights' CXMT G4 16Gb DDR5 analysis used a die from a package marked 'Powev H2G08SC522-6AF' on a Gloway module (snippet, 2026-11-01), so CXMT die can ship under a non-CXMT top mark. But that is a consumer-channel case, the module maker still knows what it bought, and I again found no case of CXMT die relabeled inside a U.S. server RDIMM. A search for a standard §5949 supplier declaration form also found none, which suggests a paperwork gap rather than a die-evidence gap. What remains is refurb/odd-lot resellers and white-box builders, the smallest and most price-sensitive segment. Model: removing the module-maker demand alone (close 10%→5%, high tier 35%→10%, packs 15%→10%) on Phase 4 inputs cuts m36 ARR from $445,543 to $187,670 (m§8b). In the full revised model CleanBOM reaches 5.3 subscribers and $58,335 ARR at m36.

*Action taken:* Killed leg: CleanBOM as 'the company', and its $2,500 module-maker die-source tier. The high tier is now white-box builders only (cb_hi_share 0.35→0.10); cb_close 0.10→0.05; cb_pack_rate 0.15→0.10. Track C is tightened to HYP's own test: at least one module maker (not a reseller) pays cash and states in writing that its ODM or component supplier refuses a die-source declaration. Without that, CleanBOM is not built. DD §0, §2, §10, §11 #2 and §14 updated; final status KILLED.

### RT-VC-DD1-01 — FATAL — VC — §3 target customer / §5 'free self-certification' / §7 §5949 row / INPUTS cb_close_bundle

> The named core buyer for CleanBOM (OEM-compatible module makers: AddOn, Axiom, Brute) is the one party in the chain that already holds die-source evidence for free: its own DRAM purchase invoices or its module ODM's certificate of conformance. Under the proposed FAR 52.240-XX it certifies after a 'reasonable inquiry' and may rely on lower-tier supplier certifications with no third-party audit (Mayer Brown, Covington, Reed Smith as cited in §7), and the SEWP/GSA resellers downstream may in turn rely on the module maker's certification. So the party with the liability already has the data, and the parties without it are allowed to rely on the first party. 01_LANDSCAPE §10.2 already lists '§5949 die-level lab screening (X-ray/decap) as the core product' as a closed path, yet DD1's moat is a lab-built library, and the model still books a 10% close rate with demand held flat in every scenario.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* At least 3 of 5 compatible-memory makers (AddOn/Axiom/Brute class) state in writing that they cannot obtain die-source certifications from their ODMs or die suppliers and will pay at least $750/month for independent evidence. A final FAR rule that bars reliance on supplier certification for memory modules would also do it.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Same mechanism as RT-HYP-DD1-01, and it is valid. The party with the liability (the module maker) already holds die-source evidence for free: its DRAM invoices or its ODM's CoC. The proposed rule lets it certify on that, and lets downstream resellers rely on it. 01_LANDSCAPE §10.2 had already closed '§5949 die-level lab screening as the core product'. DD1 tried to escape that by making the lab-built library the moat, and the library objections (RT-PHY-DD1-01, RT-VC-DD1-02, RT-HYP-DD1-02) remove that escape. Holding demand flat at a 10% close was the model's single most generous CleanBOM input. Numbers: Phase 4 + CleanBOM demand revision alone gives $187,670 ARR at m36 (from $445,543). Revised full model: 5.3 subscribers, $58,335 ARR, founder never full-time. VC's would-change-my-mind test (3 of 5 compatible-memory makers state in writing they cannot get die-source certifications and will pay ≥$750/month) has not been run. My prior that it passes is low (about 10-15%).

*Action taken:* Same killed leg as RT-HYP-DD1-01. Kill criterion 2 now requires ≥1 cash-paying module maker plus a written statement that die-source declarations are unobtainable for ≥20% of federal SKUs, in addition to ≥3 of 15 committing. The revised resurrection test runs track C first and alone. Model close and tier inputs revised (RED-TEAM REVISIONS).

### RT-VET-DD1-01 — FATAL — VET — §1 Line 3 / §3 core buyer / §5 win condition 2 (die-level library)

> Whoever holds a module can read the die maker off the package top mark: Samsung K4..., SK hynix H5.../HMC..., Micron FBGA codes (decodable on micron.com), CXMT CXDB.../logo. Module makers like AddOn, Axiom and Brute also hold the component invoice and CoC. Under the proposed FAR rule their 'reasonable inquiry' is a supplier CoC plus a visual top-mark check. That is a $0 internal SOP, as 01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 8/F4 already concedes ('lab test is an exception-driven add-on'). Decap adds information only when remarking is suspected, and §5 admits no case of CXMT die relabeled inside U.S. server RDIMMs was found. So the $2,500/month tier (35% of customers, m§1c) sells module makers information they already own, and the library is not the moat the Phase 3 VC asked for.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* In track C, at least 3 of the 5 named module makers or resellers state in writing that, for 20% or more of the SKUs they ship federally, they cannot establish die source from their own purchase records plus top-mark inspection. At least one of them also pays for a $900 pack on such a SKU.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid. Whoever holds the module can read the package top mark: Samsung K4..., SK hynix H5..., Micron FBGA codes (decodable on micron.com), CXMT CXDB... Module makers also hold invoices and CoCs. For un-remarked parts a microscope and the SPD JEP106 bytes are a $0 internal SOP, which is exactly what 01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 8/F4 says. My only counter-evidence is the Powev-marked CXMT package (TechInsights, snippet): CXMT die does sometimes ship under a third-party package brand, so a top mark is not always the die maker. That helps a downstream reseller who cannot see the supply chain. But that reseller may rely on the upstream certificate. And it is a consumer case, with no U.S. server-RDIMM instance found. So decap adds information only for remarked or third-party-branded packages, which is exception testing that AS6171 labs already sell. The $2,500 tier sold module makers information they already own.

*Action taken:* Killed leg: module-maker tier and the 'library is the moat' claim. The evidence pack is redesigned: default = package top-mark images + SPD bytes + X-ray die-outline triage; decap only on exceptions (third-party package brands, mismatches). Track C adds VET's test: 3 of 5 module makers or resellers state that for ≥20% of federal SKUs they cannot establish die source from purchase records plus top-mark inspection, and one pays for a $900 pack on such a SKU.

### RT-HYP-DD1-02 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 Line 2 / §2.3 library lift / INPUTS cb_close_bundle 0.10 vs cb_close_standalone 0.07

> The library is sampled from the wrong population. Servers decommissioned in 2026-27 are mostly 2019-21 builds, since hyperscale fleets run 5-6-year lives. Their DDR4 is Samsung, SK hynix or Micron. CXMT server RDIMMs only reached the U.S. channel in 2025-26 (the router-switch DDR5 listing in §3). So the recert bench will yield close to zero CXMT positives. Recovered DIMMs are also barred from DoD deliverables (§7 DFARS row), so nobody needs to certify the modules being sampled. The 3-point close-rate lift is worth $122k of m36 ARR ('no evidence lift' gives $323,558 vs $445,543, §2.3), and nothing in the plan produces it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A sampling plan, costed in the model, that buys currently sold new third-party DDR5 RDIMMs rather than recert flow, with at least 1 CXMT-positive among the 4 track-B samples. Or a re-run with cb_close_bundle equal to cb_close_standalone and standalone sample costs.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid. Servers decommissioned in 2026-27 are 2019-21 builds; their DDR4 is Samsung, SK hynix or Micron, and CXMT server RDIMMs only reached the U.S. 'compatible' channel in 2025-26. The bench will produce close to zero CXMT positives, and recovered DIMMs are barred from DoD deliverables anyway. The 3-point close-rate lift ($121,985 of Phase 4 m36 ARR) had no mechanism behind it. I ran HYP's second test: with cb_close_bundle equal to standalone and the bundle paying for samples, Phase 4 m36 ARR is $323,558 (the published 'no lift' row). With samples bought new at $1,000 it is $290,023, and subscribers at m18 fall to 2.9. The bundle hypothesis that 'recert is how CleanBOM gets die-source data' fails on sample selection.

*Action taken:* Killed leg: the recert-fed die-source library and the bundle close-rate lift. Model: lib_bundle_pays_module False→True, lib_module_cost $150→$600 (new third-party DDR5 RDIMMs), cb_close_bundle = cb_close_standalone = 0.05. Library samples come from currently sold third-party DDR5 RDIMMs, not recert flow. DD §1, §2.3 and §14 updated.

### RT-HYP-DD1-03 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 Line 2 ('one result identifies the die source for every stick sharing that part number and date-code family') / INPUTS cb_samples_per_sub, lab_cost

> Third-party module makers multi-source DRAM under one module part number, and they switch die vendor lot by lot as supply allows. Consumer buyers call this the 'die lottery', and some brands encode the die vendor in version numbers [UNVERIFIED-training]. In a shortage where CXMT is the marginal open-market supplier, that switching is exactly what happens. A decap result then holds for one lot, not for a part-number and date-code family. Lab cost therefore scales with lots shipped, not part numbers, and '63 lab-confirmed part numbers by m36' is not coverage. INPUTS cb_samples_per_sub 0.4/month at a $400 lab_cost understates what a defensible evidence pack costs.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Data from track B or the first 3 months showing that, for at least 2 module part numbers from at least 2 vendors, the die source stays the same across at least 3 date codes or lots.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Third-party module makers multi-source DRAM under one module part number and switch die vendor lot by lot as supply allows. In a shortage where CXMT is the marginal open-market supplier, that switching is the expected behavior. One decap therefore covers one build lot, not a P/N plus date-code family. Lab cost scales with lots shipped, and '63 lab-confirmed part numbers' was never coverage. I have no data showing within-family stability (HYP's test: ≥2 P/Ns from ≥2 vendors stable across ≥3 date codes).

*Action taken:* Library re-keyed from module P/N + date code to per-lot DRAM package marking (FBGA/marking code) + die-outline fingerprint. Evidence packs state that they apply only to the lot sampled. Lab use is limited to exceptions and validation (lib_samples_early 3→1). Track B adds HYP's stability test when samples allow. The reusable cross-reference that justified a moat no longer exists.

### RT-HYP-DD1-04 — MAJOR — HYP — INPUTS buy_frac 0.65 / units_cap_ft 600 / §10 track A

> Hyperscalers are now keeping the DDR4 that this model plans to buy. Meta runs retired-server DDR4 behind its Vistara CXL controller in production (StorageNewsletter 2026-07-02; CXL Consortium deck 2026-07). Marvell markets Structera X for DDR4 reuse, Sims redeploys recovered DDR4 into AI/HPC, and Google is reported to do the same (unverified). The DDR4 that still reaches ITADs goes out under ITRenew/Sims-class contracts in lots of thousands, on credit, to established brokers. A buyer of $3.5k lots is a broker's customer, not an ITAD's. buy_frac 0.65 has no source, and at 0.75 ARR at m36 is $250,221 and recert no longer funds the founder (m§5). A single spot-lot quote in track A does not test monthly supply.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Written ITAD quotes at no more than 0.70 of realized resale for at least 200 modules a month with a 6-month supply commitment, not a single spot lot, from 2 of the 3 named ITADs.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. buy_frac 0.65 had no source; a second search on 2026-11-01 again found no ITAD buyback or lot price, only asking prices (cheapest 32GB DDR4 RDIMM listing $148.95 on 2026-08-19; median listing $7.49/GB, DatacenterDisk snippet). Hyperscalers are keeping DDR4: Meta runs retired DDR4 behind Vistara CXL, Marvell markets Structera X for DDR4 reuse, and Sims redeploys DDR4 (HYP's sources). A $3.5k-lot buyer is a broker's customer. Phase 4 + buy 0.70 + caps 300/450 alone gives $23/module (9%), m36 ARR $341,342 and full-time at m25. At all revised inputs, the 8% contribution that track A requires needs lots at ≤62% of realized resale; breakeven is 69% and the 5% buy line is 65% (m§6g).

*Action taken:* buy_frac 0.65→0.70; units_cap_pt 400→300; units_cap_ft 600→450. Track A now needs written quotes at ≤0.62 of trailing realized resale for ≥200 modules/month with a 6-month supply commitment from 2 of the 3 named ITADs, on downstream terms a non-R2 buyer can meet. HYP's test was ≤0.70; mine is tighter because the other revisions moved breakeven down. Kill criterion 1 revised.

### RT-HYP-DD1-05 — MAJOR — HYP — INPUTS b2b_price_factor 0.88 vs buy_frac 0.65 / ebay_share_m12 0.40

> The B2B accounts compete with the founder for the same lots. xByte, TechMikeNY, PCSP and the Park Place and Curvature spares desks buy ITAD lots directly and test in-house. They will not pay 0.88× eBay realized for a module they can buy at about 0.65× and test themselves; their ceiling is their own landed cost, about 0.75-0.80. The model moves 60% of volume to B2B by m12. My re-runs: at b2b_price_factor 0.80, BASE ARR at m36 is $391k, the founder goes full-time at m20, and m36 cash is $367k. At 0.75, BASE ARR is $341k (full-time m25) and GLUT $232k (full-time m34). At 0.80 combined with buy_frac 0.70, ARR is $250k, with full-time at m33 and only 4 full-time months.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two written B2B purchase offers from accounts #6-9 at no less than 0.85× eBay realized, for at least 100 modules a month.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. xByte, TechMikeNY, PCSP and the Park Place/Curvature desks buy ITAD lots and test in-house. Their ceiling is their own landed cost, about 0.75-0.80, not 0.88× eBay. Re-running HYP's numbers on Phase 4 inputs confirms each one: at 0.80, $391,481 ARR, full-time m20, $367,204 cash; at 0.75, $341,342 (m25), GLUT $232,377 (m34); 0.80 with buy 0.70 gives $250,221 (m33). With buy at 0.70, B2B at 0.80 earns −$9/module (−6%). B2B is a liquidity channel, not a margin channel, unless the founder buys cheaper than the accounts themselves.

*Action taken:* b2b_price_factor 0.88→0.80; ebay_share_m12 0.40→0.50 (volume shifts back to eBay). Track A adds HYP's test: two written B2B offers at ≥0.85× eBay realized for ≥100 modules/month. Without them, recert is planned eBay-only.

### RT-HYP-DD1-06 — MAJOR — HYP — INPUTS cb_hi_share 0.35 / cb_hi_price $2,500 / m§6f ($1M needs ~61 subs)

> The $2,500 tier has almost no universe. Federal buyers mostly buy OEM-branded memory, because Dell, HPE and Lenovo support contracts cover OEM parts. The third-party makers in the federal channel number about ten (AddOn, Axiom, Brute, a few others), and Kingston and Crucial need no outside die check. The model's 35% high-tier share of 23.5 subscribers means about 8 module makers by m36, close to the whole universe. At a 15% high-tier share, ARR at m36 falls to $347k in BASE and $335k in GLUT (my run). No customer list sits behind the $1M path of 61 subscribers.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A count from GSA Advantage and SEWP catalogs showing at least 25 distinct firms selling non-OEM-branded server memory into federal channels, with at least 3 of them in track C.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Federal buyers mostly buy OEM-branded memory, because OEM support contracts cover OEM parts. Third-party makers in the federal channel number about ten, and Kingston and Crucial need no outside check. A 35% high-tier share of 23.5 subscribers meant about 8 module makers by m36, close to the whole universe. HYP's runs are confirmed: at 15%, $346,764 BASE and $335,234 GLUT. After accepting the FATALs, module makers leave the high tier entirely, so it holds only white-box and appliance builders. Revised ARPA is $925, and $1M ARR would need ~90 subscribers (m§6f) with no customer list behind it.

*Action taken:* cb_hi_share 0.35→0.10, and the high tier is redefined as white-box builders. Track C adds HYP's count: from GSA Advantage and the SEWP catalogs, list the firms selling non-OEM-branded server memory federally (target ≥25), with ≥3 of them among the 15 conversations. The $1M-ARR path is withdrawn.

### RT-PHY-DD1-01 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 Line 2 / §2.3 library synergy / INPUTS: lib_module_cost

> The recert bench samples the wrong population for a CXMT library: 2026-27 U.S. ITAD lots are 2018-22-vintage, OEM-qualified DDR4 (90% of the model mix, INPUTS mix_ddr4_32/64), which predates CXMT server parts and should be almost all Samsung, SK hynix or Micron die. The at-risk population is new third-party 'OEM-compatible' DDR5 RDIMMs sold into federal channels (§3, router-switch CXMT 64GB DDR5), which recert never touches. Decap also destroys the module, yet the model prices bundled library samples at $0 module cost (provenance-recert.py line 323) and standalone samples at $150 (INPUTS lib_module_cost). At $1,045 (used 64GB DDR5, INPUTS ddr5_64_price) to ~$3,000 (new, Fusion per DD4 §3), the 63 m36 samples cost ~$66-190k, not $0-9.5k. That erases the ~$122k 'library lift' that justifies the bundle.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Kill-track B or the first two ITAD lots turn up at least one CXMT die among 40 or more decommissioned modules. Or a re-run with library samples drawn from new compatible DDR5 RDIMMs at market price (lib_module_cost ≥ $1,000, bundled too) still reaches ≥10 subscribers by m18.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid on both counts. (1) Population: 2026-27 ITAD lots are 2018-22 OEM-qualified DDR4, 90% of the Phase 4 mix, which predates CXMT server parts. The at-risk population is new third-party DDR5 RDIMMs, which recert never touches. (2) Cost: decap destroys the module, yet the model priced bundled samples at $0 (old line 323). Re-run on Phase 4 inputs with library modules at $1,000, bundled too: m36 ARR $290,023 and subscribers at m18 only 2.9. PHY's ≥10-subscriber condition fails, so the ~$122k 'library lift' is erased as PHY says. I chose $600 per sample (a 16-32GB new DDR5 RDIMM at ~$25/GB) and cut samples to 1/month, because the library is re-keyed to per-lot package markings (RT-PHY-DD1-02).

*Action taken:* Same killed leg as RT-HYP-DD1-02. The bundle now pays for every library module (lib_bundle_pays_module=True, lib_module_cost $600); lib_samples_early 3→1, lib_samples_late 1→0.5. The '63 lab-confirmed part numbers' claim is withdrawn (24 validation samples by m36, no coverage claim).

### RT-PHY-DD1-02 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 Line 2 ('one result identifies the die source for every stick sharing that part number and date-code family') / §5 win condition 2

> The step from one decap to a whole part-number and date-code family has no physical basis. OEM option P/Ns are routinely filled by modules from two or three DRAM vendors, and third-party module makers change DRAM vendor or die revision between builds without changing the module P/N. The module label date code is not the DRAM package date code, and a decap reads only one of the 18-40 packages on the stick. Each lab result therefore covers one build lot, so the '63 lab-confirmed part numbers' (m§3) overstate coverage by an unknown multiple. The cross-reference key must be the DRAM package FBGA/marking code plus a die fingerprint, not the module P/N.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Decap data on 3 or more modules of the same P/N and date-code family, from different lots or sellers, showing the same die vendor and revision. Or ≥90% within-family consistency across a 20-sample set.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid physics. OEM option P/Ns are filled by two or three DRAM vendors, and third-party makers change die vendor or revision without changing the module P/N. The module label date code is the assembly week, not the DRAM package lot. A decap reads one of 18-40 packages. So each lab result covers one build lot, and the cross-reference key must be the DRAM package marking plus a die fingerprint, exactly as PHY says. One caveat, from my own search: an X-ray die-outline fingerprint is only a triage screen. CXMT G4 16Gb DDR5 is ~67-68 mm² against ~49 mm² for current Samsung die, but first-generation (2021) big-3 16Gb DDR5 dies were ~66-72 mm² (3DNews citing TechInsights, snippet). The outline separates CXMT from current big-3 shrinks, not from old big-3 dies.

*Action taken:* Library key changed to package marking + die outline. Per-lot evidence language added. The 'one result identifies the die source for every stick sharing that part number and date-code family' sentence is withdrawn in §14. Track B tests ≥3 modules of one P/N from different lots or sellers if budget allows.

### RT-PHY-DD1-03 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 Line 1 step 2 / §10 track A test protocol / INPUTS: fail_rate, return_rate

> The burn-in cannot see the faults that matter. memtest86+ does not report ECC-corrected errors (v7 ECC support is 'preliminary', on some Ryzen CPUs only: GitHub discussion #248; HotHardware 2024-01), so on a Gen10 Xeon bed with ECC on, a stuck bit is silently corrected and stressapptest, which compares post-correction data, passes it too. DDR5 on-die ECC (JESD79-5 SEC) hides single-cell faults from the host entirely. A room-temperature soak misses retention-weak and VRT cells, whose retention falls steeply with temperature and which surface in 85°C chassis; DIMMs with prior CEs are far likelier to error again (Schroeder et al., SIGMETRICS 2009), and ITAD lots arrive with no CE history. The 3% fallout and 2% returns (INPUTS) come from C_notes, not from a protocol able to measure them, so the per-serial test record (win condition 4) certifies less than buyers assume.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Lot-1 data from a protocol that logs per-DIMM CEs through Linux EDAC/rasdaemon under stressapptest (any CE = reject), or with ECC disabled for the memtest pass, plus a heated soak at ≥70°C DIMM temperature. It should show fallout ≤3% and 90-day returns ≤2%.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid physics. Corrected errors are invisible to memtest86+ on this bed, and stressapptest compares post-correction data. DDR5 on-die ECC hides single-cell faults from the host. Retention and VRT cells degrade steeply with temperature. And ITAD lots arrive with no CE history. The 3% fallout and 2% returns came from C_notes, not from a protocol able to measure them, so the per-serial test record certified less than buyers would assume. I adopted PHY's protocol, CE logging plus a heated soak; ≥70°C DIMM temperature is achievable in a 55-65°C ambient box under load.

*Action taken:* Same protocol and model changes as RT-VET-DD1-03. Test records now state the protocol, temperature and CE count. DDR5 records carry a note that on-die ECC limits host visibility.

### RT-VC-DD1-02 — MAJOR — VC — §1 Line 2 / §2.3 'library worth ~$122k' / INPUTS lib_samples_early, lib_module_cost

> The library is sampled from ITAD decommissioned flow: DDR4 RDIMMs pulled from 2017–2022 Dell/HPE fleets, overwhelmingly Samsung, SK hynix and Micron OEM modules. The risky population is new CXMT-die DDR5 RDIMMs sold into federal channels in 2026–27 (the router-switch listing in §3), and it will not appear in ITAD lots until roughly 2030 decommissions. The 63 lab-confirmed part numbers by m36 would therefore characterize modules nobody needs proven. The $121,985 of m36 ARR credited to the library ($445,543 vs $323,558 'no evidence lift', m§4) rests on an unmeasured 7%→10% close-rate lift from the wrong sample. The bundle hypothesis that 'recert is how it gets die-source data' fails on sample selection.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A lot manifests showing at least 10% of 2026–27 ITAD lots carry third-party-brand DDR5 RDIMMs or post-2024 date codes. Alternatively, re-run the bundle with lib_module_cost charged for buying new compatible SKUs at retail, and keep the lift only if the library still pays.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid. ITAD flow is 2017-22 Dell/HPE fleet DDR4, almost all big-3 OEM modules. The risky population (new CXMT-die DDR5 RDIMMs) will not show up in ITAD lots until about 2030 decommissions. The $121,985 credited to the library rested on an unmeasured 7%→10% lift from the wrong sample. VC's alternative test was to re-run with sample modules bought new at retail. Done: library modules charged at $600-1,000 even in the bundle, and the lift removed. The library no longer pays for itself as a moat. It survives only as a small validation cost inside CleanBOM ($1,200/month in m4-18, then $600).

*Action taken:* Same killed leg as RT-HYP-DD1-02. Track A additionally records lot manifests (brand, DRAM package marks, date codes) so that VC's '≥10% third-party DDR5 or post-2024 date codes' condition is measured, not assumed.

### RT-VC-DD1-03 — MAJOR — VC — §1 Line 2 ('one result identifies the die source for every stick sharing that part number and date-code family')

> This inference is least safe for exactly the compatible-memory makers DD1 targets. Module makers qualify multiple die sources behind one SKU, and even Micron markets 'design-ID-agnostic' server DRAM with interchangeable parts kept transparent behind one part number (Simms, snippet, 2026-11-01). A maker that switches from Samsung to CXMT die partway through a date-code family under an unchanged part number is the case that matters, and a library sampling 3 modules a month will report 'Samsung' for it. A false-negative evidence pack is a False Claims Act-adjacent liability, which DD1 covers with a $400/month E&O policy and a $6k template.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Lab results showing at least 90% die-source consistency within part-number plus date-code families, from at least 3 samples per family across at least 10 families, including at least 2 compatible brands.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid, and it is the worst case for liability. Compatible-memory makers qualify several die sources behind one SKU, and Micron itself sells 'design-ID-agnostic' server DRAM (Simms snippet). A maker that switches from Samsung to CXMT die partway through a family under an unchanged P/N would get a false-negative pack from a library sampling 3 modules a month. That is FCA-adjacent exposure, and the plan covered it with a $400 E&O policy and a $6k template. No within-family consistency data exist (VC's test: ≥90% consistency, ≥3 samples per family, ≥10 families, ≥2 compatible brands). That test would cost about 30 decaps (~$36k at $600 lab + $600 module), more than the founder's $40k reserve allows.

*Action taken:* No family-level inference in any pack: evidence covers only the lot sampled, states its sample basis, and avoids 'no CXMT' assessment language unless E&O without a government-claims exclusion is bound (kill criterion 5, revised). VC's 30-sample consistency study is listed as unaffordable at T0, which is one more reason the library cannot be a moat.

### RT-VC-DD1-04 — MAJOR — VC — §2.2 cash low point / model simulate() recert cash timing / §2.5 #2

> The model books recert cash in the month of sale and runs m6–m10 within ~$3k of the $5,000 floor, with $55–71k of lots outstanding each month. Three real frictions are missing. eBay new-seller limits commonly start near 10 items and $500 a month and take months to lift (nifty.ai, closo, snippets), while the model sells ~25 eBay units (~$6.8k) in Jan-27. New-seller payouts are held for roughly 2–4 weeks. And B2B refurbishers (xByte, PCSP, TPM desks), 55% of the mix by m10, buy on net-30. At m10 that is ~$40k of B2B receivables plus ~$20–30k of held eBay funds, more than the founder's entire $40k. The T0 claim depends on a cash cycle the model does not simulate.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A re-run with eBay revenue capped by a realistic new-seller ramp, a 21-day eBay payout hold and net-30 on B2B. If the cash low stays at or above $0 on $40k, I withdraw this.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid as a modeling gap, and I modeled it as VC asked. eBay new-seller limits ramp over the first three selling months (20/50/100 units, excess sold B2B), eBay proceeds are held a month in those months, and B2B is collected net-30. Result on Phase 4 inputs: the cash low stays at the $5,000 reserve (m4), never below $0, because the operator simply buys fewer lots. By VC's own condition, the T0 claim survives. But the ramp slows: full-time moves m14→m21, m36 ARR $445,543→$381,840, m36 cash $610,097→$401,278. In the revised model the cash low is −$5,863 at m18. The cause is CleanBOM's fixed costs (E&O, tools, library, ISO at m15) with no recert profit, not the cash cycle.

*Action taken:* Model: new inputs ebay_new_seller_caps (20,50,100), ebay_hold_selling_months 3, b2b_terms_months 1; receivables tracked (AR column). The T0 statement in §0 now says the revised bundle needs ~$46k, or ~$6k beyond the $40k, unless the CleanBOM fixed costs are deferred.

### RT-VC-DD1-05 — MAJOR — VC — INPUTS buy_frac=0.65 / §2.4 sensitivity / §2.5 #1

> The model's own INPUTS note says an open-market lot (PCSP, 24×32GB) lists at ~$140/module, which is about realized resale: a buy fraction near 1.0, against a 0.78 breakeven. So 0.65 assumes direct ITAD allocation of $70–100k a month for a part-time newcomer with no R2 certificate, while Iron Mountain's ALM revenue rose 88% YoY because it keeps the spread (§2.7). At 0.75, m36 ARR falls to $250,221 and m36 cash to $163,673. Recert is 85% of cumulative revenue, so one unsourced input decides the bundle.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two written ITAD lot quotes at or below 70% of trailing amcompute realized resale, for at least $20k a month of volume, on downstream terms a non-R2 buyer can meet.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The model's own INPUTS note shows an open-market lot (PCSP, 24×32GB) listed at ~$140/module, about realized resale: a buy fraction near 1.0. That leaves 0.65 as an assumption of direct ITAD allocation of $70-100k a month for a part-time newcomer with no R2 certificate, while Iron Mountain's ALM revenue rose 88% YoY because it keeps the spread. Phase 4 numbers at 0.75 are confirmed: $250,221 ARR and $163,673 cash at m36. In the revised model the input decides recert outright. At 0.70 recert earns −$3/module (−1%); at 0.62 it earns $16 (8%) and adds ~$71k of cumulative EBITDA, but the founder still never goes full-time.

*Action taken:* Same input changes as RT-HYP-DD1-04. VC's quote condition (two written quotes ≤70% for ≥$20k/month) is adopted, tightened to ≤62% for ≥200 modules/month. Recert is reframed from 'cash engine' to 'optional side trade if track A passes'.

### RT-VC-DD1-06 — MAJOR — VC — §2.5 #4 / §5 'Where you lose' / §7 'Raise?' (exit)

> Ceiling and exit. CleanBOM reaches 23.5 subscribers (~$384k ARR) at m36, and $1M ARR needs 61. My [ESTIMATE] of the buyers who cannot lean on an upstream certification is roughly 20–40 compatible-module and white-box builders plus a few hundred SEWP/GSA resellers. Even 40 × $30k plus 300 × $9k is about $3.9M at 100% share. DD1's own exit is a dataset sale to Z2Data, Accuris or OBSIDIA, but §5 prices replication at ~$25k (63 samples × $400). A buyer will not pay more than the cost of rebuilding a dataset it can replicate in a quarter. As a VC I see a $0.4M-ARR lifestyle business, 85% of whose revenue is a 15%-margin resale trade.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* An LOI from a compliance-data vendor to license the dataset at $100k/yr or more. Alternatively, a bottom-up count from GSA eLibrary and the SEWP V holder list showing at least 150 entities that must certify without an upstream certificate they can rely on.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The ceiling was already lifestyle-scale in Phase 4 (23.5 subscribers, ~$384k of CleanBOM ARR). After the red team, revised ARPA is $925, $1M ARR needs ~90 subscribers, and the model reaches 5.3. Even if track C passes, m36 ARR is $171,852-226,833 (m§8d). The dataset-sale exit fails on VC's logic: §5 itself priced replication at ~$25k, and after re-keying, per-lot top-mark images are cheaper still to rebuild. No buyer pays more than its rebuild cost. There is no LOI from a compliance-data vendor and no count of ≥150 entities that cannot rely on an upstream certificate.

*Action taken:* Dataset-sale exit and the 'small raise at m18' path removed from §7/§14. Z2Data/Accuris move from 'customer #10/acquirer' to 'possible licensee only with a ≥$100k/yr LOI'. The concept is classed as at most a lifestyle side line, and the final status is KILLED as a venture candidate.

### RT-VET-DD1-02 — MAJOR — VET — §1 Line 2 ('one result identifies the die source for every stick sharing that part number and date-code family')

> The library is keyed wrong for exactly the modules where die source is uncertain. Memory-maker-branded modules (Samsung M393..., SK hynix HMA..., Micron MTA...) need no library, because brand = die. Third-party and OEM-relabeled part numbers (Kingston, AddOn, Dell/HPE P/Ns) are multi-sourced under one P/N, and swapping between Hynix, Micron and Samsung die under an unchanged P/N is routine. The module date code is the assembly week, not a DRAM lot identifier. A P/N + date-code cross-reference will therefore be wrong on the modules that matter, which makes it an E&O liability, not an asset. And 63 lab-confirmed part numbers by m36 (m§3) is a rounding error against the thousands of compatible-module P/Ns in the channel.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Buy 3 lots of one third-party P/N with different date codes and show the same die source in 90% or more of modules. Otherwise, redesign the library around per-lot top-mark images and re-cost it.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Memory-maker-branded modules (Samsung M393..., SK hynix HMA..., Micron MTA...) need no library, because brand = die. Third-party and OEM-relabeled P/Ns are multi-sourced, and the module date code is the assembly week. A P/N + date-code cross-reference would be wrong on exactly the modules that matter, which makes it a liability, not an asset. Against thousands of compatible-module P/Ns, 63 lab-confirmed P/Ns was a rounding error. VET's alternative is to redesign around per-lot top-mark images and re-cost it. I did that: per-lot images are cheap, but anyone can collect them, so they are no moat.

*Action taken:* Library redesigned as per-lot package top-mark images keyed on DRAM marking codes, with decap only for exceptions. Re-costed in the model (1 sample/month at $600 lab + $600 module). The moat claim is withdrawn; CleanBOM competes on service and price only.

### RT-VET-DD1-03 — MAJOR — VET — §1 Line 1 test flow / §10 track A / INPUTS fail_rate 0.03, return_rate 0.02

> On an ECC RDIMM in a Gen10 (Skylake-SP/Cascade Lake) bed, single-bit errors are silently corrected. memtest86+ does not report corrected ECC errors there (memtest86+ discussion #248; v7's preliminary ECC support is AMD Zen only). Fleet DIMMs are disproportionately retired because they crossed correctable-error/PFA thresholds, which is the very signal behind the Lenovo MPFA and Intel MFP that §5 cites. Those modules pass memtest86+ clean and alarm in the buyer's BMC within weeks. There is also no thermal or voltage margining: retention and VRT fails appear at 85°C+ and not on a room-temperature bench. My run with fallout 8% and returns 5% cuts unit contribution from $36.9 to $19.1 (15% to 8%) and m36 ARR to $351,765, and moves full-time from m14 to m24 (m34 in GLUT).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Test the track A lot under Linux stressapptest with rasdaemon/EDAC logging corrected errors per DIMM, ideally in a 55-65°C ambient box. On the first 200 units, measure CE-inclusive fallout of 4% or less and 90-day returns of 2% or less.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. On a Gen10 Xeon bed with ECC on, memtest86+ does not report corrected errors (discussion #248; v7 ECC is preliminary and AMD-only), so a stuck bit passes silently. Fleet DIMMs are over-represented among CE-threshold retirements, and a room-temperature bench misses retention and VRT fails. VET's run is confirmed: 8% fallout and 5% returns give $19.1/module (8%), $351,765 ARR and full-time m24. The fix is cheap: rasdaemon/EDAC logging under Linux stressapptest (any CE = reject) plus a 55-70°C soak. It raises fallout but cuts returns. I model 7% fallout, 3% returns and 15 minutes per module; alone on Phase 4 that gives $25/module (10%), $381,840 ARR and full-time m21.

*Action taken:* Test protocol changed: Linux stressapptest with rasdaemon/EDAC per-DIMM CE logging, any CE = reject, plus a heated soak at 55-70°C ambient (+$500 enclosure). Model: fail_rate 0.03→0.07, return_rate 0.02→0.03, minutes_per_module 12→15, capex_m1 $4,200→$4,700. Track A pass condition: CE-inclusive fallout ≤4% on the lot and 90-day returns ≤2% tracked after the decision.

### RT-VET-DD1-04 — MAJOR — VET — INPUTS mix_ddr5_64 = 0.10 / m§1a per-SKU contribution

> DDR5 server platforms shipped from Nov-2022 (Genoa) and Jan-2023 (Sapphire Rapids). On 5-6-year lives, almost none are decommissioned before 2028. In a shortage, operators extend lives and redeploy DIMMs, and §5 itself cites Sims redeploying them into CXL. Yet this 10% slice earns 45% of per-module contribution: $167 per DDR5 unit, $16.7 of the $36.9 blend. With mix_ddr5_64=0 (70/30 DDR4), the model gives $21.3 per module, m36 ARR $372,010, cash $344,648 and full-time at m22. Add realistic fallout and returns (8%/5%) and a 0.70 buy fraction, and recert contributes nothing: −$0.4 per module, m36 ARR $237,798, full-time m34.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Three track A ITAD quotes in which 64GB DDR5 RDIMMs are 10% or more of units, offered at 70% of realized resale or less.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. DDR5 platforms shipped from Nov-2022 (Genoa) and Jan-2023 (Sapphire Rapids). On 5-6-year lives almost none are decommissioned before 2028, and operators extend lives in a shortage. The 10% DDR5 slice produced 45% of per-module contribution. VET's runs are confirmed: with no DDR5, $21.3/module, $372,010 ARR, $344,648 cash, full-time m22; adding 8%/5% and buy 0.70 gives −$0.4/module, $237,798 ARR and full-time m34. I keep a 3% residual (excess and new-pull stock), since amcompute does record used DDR5 sales.

*Action taken:* mix 0.60/0.30/0.10 → 0.65/0.32/0.03. Track A records the DDR5 share of each quoted lot (VET's test: ≥10% DDR5 at ≤70% of resale in 3 quotes). The DDR5 test bed (m6, $4k) is bought only if quotes show DDR5 volume.

### RT-VET-DD1-05 — MAJOR — VET — INPUTS b2b_price_factor 0.88 / ebay_share_m12 0.40 (B2B is 60% of volume from m12)

> The B2B buyers in §3 (#6-9: xByte, TechMikeNY, PCSP, Park Place/Curvature) buy ITAD lots and run their own test benches. They will pay a middleman only up to their own lot cost plus test cost, not 88% of eBay realized. If you can buy at 65% of resale, so can they. My run at b2b_price_factor=0.75 cuts unit contribution to $16.7 (7%) and m36 ARR to $341,342, and moves full-time to m25 (m34 in GLUT). Because the model shifts 60% of volume to B2B by m12, the price these accounts actually pay is a first-order input. It has no source.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two signed B2B purchase orders or written price sheets from the §3 accounts at 0.85x eBay realized or better, for tested 32GB DDR4.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. It is the same point as RT-HYP-DD1-05 from the supply side: if the founder can buy at 65%, so can the B2B accounts. VET's run is confirmed exactly: at 0.75, unit contribution is $16.7 (7%), m36 ARR $341,342, full-time m25 (m34 in GLUT). With 60% of volume shifted to B2B by m12, the B2B price was a first-order input with no source.

*Action taken:* Same changes as RT-HYP-DD1-05. Track A requires two signed POs or written price sheets at ≥0.85× eBay realized for tested 32GB DDR4 before any B2B volume is planned.

### RT-VET-DD1-06 — MAJOR — VET — §10 track B / INPUTS lab_cost $400 / kill criterion 3 (15 business days)

> A die ID that will stand behind an attestation with False-Claims exposure is not 'X-ray + decap + die photo for $400'. Most AS6171 labs hold no CXMT or YMTC reference-die images (TechInsights has them, at subscription prices), so 'die source identifiable' depends on die logos CXMT may not print. Accredited labs add minimum lot charges and report fees, and the DD's own weak source says turnaround is 2-6 weeks. Track B is therefore likely to fail its 15-business-day rule on day 29 with zero slack, which drops the library (−$122k of m36 ARR, m§4). At lab_cost $1,200 with no close-rate lift (7%), my run gives m36 ARR $297,042 and full-time at m19.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Three written AS6171 lab quotes, including minimum and report charges, at $600 per sample or less and 15 business days or less. At least one lab must confirm in writing that it holds a CXMT DDR4/DDR5 reference die image.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Mostly valid. My search supports VET's point on reference images: TechInsights holds CXMT G4 die analysis behind a paywall, and nothing shows that ordinary AS6171 labs hold CXMT DDR4/DDR5 reference die. Minimum lot and report charges are plausible, and the 2-6 week turnaround in the DD's own weak source would fail the 15-business-day rule. VET's run is confirmed: lab $1,200 with no lift gives $297,042 ARR and full-time m19. Two things soften the hit. The library is no longer a coverage asset, so the lab is used for ~1 validation sample a month plus customer pass-through at cost × 1.6. And the calendar problem is fixable by shipping samples by day 5.

*Action taken:* lab_cost $400→$600 (the track-B ceiling) in the model. Track B quotes must be all-in (minimum lot, report fees) and in writing from 3 labs, and at least one lab must confirm in writing that it holds CXMT DDR4/DDR5 reference die images (or can identify CXMT die by logo/dimension). Samples ship by day 5, not day 7. The 15-business-day and $600 thresholds are unchanged.

### RT-HYP-DD1-07 — MINOR — HYP — §5 win condition 2 / §10 track B / §6 JEP106 row

> In the non-counterfeit case, a cheap check already does the job. At receiving, read the package top-mark under a microscope and the SPD JEP106 bytes; CXMT's 0x0A11 is in memtest86+'s table (§6). Decap is needed only for remarked parts, and the DD itself found no case of relabeled CXMT die inside U.S. server RDIMMs (§5). The X-ray/decap/SPD evidence pack therefore over-serves the common case and under-prices the rare one.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* One documented case of a server RDIMM sold in the U.S. with remarked or relabeled CXMT die, from track B or from GIDEP/ERAI alerts.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. In the non-counterfeit case a receiving-desk check (package top mark under a microscope plus SPD JEP106 bytes; CXMT 0x0A11) does the job, and decap is needed only for remarked or third-party-branded packages. DD1 found no case of relabeled CXMT die in U.S. server RDIMMs, and this search found none either. The one wrinkle, the Powev-marked CXMT package in TechInsights' analysis, argues for an exception tier, not routine decap. HYP's condition (a documented U.S. server-RDIMM relabeling case) is not met.

*Action taken:* Evidence pack redesigned: default = top-mark images + SPD bytes + X-ray die-outline triage; decap priced as an exception add-on at lab cost × 1.6. cb_pack_rate 0.15→0.10. The pack is now a commodity service that any receiving desk can replicate.

### RT-HYP-DD1-08 — MINOR — HYP — INPUTS opex_eo $400/month / §11 kill criterion 5

> E&O at $400 a month looks optimistic for a one-person firm whose assessments feed federal certifications and carry False Claims Act exposure. I would expect underwriters to add a regulatory or government-claims exclusion, or to set a minimum premium well above $4.8k a year [ESTIMATE]. If the exclusion applies, the liability cap is the only protection left. My legal team would not accept a capped attestation as a basis for reliance.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A bound quote at or below $400 a month that covers government-contract reliance claims with no regulatory exclusion.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Partly right. On price, the $400/month assumption is not obviously optimistic. Published small-firm tech E&O averages ~$67-110/month, with a range of ~$500 to over $9,000 a year (Insureon/TechInsurance snippets, undated), so $4,800/yr sits in the upper part of the range. On the exclusion, HYP is right and I have no evidence: generic policies commonly exclude fines and regulatory or government claims [UNVERIFIED-training], and a capped attestation is no basis for reliance by a hyperscaler legal team. The answer is to change the product, not the premium.

*Action taken:* opex_eo $400→$600/month. Kill criterion 5 revised: a bound quote that covers government-contract reliance claims with no regulatory exclusion at ≤$600/month, or CleanBOM sells raw evidence only (images, SPD bytes, lab reports) with no assessment or 'no CXMT' language.

### RT-PHY-DD1-04 — MINOR — PHY — §1 Line 4 / §6 'failure prediction' / INPUTS: dh_price

> The corrected-error trend feed rests on host-visible CE streams, which is what the cited predictors are trained on (IBM; arXiv 2212.10441; Intel MFP at Tencent). On DDR5, on-die ECC absorbs most single-bit cell faults before the host sees them; only coarse ECS counts in mode registers remain, so CE-trend signal thins out. On DDR4 the fleets are being retired, and BMC-level prediction (Lenovo MPFA, Intel MFP in AMI/Insyde, ParkView) is already free. The DD quotes no precision or recall for any predictor at a useful lead time, so the '4.5× DIY cost' price has no physical value anchor.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A backtest on one TPM's DDR4 CE logs that beats MPFA/ParkView by a stated margin (e.g., ≥50% precision at ≥7-day lead), or dropping the predictive claim and selling only tested spares.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid. The predictors cited (IBM, arXiv 2212.10441, Intel MFP) are trained on host-visible CE streams, and DDR5 on-die ECC absorbs most single-bit faults, leaving only coarse ECS counts. DDR4 fleets are being retired, and BMC-level prediction (Lenovo MPFA, Intel MFP in AMI/Insyde, ParkView) is free. DD1 quotes no precision or recall at any lead time, so the 4.5× DIY-cost price had no value anchor. I cannot run PHY's backtest (no TPM CE logs), so I take PHY's alternative: drop the predictive claim. Dropping the subscription from the bundle on Phase 4 inputs costs $61,013 of m36 ARR ($445,543→$384,530).

*Action taken:* Killed leg: C-85 DIMM-health predictive subscription (dh_in_bundle True→False). Tested spares are sold only through the recert B2B channel. Track D and kill criterion 12 are retired.

### RT-PHY-DD1-05 — MINOR — PHY — §1 'Optane PMem rides along' / INPUTS: pmem_buy_frac, fail_rate

> Optane PMem is non-volatile, so ITAD lots carry residual customer data. Modules left with security enabled and a passphrase set may not unlock or secure-erase without that passphrase. The model applies the generic 3% fallout and adds no sanitization or chain-of-custody cost for media that is still readable. PMem also works only on 2nd and 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable with matched firmware, and media wear ('percentage remaining' in ipmctl) should be logged and priced per module.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* First-lot data showing ≤5% of PMem modules are locked or unsanitizable, plus a per-serial NIST SP 800-88 sanitization certificate from each ITAD.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. PMem is non-volatile. Modules left with security enabled and a passphrase set may not unlock or secure-erase, and residual customer data creates a chain-of-custody duty. The Phase 4 model applied no fallout to PMem at all. It works only on 2nd/3rd-gen Xeon Scalable with matched firmware, and media wear should be logged. The line is small and sunsets with vSphere 8 (2027-10-11). Effect on Phase 4: m36 cash −$10.9k; PMem contribution today falls from 23% to 14% per module.

*Action taken:* Model: pmem_fail_rate 0→0.10, pmem_return_rate 0.02→0.05. Track A: read security state and 'percentage remaining' with ipmctl on every lot module, and require a per-serial NIST SP 800-88 sanitization certificate from the ITAD. Stop PMem buying if >5% of a lot is locked or unsanitizable (kill criterion 11 revised).

### RT-PHY-DD1-06 — MINOR — PHY — §10 track B (positive control and identifiability threshold)

> Track B's only known-CXMT control is a consumer DDR5 kit (KingBank/Lexar). Counterfeit labs check die marks against reference images, and they may recognise a consumer CXMT die but not the server RDIMM die revision, so the test does not falsify identifiability for the at-risk population. The plan also skips a cheap nondestructive screen: X-ray die outline. The CXMT G4 16Gb die is 66.99 mm² versus 52.6 mm² for 1-alpha (01_LANDSCAPE §4), which could triage samples before any decap.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A known CXMT server RDIMM (e.g., CXMR4E464R8AS, §3) added as a second control, written confirmation from ≥2 of the 3 labs that they hold CXMT reference die images, and an X-ray die-dimension step costed in the protocol.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. A consumer CXMT kit does not prove the lab can identify the server RDIMM die revision, and an X-ray die outline is a cheap nondestructive screen. I add both, with one correction from my search. The outline (CXMT G4 ~67-68 mm² vs ~49 mm² for current Samsung) separates CXMT from current big-3 shrinks, but it overlaps first-generation 2021 big-3 16Gb DDR5 dies (~66-72 mm²; 3DNews/TechInsights snippet). It is triage, not proof.

*Action taken:* Track B: the consumer CXMT kit is replaced by a known CXMT server RDIMM (CXMR4E464R8AS-class, or a 16-32GB CXMT DDR5 RDIMM, ~$800 [ESTIMATE]) as the positive control. An X-ray die-dimension step is added before any decap. The lot is shrunk to ~$2,900 so the $9,500 hard cap holds. The written reference-image confirmation from RT-VET-DD1-06 applies.

### RT-VC-DD1-07 — MINOR — VC — All models: no income-tax line (models/provenance-recert.py; same in DD2–DD5)

> None of the five models provisions income or self-employment tax. In DD1, BASE cumulative EBITDA of $662,971 flows through a pass-through LLC to the founder, who owes roughly 25–35% combined tax on it even if the cash is recycled into inventory. So the $610,097 m36 cash (and the m14 full-time trigger, which is tested on pre-tax cash) is overstated. The other models are smaller but have the same flaw.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Re-run all five models with a 30% provision on positive cumulative EBITDA, and show the full-time triggers still fire in the same months.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. A pass-through LLC owes federal, state and self-employment tax on profit even when the cash is recycled into inventory, and the Phase 4 full-time trigger tested pre-tax cash. With a 30% provision on positive taxable income (EBITDA after salary, capex expensed, losses carried forward) on Phase 4 inputs: full-time moves m14→m17, m36 ARR $445,543→$419,301, m36 cash $610,097→$378,961, and $181,150 of tax is provisioned. So the full-time trigger does not fire in the same month, which was VC's test. In the revised base no tax is due, because there is no cumulative profit.

*Action taken:* Model: tax_rate 0.30 with loss carry-forward, paid monthly; Tax column added; the full-time trigger tests post-tax cash. Applies to DD1 only (other DD models are owned by other agents).

### RT-VET-DD1-07 — MINOR — VET — §1 Optane PMem SKU / INPUTS pmem_* (fallout treated like RDIMMs)

> ITAD workflows often leave PMem 100/200 modules passphrase-locked or security-frozen. Without the passphrase they cannot be reprovisioned short of an OEM/Intel RMA path. They also run only on matching Xeon generations: PMem 100 on Cascade Lake, PMem 200 on Ice Lake, with BIOS/firmware dependencies. Expect fallout and platform-mismatch returns well above the 3%/2% assumed. The line is small and sunsetting with vSphere 8, so this does not move the verdict.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* In track A, read the security state of 20 lot PMem modules with ipmctl: 90% or more 'disabled/unlocked', and fewer than 5% returns on the first 50 sold.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid and, as VET says, small. Passphrase-locked or security-frozen PMem 100/200 cannot be reprovisioned without the passphrase, short of an OEM RMA. PMem 100 runs only on Cascade Lake and PMem 200 only on Ice Lake, with BIOS dependencies, so mismatch returns will exceed 2%. Modeled together with RT-PHY-DD1-05. The verdict does not move.

*Action taken:* Same model changes as RT-PHY-DD1-05. Listings state the platform generation (Cascade Lake vs Ice Lake). VET's track A test is adopted: ≥90% of 20 lot modules read 'disabled/unlocked' in ipmctl, and returns <5% on the first 50 sold.

## DD2 — Memory Lifecycle & Cost Intelligence for OEMs (lifecycle-intel)

**Final status after red team: DOWNGRADED.** No objection was refuted. The FATAL objection, confirmed by the model, killed the SaaS-fed Memory Cost Escalation Index: the panel never reaches the 15 contributors needed to publish within 36 months, and the index has no precedent for a glut upside. That index was the leg the bundle existed to borrow its moat and cycle scores from. With 15 further downgrades, revised BASE m36 ARR falls from $421,548 to $149,629 and the founder is never paid within 36 months. The SaaS still earns first revenue at m6 and breaks even (founder unpaid) at m17, so it is demoted to a side line, not killed; the 30-day kill test decides.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: Yes for the SaaS: TRL 9 software on public APIs and PDN/PCN documents. No for the index as designed: a SaaS-fed panel reaches 14 contributors by m36, below the 15 needed for 3 grade-homogeneous cells. It becomes possible only if the kill test signs >=10 non-subscriber contributors, and even then the model sells <=2.8 licences by m36. · Profitable: No as a venture; marginal as a side business. Revised BASE ARR is $69,915 at m18 and $149,629 at m36 (GLUT $104,077, SEVERE $83,031). Breakeven with the founder unpaid comes at m17 (2028-04), and the $10k/month salary is never covered within 36 months. The cash low is $6,100 at m16, a $33,900 draw. A 30% miss on price, conversion or COGS exhausts the $40k. · Doable by this founder: Yes technically (pure software, part-time, T0), but conditional on a written employer carve-out before any code (K0) and on booking 25 qualified calls in 21 days from cold outreach outside the employer's network (K6). Part-time capacity caps the funnel at ~20 trials/month.

### RT-VC-DD2-01 — FATAL — VC — §1 steps 3–4 / §8 m9 gate / INPUTS contributor_share=0.40, index_methodology_month=9, index_first_sale_month=12

> The index is the only leg the bundle exists to borrow its moat and cycle scores from, and it cannot be computed at the model's own account counts. At m9 the model has 5.8 paying accounts × 40% contributors = 2.3 contributors; at m12 it has 11.8 × 0.4 = 4.7. The §8 gate demands at least 10 contributors and at least 5 per DDR4/DDR5 cell at m9, and the methodology needs at least 5 companies per price cell. Even at m36, 88 accounts give ~35 contributors spread across DDR3/4/5, LPDDR, eMMC, NOR and EEPROM cells and densities, so most cells still fail the 5-contributor rule. Licence revenue from m12 is booked against an index that cannot yet exist.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* At least 10 signed PO-history contribution agreements before m9, independent of SaaS conversions (K4 asks for only 5), plus a cell map naming at least 3 cells that reach 5 contributors.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Accepted as fatal to the index as designed. Your arithmetic reproduces: 5.8 accounts x 0.40 = 2.3 contributors at m9 and 4.7 at m12. The plan published at m9 with >=10 contributors and >=5 per cell, and it booked licence revenue from m12. With a 15-contributor gate on the Phase 4 funnel, publication slips to m23 (BASE) and m34 (GLUT). On the revised funnel (0.25 contributor share plus 5 seed contributors) the panel reaches only 14.2 by m36, so the index is never published in any scenario. Your remedy, >=10 non-subscriber contribution agreements, buys little. Ten seeds give publication at m20 and 1.8 licences at m36: BASE m36 ARR is $165,118 vs $149,629 without them. Ten seeds with a 10-contributor gate give 2.8 licences and $174,279. The SaaS-fed index is killed, and it was the leg the bundle existed to borrow its moat and cycle scores from. It survives only as an option that the revised K4 can re-open.

*Action taken:* Named leg killed: the SaaS-fed index (C-86 as designed). Model: panel gate (panel_min_contributors 15, seed_contributors 5, contributor_share 0.25); seeded variants reported in model s10. K4 revised: >=10 signed contribution agreements independent of SaaS conversions, plus a cell map with >=3 cells reaching 5 contributors; otherwise the index option is closed for good. s0 verdicts and the recommendation rewritten.

### RT-HYP-DD2-01 — MAJOR — HYP — INPUTS contributor_share 0.40 / index_methodology_month 9 / index_rate 0.60 / §8 Q3 gate

> The index has no panel on the model's own funnel. At a 40% contributor share, the model's account counts give 2.3 contributors at m9 (5.8 accounts), 4.7 at m12 and 10.8 at m18. Yet the roadmap publishes at m9 with '≥10 contributors and ≥5 per DDR4/DDR5 cell', and the model sells its first licence at m12. index_rate is not linked to panel size at all. SMB industrial OEMs mostly buy DDR4, so the DDR5 cell stays empty. Pushing the first sale to m18 costs only $11k of m36 ARR ($410k), which shows the model never makes the index depend on its own data.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* At least 10 signed data-contribution agreements by m9, each with 24 months of component-level memory PO history and at least 5 covering DDR5. Or a model change that gates index sales on panel size.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and the model confirms it. On the Phase 4 inputs the panel is 0.40 x accounts = 2.3 contributors at m9, 4.7 at m12 and 10.8 at m18, yet the roadmap published the index at m9 and sold it from m12, and index_rate had no link to panel size. Your m18-first-sale run reproduces exactly: BASE m36 ARR $410,092 vs $421,548 (-$11k), which shows the index was booked independently of its own data. I added a panel gate: the index is published only when contributors (0.25 x accounts + 5 seed contributors signed in the kill test) reach 15, and first sold 3 months later. On the revised funnel the panel reaches 9.3 contributors at m18 and 14.2 at m36, so the index is never published within 36 months in BASE, GLUT or SEVERE. Even on the Phase 4 funnel the gate alone pushes publication to m23 (BASE) and m34 (GLUT). DDR5 is dropped from the cell plan; SMB industrial buyers mostly buy DDR4, eMMC and SPI NOR. The SaaS-fed index as designed is dead. It can be re-opened only if the kill test signs at least 10 non-subscriber contributors (revised K4), and even then (10 seeds, 10-contributor gate) the model sells only 2.8 licences by m36.

*Action taken:* Named leg killed: the SaaS-fed Memory Cost Escalation Index licensed from m12. Model: panel gate added (panel_min_contributors 0->15, seed_contributors 0->5, contributor_share 0.40->0.25, index_lag_after_panel 3); index revenue is now $0 in all revised scenarios. Deep dive: index demoted to a K4-gated option in s0/s1/s2/s8/s10/s11; old m9 gate ('>=10 contributors, >=5 per DDR4/DDR5 cell') replaced by the VET-01 publication test.

### RT-HYP-DD2-02 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 step 3 (POOL) / §10 K4

> Small OEMs don't hold memory contract prices. A 60-person rugged-PC maker buys modules from a franchised distributor, or buys turnkey from an EMS that folds memory into the board price. What it can contribute is a distributor or EMS sell price that lags 1-2 quarters. The panel would then measure distributor markup, with large basis risk against the supplier contract prices that real LTAs settle on. Those contract prices sit with OEMs on direct LTAs, hyperscalers and Tier-1 EMS, none of whom will put them in a startup's pool. My own supply agreements forbid disclosing supplier pricing.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K4 contributors who can show line-item memory POs, direct or through franchised distribution, with at least $250k a year of memory spend, and who are not EMS-turnkey buyers.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Mostly right. A 60-person OEM holds distributor or EMS sell prices that lag 1-2 quarters, not supplier contract prices, and an EMS-turnkey buyer has no memory line item at all. One part does not hold: for the one use that remains, an SMB OEM passing its own memory cost through to its customer (the Unipi and Revolution Pi pattern), the seller's real cost basis IS the distribution price, so a distribution-channel index is the correct measure for that clause and TrendForce contract is the one carrying basis risk. That rescues only a narrow case. It removes LTA settlement, Tier-1 EMS and federal uses from the index market and shrinks the contributor pool, because only line-item buyers can contribute.

*Action taken:* Index re-scoped to a distribution-channel memory cost index for OEM-to-customer surcharges; EMS-turnkey buyers excluded as contributors; K4 counts only contributors with line-item memory POs (direct or franchised distribution) and >=$250k/yr memory spend. Model: contributor_share 0.40->0.25, index_rate 0.60->0.40.

### RT-HYP-DD2-03 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 ('Memory has no such index') / §5 TrendForce row / §10 K3

> Memory already has a de facto reference. DRAMeXchange publishes contract prices for DDR4/DDR5 chips and modules, and the DD's own §5 table calls TrendForce 'the default reference'. Big buyers and suppliers adjust prices off TrendForce contract quotes and the supplier's quarterly price letter; nobody licenses a separate index to write a clause. The 2025-26 pass-through came through list-price hikes (Dell +15-20% from mid-Dec-2025, TrendForce 2025-12-05) and at-cost surcharges that name no index (Unipi, Revolution Pi). No U.S. surcharge notice was found at all (§3). K3's pass bar, 2 of 10 saying they 'would cite within 6 months', is a free opinion, not a commitment.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* By m15, one executed contract or published surcharge schedule between unaffiliated parties that names this index, not an LOI.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The s1 claim 'memory has no such index' was wrong in substance. DRAMeXchange has used the 8GB PC DRAM module contract price as its contract-trend base since 2019 (TrendForce press release 2018-11-05, via snippet). The 2025-26 pass-through by U.S. OEMs came as list-price hike notices (Dell +15-20% from mid-Dec-2025; Lenovo quotes expiring 2026-01-01; TrendForce 2025-12-05) or as at-cost surcharges naming no index (Revolution Pi RAM surcharge since 2026-02-16, adjusted 2026-04-02; Unipi). One more search found no U.S. surcharge schedule that names any index. The K3 pass bar ('would cite within 6 months') was a free opinion, as you say.

*Action taken:* s1/s5 corrected: TrendForce contract prints are the de facto reference and the index must beat them. K3 now needs >=2 signed LOIs naming a live contract or surcharge schedule. New m15 kill criterion adopts your test: one executed contract or published surcharge schedule between unaffiliated parties naming the index, or the index option closes. Model: index_rate 0.60->0.40. Sources logged in p5r-lifecycle-intel.jsonl.

### RT-HYP-DD2-04 — MAJOR — HYP — §5 competitors / §10 K2 / INPUTS trial_conversion 0.12

> Lifecycle and EOL alerts already reach the buyer for free. Octopart's BOM tool is free (§5), franchised distributors email PCN/PDN notices to account holders at no charge, and Arrow's BOM tooling runs on its own SiliconExpert data [UNVERIFIED-training]. The procurement lead already gets the EOL email that steps 1-2 charge $249 for. I expect K2 to fail, with at least 60% saying they are already covered. At a conversion of 0.08 instead of 0.12, BASE ARR at m36 is $257k and the founder goes full-time at m32 (my run).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K2 results in which at least 40% of the 25 qualified calls name a specific memory EOL or LTB deadline they missed in 2025-26 with their current tools.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Your run reproduces: trial conversion 0.08 on the Phase 4 inputs gives BASE m36 ARR $257,424 and full-time at m32. The substitution is real: Octopart's BOM tool is free and franchised distributors e-mail PDNs to account holders. Free tools miss memory-specific events: die-revision and fab-transfer PCNs under an unchanged MPN (RT-PHY-DD2-05), LTB quantity sizing, and memory alternates. None of those is proven to convert yet. I cut trial_conversion from 0.12 to 0.10 as the revised central value and adopted your test as K2b. On the revised model 0.08 gives m36 ARR $119,703 and a cash low of $486 at m22, which uses up almost all of the $40k. At 0.10 it is $149,629 and the founder is never paid.

*Action taken:* Model: trial_conversion 0.12->0.10; conversion-0.08 variant reported in model s10. Kill test: new K2b, >=10 of 25 calls must name a specific memory EOL, LTB deadline or die-revision PCN they missed in 2025-26 with current tools, or lifecycle becomes a free funnel only. Die-revision PCN alerts made the lead differentiator.

### RT-HYP-DD2-05 — MAJOR — HYP — §12 cycle test / INPUTS index_floor 0.35, vol_ref 0.15

> 'Survives a glut partially' really means 'survives only if the unproven index sells'. With index_rate set to 0, GLUT ARR at m36 is $156k and the founder is never paid (be_sal = None in my run), against $334k with the index. The +31% glut uplift assumes buyers invoke down-adjustments under clauses that no one has signed yet. In a glut the buyer holds the leverage and simply re-bids, with no index needed.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Evidence of a memory price-adjustment clause tied to a third-party index that triggered a downward adjustment in the 2019 or 2023 downturn.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Confirmed. On the Phase 4 inputs with index_rate = 0, GLUT m36 ARR is $156,308 and the salary is never covered, against $333,977 with the index. The '+31% in a glut' was an assumption with no precedent: my search for an index-linked memory down-adjustment in 2018-19 or 2023 found only contract-price reporting and no clause. I made two changes. Falling prices now add no index demand beyond the floor, and licences churn 3%/month while the 3-month move is below -5%. On the Phase 4 inputs these two changes alone cut GLUT index revenue at m36 from $7,318 to $3,371/month, below BASE's $3,859, and GLUT m36 ARR from $333,977 to $246,895. On the revised model GLUT m36 ARR is $104,077 and SEVERE $83,031, and the founder is never paid in any scenario.

*Action taken:* Model: vol_down_weight 1.0->0.0, index_churn_falling 0.008->0.03. s12 verdict changed from 'survives a glut partially' to 'survives a glut only as part-time side income; the index is no glut hedge'. The 'earns more in a glut' claim is withdrawn from s0/s1/s12.

### RT-PHY-DD2-01 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 step [2] / §10 step 1 (§5949 flag) / §3 customers #4-6

> The §5949 flag is keyed on MPNs and the manufacturer field ('flags CXMT/YMTC manufacturers', §10), so it identifies the seller, not the die. Modules, eMMC, SSDs and MCPs sold under Kingston, ATP, Innodisk or SMART MPNs can contain CXMT DRAM or YMTC NAND die.
