DD1 itself establishes that SPD bytes are assembler-written and that only decap proves die source. The flag therefore gives false negatives in exactly the risky category. Yet §3 says to 'lead with the CXMT/YMTC flag' for Trenton, CP Tech and Core Systems, and a customer relying on a false negative carries FCA-adjacent exposure.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Measure the flag's recall on 20 module/eMMC MPNs of known die source (e.g., from DD1's lab results). Otherwise relabel it 'brand-level only, not die-level' and take it out of the pitch to #4-6.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct. A manufacturer-field flag identifies the seller, not the die. A Kingston, ATP, Innodisk or SMART module, eMMC, SSD or MCP can carry CXMT DRAM or YMTC NAND die, and DD1 shows that only decap or supplier die-source documents prove the source. A false negative is the worst failure mode for a s5949 feature, and leading the pitch to Trenton, CP Tech and Core Systems with it invited exactly that liability. No revenue was attributed to the flag, so the economics do not change.

*Action taken:* Flag relabelled 'brand-level screen only, not die-level; a negative result is NOT evidence of compliance', with a counsel-reviewed disclaimer. For module/eMMC/SSD/MCP lines it prompts the customer to request a supplier die-source certificate and points to DD1's die-level service. Removed from the pitch to customers #4-6, who are now led with the LTB sizer and die-revision PCN monitoring (s3). If DD1 lab data exists, measure recall on 20 known-die MPNs before any compliance claim.

### RT-PHY-DD2-02 — MAJOR — PHY — §8 m9 gate ('≥10 contributors and ≥5 per DDR4/DDR5 cell') / §1 step [4] index design

> Memory is not one commodity. Prices diverge by generation (DDR3/4/5/LPDDR4X), die density, width, package, speed bin and above all temperature grade (commercial vs industrial -40°C vs AEC-Q100). The §3 buyers (industrial PC, rugged, medtech) mostly buy industrial-grade, long-life DDR3/DDR4 components from Micron, ISSI, Alliance, Winbond or Nanya. Their 2026 price path is driven by EOL, not by the DDR5 contract path the model imports. Ten contributors at ≥5 per cell can fill only one or two broad cells, and those average across grades with large price spreads. The resulting basis risk lets any counterparty reject the index (§5 'use TrendForce').

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Tabulate the memory lines in the 5 design partners' BOMs. If ≥70% of memory spend falls into ≤4 grade-homogeneous cells that each have ≥5 contributors, the design works.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The s3 buyers (industrial PC, rugged, medtech) mostly buy industrial-temperature DDR3/DDR4 components and modules, eMMC and SPI NOR from Micron, ISSI, Alliance, Winbond and Nanya. Their 2026 price path is driven by EOL and allocation, not by the DDR5 contract path the model uses as its urgency proxy. An index that averages across temperature grades or generations measures mix, not price, and any counterparty can reject it. Ten contributors fill one or two broad cells at most. I also note in s12 that the DDR5 path is only a proxy for these buyers.

*Action taken:* Index limited to <=4 grade-homogeneous cells (generation x density x width x temperature grade; candidates: DDR4 8Gb x16 industrial-temp components, DDR4 8/16GB industrial SO-DIMMs, 32-64GB industrial eMMC, 128Mb SPI NOR), published quarterly. Model: panel_min_contributors 0->15. Your test adopted at m3: if <70% of the 5 design partners' memory spend falls in <=4 such cells that can each reach 5 contributors, the index option closes (s8, s11).

### RT-VC-DD2-02 — MAJOR — VC — §1 index rationale / §5 'On credibility' / §3 'No U.S. OEM surcharge notice was found'

> The panel measures the wrong price. SMB OEMs with $1–50M of BOM spend buy memory through franchised distribution and brokers (Avnet, Arrow, DigiKey) at allocation-inflated distribution prices. The escalation clauses in EMS and federal contracts reference supplier contract prices. An index built from five SMBs' distributor POs carries basis risk against both TrendForce contract and spot (spot ran ~2× contract in 2026, §5). The counterparty on the other side of every surcharge clause has a ready objection: 'unrepresentative, use TrendForce.' No U.S. OEM surcharge was found to cite any index at all, and the two European examples (Unipi, KUNBUS) charge 'at cost'.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K3 passes with at least 2 of 10 prospects committing, and in at least one live contract both buyer and seller agree in writing to cite the index. Or a back-test showing the panel would have tracked TrendForce contract within ±10% through 2025–26.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted, with the one narrow refutation given under RT-HYP-DD2-02: for an SMB OEM passing its own memory cost to its customer, a distribution-price index is the correct basis. That narrow case is where the basis dilemma bites hardest. If the panel tracks TrendForce contract within +/-10%, it is redundant. If it does not, the larger counterparty says 'unrepresentative, use TrendForce', and the two European surcharges found (Unipi, KUNBUS) charge at cost with no index. The licence market that survives is small.

*Action taken:* Your back-test adopted as a pre-licence requirement: before any licence, back-test the panel against TrendForce contract and spot prints for 2025-26 and publish the basis. K3 now requires that in at least one live contract both buyer and seller agree in writing to cite the index. Model: index_rate 0.60->0.40, contributor_share 0.40->0.25.

### RT-VC-DD2-03 — MAJOR — VC — §6 data-rights table / §12 module table / INPUTS display_licence=$1,000 from m12

> 39% of BASE m36 revenue is the 'buy/hold + allocation alerts' module, which is a price-trend product. By DD2's own reading, Octopart's terms bar historical pricing and trend use, bar caching beyond 24 h, and exclude 'software developers building applications for that market'. DigiKey's display and redistribution terms were not found, and its API caps at 1,000 requests a day. Without a lawful historical price series there are no buy/hold signals. The model's $1,000/month 'display licence' from m12 has no quote behind it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Written permission (K5) from Nexar or DigiKey for in-app display and historical use at $1,000/month or less. Otherwise delete the price-driven line and re-run; my [ESTIMATE] is that BASE m36 ARR falls toward $250k.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Mostly accepted. The Octopart terms forbid historical price and inventory trend use and caching beyond 24 h, and they exclude software developers building applications for the distribution market. That blocks the per-part price history the buy/hold module implied, and DigiKey's display terms are still unknown. Part of the objection does not hold: market-level buy/hold signals can be built from public prints (the DXI headline, TrendForce press releases) and the contributed panel without per-part distributor history. That product is weaker than Supplyframe CIQ. I ran your variant. With the price-driven line deleted, Phase 4 BASE m36 ARR is $159,231, worse than your $250k estimate, and the salary is never covered. On the revised model it is $69,289 with a cash low of $947. Without data rights, the SaaS is not worth running.

*Action taken:* Model: display_licence 1,000->2,000; new price_line_on switch; K5-fail variant reported in model s10. K5 tightened: written permission for in-app price/stock display plus a quote of <=$1,000/month by 2026-12-15. If K5 fails, the price line runs on public prints only and K1 is re-measured on a demo without distributor price display. If K1 then fails, the SaaS (and so DD2) is killed (s10, s11).

### RT-VC-DD2-04 — MAJOR — VC — §2.4 'What must be true' / INPUTS tam_us_smb_oems=6,000 ('unsourced order-of-magnitude')

> This is the low-ACV picks-and-shovels SaaS I have lost money on. $1M ARR needs 225–249 accounts (3.7–4.2% of a guessed 6,000) at a $335 ARPA, sold against SiliconExpert's $250 Essentials tier and Octopart's free BOM tool with lifecycle flags. Even 10% penetration of 6,000 at $4,020/yr is $2.4M ARR, and that is the absolute ceiling before the 2028 turn removes the price-motivated half (−57% GLUT, −86% SEVERE on that module). The TAM itself has no source.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A sourced count (Census County Business Patterns, NAICS 3341/3342/3345/3391 at 20–500 employees, filtered for memory on board) of at least 4,000 firms. Plus K2 showing under 40% say Octopart, SiliconExpert or their EMS already covers the need.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The 6,000 was unsourced. My one attempt to source it found a third-party aggregator figure of 9,698 NAICS 334 firms of all sizes (Mewayz, via snippet). It has not been checked against Census, and the Census API was unreachable from this session. Taking ~35% with 20-499 staff, adding ~600 electronic medical-device firms, and keeping the ~70% that buy memory line-item gives ~2,400. I now use 3,000, range 2,000-4,500 [ESTIMATE]. That fails your >=4,000 bar. On the revised model, $1M ARR at m36 needs 3.3x the trial inflow and ~235 accounts, 7.8% of 3,000; $2M needs 6.4x and ~476 accounts, 15.9%. Pure SaaS needs 245 accounts per $1M at $4,083/yr. This confirms your reading: a low-ACV picks-and-shovels SaaS with a realistic 36-month ceiling of $0.15-0.3M ARR, not a venture.

*Action taken:* Model: tam_us_smb_oems 6,000->3,000 with the derivation in the INPUTS basis; s8 penetration figures recomputed. Deep dive s2.4 and s14 state the $0.1-0.3M ARR side-business ceiling, and the recommendation is demoted from FALLBACK to side line. A Census CBP pull (NAICS 3341/3342/3345/3391, 20-499 employees) is logged as an open gap.

### RT-VC-DD2-05 — MAJOR — VC — §3 customer #1 / §6 FTO risk #3 / INPUTS trials_at_launch=6 ('founder network + day-job peers')

> Founder conflict is sharpest here. The day job is product development and operations at a design/manufacturing company, which is the buyer persona. The plan names the employer as design partner #1 and draws the first 6 trials a month from 'day-job peers'. A standard invention-assignment and moonlighting clause would let the employer claim the code or bar selling into its own industry and suppliers. The founder's procurement contacts are likely bound by confidentiality over PO prices, which is the very data the panel needs. If clearance fails, the launch funnel and the panel lose their first source together.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written carve-out from the employer before any code is written, covering the product, the target industry, and third parties contributing their own PO data.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The plan built a prototype in kill-test week 1, before the written clearance that s6 itself required, and drew launch trials from day-job peers. A standard invention-assignment and moonlighting clause could let the employer claim the code or bar the business. The founder's procurement contacts are bound on PO prices, which is the very data the panel needs. If clearance is refused, the funnel and the panel lose their first source together, so this is a binary risk to DD2 for this founder.

*Action taken:* New gate K0: a written carve-out from the employer covering the product, the target industry and third-party PO contributions, signed before any code is written. If it is refused, DD2 is dead for this founder. The employer is no longer design partner #1 unless the carve-out names it. Model: trials_at_launch 6->4 (no day-job-peer trials) and saas_launch_month 5->6. DOABLE verdict made conditional on K0. Kill-test budget +$500 for the founder's own attorney to review the employment agreement.

### RT-VET-DD2-01 — MAJOR — VET — §1 steps 3-4 / §8 Q3 gate ('≥10 contributors, ≥5 per DDR4/DDR5 cell')

> A memory price cell that means anything is density x organization x speed x temperature grade x package x supplier class. Examples: DDR4 8Gb x16-3200 commercial vs industrial-temp (Micron IT, ISSI and Alliance at large premiums), DDR3L 4Gb, LPDDR4, eMMC by capacity, SPI NOR by density, industrial SO-DIMMs from Innodisk/ATP/Swissbit/Virtium. A 60-person OEM buys DRAM a few times a year, often as LTB or broker buys. With 10-20 contributors, at most one or two cells will have 5 buyers in a given month. Everything else is composition noise: the index moves with who bought what, not with price. That is on top of the ~2x spot/contract basis risk §5 already concedes.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* From the K4 contributors' 24-month PO histories: 3 or more cells with 5 or more independent buyers in 80% or more of months, and bootstrapped month-to-month index noise below one-third of the TrendForce contract move over the same period.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. A meaningful cell is density x organization x speed x temperature grade x package x supplier class, and a 60-person OEM buys DRAM a few times a year, often as LTB or broker buys. With 10-20 contributors a monthly index measures who bought what, not price, on top of the spot/contract basis risk s5 already conceded. The model never reaches even the 15-contributor proxy in 36 months, and a 20-contributor gate changes nothing (still never published).

*Action taken:* Index made quarterly (more observations per cell) and limited to <=4 grade-homogeneous cells (see RT-PHY-DD2-02). Model: panel_min_contributors 15; 20-contributor variant in model s10. Your test replaces the old m9 gate: publish only if the K4 contributors' 24-month PO histories give >=3 cells with >=5 independent buyers in >=80% of quarters, and bootstrapped period-to-period noise below one-third of the TrendForce contract move over the same period.

### RT-VET-DD2-02 — MAJOR — VET — §1 ('Memory has no such index') / §5 win condition 2

> In practice there is a reference: TrendForce/DRAMeXchange publishes monthly contract prices (DDR4 8Gb, DDR5 16Gb, PC modules, server RDIMMs). Memory suppliers, module houses and large OEMs already negotiate against those prints, whatever DXI is. In the EMS segment (§3 #10), turnkey memory is normally passed through at actual invoice cost under open-book terms, which needs no index. Counterparties will pick the print their supplier already quotes against, or pass through actual cost. A one-person index has to beat both, not a vacuum.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* In K3, 2 signed commitments in which the counterparty explicitly considered and rejected both TrendForce contract prints and open-book pass-through in favor of this index.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. Suppliers, module houses and large OEMs already negotiate against TrendForce/DRAMeXchange contract prints (8GB module base since 2019, TrendForce 2018-11-05, via snippet). In EMS, turnkey memory is normally passed through at invoice cost under open-book terms, which needs no index. A one-person index has to beat both, not a vacuum. The licence universe shrinks to OEM-to-customer surcharges where the end customer refuses open-book and TrendForce has no matching cell.

*Action taken:* Tier-2/3 EMS (#10) removed as an index target (kept as a Pro SaaS prospect). s1/s5 rewritten. K3 commitments must be signed LOIs that state why neither TrendForce prints nor open-book pass-through serves that contract, for example because the end customer refuses open-book and TrendForce has no industrial-temperature DDR4 cell. Model: index_rate 0.60->0.40.

### RT-VET-DD2-03 — MAJOR — VET — §12 index +31%/+34% in GLUT/SEVERE / INPUTS vol_ref, index_rate, index_floor

> The model makes index demand rise with volatility in either direction. The industry has already run that experiment. DRAM roughly tripled in 2016-18 and fell about 60% in four quarters in 2018-19 (01_LANDSCAPE §1 row 4a), and no memory escalation index or clause-licensing business came out of it; DD2 found none in 2026. In a crash the payer simply stops paying the surcharge (Unipi: 'reduced once memory prices normalize'). Nobody licenses a $6k/yr index to compute a refund they can negotiate away. If GLUT index revenue at m36 is half of BASE ($2,785/month) instead of $7,318, GLUT m36 ARR falls by about $54k, from $334k to about $280k [ESTIMATE: (7,318−2,785)x12].

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* One documented 2019 or 2023 commercial hardware contract that invoked an index-based memory down-adjustment, or 2 K3 commitments whose draft clause includes a symmetric down-adjustment.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The 2016-18 tripling and the ~60% fall in 2018-19 produced no memory escalation index. My search for an index-linked down-adjustment in 2018-19 or 2023 found only contract-price reporting and no clause (TrendForce 2018-11-05; TechNews 2019-08-01, via snippet). In a crash the payer drops the surcharge, as Unipi's 'reduced once memory prices normalize' already says. On the Phase 4 inputs your mechanism cuts GLUT index revenue at m36 from $7,318 to $3,371/month (-54%, now below BASE's $3,859). GLUT m36 ARR falls by $87,082 to $246,895. That is larger than your -$54k estimate because the lost index revenue also delays the founder's full-time switch: the salary is covered at m30 instead of m23.

*Action taken:* Model: vol_down_weight 1.0->0.0 (a falling move adds no demand beyond index_floor), index_churn_falling 0.008->0.03 (licences cancelled while prices fall >5% a quarter). The 'index earns more in a glut' claim is withdrawn from s0, s1 and s12, and the s12 module table is recomputed.

### RT-HYP-DD2-06 — MINOR — HYP — §6 FTO risk 1 / INPUTS nexar_standard $300, display_licence $1,000

> Octopart/Nexar terms bar historical use and bar 'software developers building applications for that market'. The $300 and $1,000 monthly figures guess at prices nobody has published. At $1,000 for Nexar and $2,500 for display, the cash low drops to $15.3k and full-time slips to m28 (my run). A refusal removes live pricing, which is the C-80 buy/hold feature.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written Nexar or DigiKey licence quote covering in-app price display.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Your run reproduces: Nexar at $1,000 and display at $2,500 on the Phase 4 inputs give a cash low of $15,263 and full-time at m28. Nexar's paid tier prices were still unpublished on 2026-11-01 (compare-plans page, via snippet), so neither my figures nor yours are sourced. I moved both to the midpoint: Nexar Standard $300->$600, Pro $1,000->$2,000, display licence $1,000->$2,000. Fixed data costs are now $3,167/month from m12, which makes m12 gross margin -13%. On the revised model your stress gives a cash low of -$1,884 at m18, so the $40k would be exhausted.

*Action taken:* Model: nexar_standard 300->600, nexar_pro 1,000->2,000, display_licence 1,000->2,000; HYP stress reported in model s10. K5 now requires written permission plus a quote of <=$1,000/month; above that, in-app price display is deferred until >=20 accounts. s0 capital note changed: 'T0, barely'.

### RT-HYP-DD2-07 — MINOR — HYP — §6 FTO risk 2 (antitrust) / INPUTS legal_onetime $7k

> Pooling competing OEMs' purchase prices for the same allocated parts is the information-exchange pattern DOJ has targeted since it withdrew the safety zones in 2023; its 2024 RealPage complaint attacks pooled pricing data [UNVERIFIED-training]. A one-off $7k review is not an ongoing compliance program. Risk-averse procurement and legal teams like mine would decline to contribute on that basis alone.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written antitrust opinion that approves the specific cell rules (count, lag, aggregation), accepted by contributors' counsel during K4.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Accepted. U.S. agencies withdrew the information-exchange safety zones in 2023, and DOJ's 2024 RealPage complaint targets pooled nonpublic pricing data [UNVERIFIED-training]. Pooling buyers' purchase prices is still an information exchange among competitors. A one-off $7k review is not a compliance programme, and contributors' counsel will rightly ask for one. The cost is real but modest. A $5,000 opinion lowers the cash low by $5,000 on the Phase 4 inputs, and the revised BASE cash low of $6,100 already includes it. Beyond what the index kill already took, prospects are not materially lower.

*Action taken:* Model: antitrust_opinion $0->$5,000 at m2 (written opinion on cell rules before any contributor data arrives), antitrust_compliance_month $0->$400 from index publication; the $7k m8 review is kept. Cell rules written into s6/s10: >=5 contributors per cell, >=1-quarter lag, no contributor >25% of a cell's weight, aggregates only, no forward-looking data. K4 counts only contributors whose counsel accepts those rules.

### RT-PHY-DD2-03 — MINOR — PHY — §1 step [2] C-29 ('this EEPROM can become an F-RAM') / §8 Q3 NVM cross-reference

> Automated EEPROM/NOR→F-RAM/MRAM swap alerts are not drop-in recommendations. F-RAM reads are destructive and count toward endurance (FM18W08-class 1e14): a word polled at 1 MHz hits 1e14 in ~3.2 years. Many F-RAMs run at 2.0-3.6 V, versus 1.7-5.5 V for EEPROMs. F-RAM tops out around 8-16 Mbit, so it cannot replace 128 Mbit-2 Gbit NOR code storage. Everspin's 16 Mb parallel MRAM is rated to only 8,000 A/m (Everspin AN 02049). An unvalidated swap alert that a customer acts on becomes a design-failure liability.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Each cross-reference pair ships with automatic checks on voltage, density, endurance including reads, temperature grade, command set and magnetic rating, plus a counsel-reviewed 'engineering validation required' disclaimer.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct on every physics point. F-RAM reads are destructive and count toward ~1e14 endurance. Many F-RAMs run at 2.0-3.6 V where EEPROMs run at 1.7-5.5 V. F-RAM tops out near 8-16 Mbit, so it cannot replace 128 Mbit-2 Gbit NOR code storage. Everspin's 16 Mb parallel MRAM is rated only to 8,000 A/m. An alert saying 'this EEPROM can become an F-RAM' that a customer acts on is a design-failure liability. C-29 carries no modelled revenue, so prospects are unchanged.

*Action taken:* Cross-reference pairs ship only after automated checks on supply-voltage range, density, endurance counting reads at the customer's stated access rate, temperature grade, package/pinout, command set and magnetic-field rating. Each carries a counsel-reviewed 'candidate only; engineering validation required' disclaimer, and the wording 'can become' is removed from s1. E&O insurance ($150/month) stays in the model.

### RT-PHY-DD2-04 — MINOR — PHY — §1 C-61 LTB sizer / §2.1 LTB study

> The LTB sizer ignores storage physics. Multi-year LTB stock of BGA DRAM and eMMC is typically MSL-3, so J-STD-033 floor-life and bake limits apply. Repeated bakes grow intermetallics and degrade solderability (J-STD-002 retest), and long holds need dry or N2 storage. Without an attrition and holding-cost term, the sizer systematically under-buys, and that error is only found at the reflow line years later.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The sizer exposes attrition, bake-cycle and storage-cost inputs anchored to die-bank practice (e.g., Rochester), and a back-test on one design partner's past LTB.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct. Multi-year LTB stock of BGA DRAM and eMMC is typically MSL-3. J-STD-033 floor life, repeated bakes, intermetallic growth, solderability retest and dry or N2 storage all add cost and attrition. A sizer without them under-buys, and nobody finds out until the reflow line years later. The fix is cheap and lowers study margin from 67% to 62%.

*Action taken:* LTB sizer spec gains inputs for attrition %, expected bake cycles, MSL floor life, yearly storage cost (dry cabinet, N2 or a GEIA-STD-0003/Rochester-style bank) and solderability-retest interval. Every LTB study gets a storage-and-attrition section. Model: ltb_cogs_share 0.30->0.35. No LTB study is sold until the sizer is back-tested on one design partner's past LTB (s8).

### RT-PHY-DD2-05 — MINOR — PHY — §1 step [2] alerts / §10 step 1 lifecycle source (DigiKey lifecycle field, PDN crawler)

> For DRAM, the event that forces requalification usually comes as a die-revision or fab-transfer PCN under an unchanged MPN, not as a PDN. Such a change can alter IDD, tRFC and refresh or retention behaviour. DigiKey and Nexar lifecycle status stays 'Active' through it. An alert engine keyed on PDNs and lifecycle fields therefore misses the change that actually costs an industrial or medtech OEM a requalification.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A 12-month back-test showing the crawler catches ≥90% of die-revision PCNs from Micron, ISSI, Winbond and Nanya.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct, and it is the most useful objection for the SaaS. Die-revision, fab-transfer and assembly-site PCNs under an unchanged MPN leave the DigiKey/Nexar lifecycle field at 'Active', and they are what force requalification at industrial and medtech OEMs. A PDN-only engine misses them. Catching them is also the one thing free lifecycle flags do not do, so it becomes the SaaS's lead differentiator. The extra MVP scope costs one month of launch, about $11k of m36 ARR on the Phase 4 inputs, and that cost is inside the revised BASE.

*Action taken:* PCN parsing (die revision, fab transfer, assembly site) added to MVP scope. Model: saas_launch_month 5->6, shared with RT-VC-DD2-05. The product claims 'PDN coverage only' until a 12-month back-test shows >=90% capture of die-revision PCNs from Micron, ISSI, Winbond and Nanya. K2b tests whether buyers missed such PCNs.

### RT-VC-DD2-06 — MINOR — VC — §2.3 / INPUTS trials_growth=2.0, trials_cap_pt=25, founder_sales_h_per_trial=1.0

> Qualified trials ramp from 6 to 25 a month on $900/month of marketing and $35 per trial, while a 15 h/week founder also builds the product, the PDN crawler, the index methodology and monthly content. Twenty-five one-hour demos take ~25 of ~65 monthly hours. Price or close rate 30% low pushes the salary date to 'never' (§2.3), so this unbenchmarked volume ramp carries the whole case.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The kill test's 25 qualified calls booked within 3 weeks from cold outreach outside the employer's network, showing that 25 a month is reachable part-time.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. At 15 h/week (~65 h/month), 25 one-hour demos plus follow-up leave too little time for the MVP, the PCN crawler, the panel and the monthly content. On the Phase 4 inputs the cap cut alone lowers m36 ARR from $421,548 to $392,865 and delays the salary from m23 to m25. In the revised model it is one of the four funnel changes (with conversion 0.10, 4 launch trials and the m6 launch) that together keep the founder part-time through m36. With the Phase 4 funnel restored, the revised model would pay the founder from m31.

*Action taken:* Model: trials_cap_pt 25->20. Your test adopted as K6: the 25 qualified calls must be booked within 21 days from cold outreach outside the employer's network. If not, the part-time funnel volume is not reachable and DD2 stops (s10, s11).

### RT-VET-DD2-04 — MINOR — VET — §6 IP ('contributed price panel ... the only real moat') / INPUTS contributor_share 0.40, contributor_discount 0.10

> Distributor and franchised-supplier price agreements commonly carry confidentiality terms. Procurement leads at medtech and defense-adjacent OEMs will need contracts or legal sign-off before handing 24 months of PO prices to a one-person startup. The model assumes 40% of accounts do so for a 10% discount ($25-65/month). That is a weak trade for a supplier-confidentiality risk. K4 already gates this, so MINOR, but the K4 bar is too soft.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K4 commitments signed by each contributor's legal or contracts function, not only the buyer, and stating that there is no supplier-confidentiality conflict.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. Distributor and franchised-supplier agreements commonly carry price-confidentiality terms. Medtech and defense-adjacent procurement leads cannot hand over 24 months of PO prices without legal sign-off, and a 10% discount ($25-65/month) is a poor trade for that risk. Combined with the panel gate, this is part of why the SaaS-fed index never publishes in the revised model.

*Action taken:* Model: contributor_share 0.40->0.25. K4 commitments must be signed by each contributor's legal or contracts function and must state there is no supplier-confidentiality conflict. The discount is kept but no longer treated as the reason anyone contributes.

### RT-VET-DD2-05 — MINOR — VET — §1 step 2 / C-29 NVM cross-reference (EEPROM/NOR -> F-RAM/MRAM)

> For most parts this is not a real alternate path. SPI NOR runs to 1-2 Gb at roughly $1-5, while F-RAM tops out at a few Mb and Toggle MRAM at 16-32 Mb for $40+ (DD3 §0: $21,504/GB). EEPROMs are multi-sourced commodities (Microchip, ST, onsemi, Rohm) that seldom truly go EOL, and F-RAM drop-ins cost 5-10x more. For customer #9 (medtech), any memory substitution is a design change under FDA design controls. The feature will generate alerts, not swaps.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Across the 5 design-partner BOMs, at least 1 NVM part per BOM that has an EOL notice and a pin-compatible F-RAM/MRAM alternate within 3x the price.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Accepted. SPI NOR at 1-2 Gb for $1-5 has no F-RAM (a few Mb) or Toggle MRAM (16-32 Mb at $40+) replacement. EEPROMs are multi-sourced commodities that rarely go truly EOL, and F-RAM drop-ins cost 5-10x more. For medtech, any memory substitution is a design change under FDA design controls. The feature generates alerts, not swaps. C-29 carries no modelled revenue, so prospects are unchanged.

*Action taken:* C-29 demoted from the pitch to a parametric candidate filter, scoped to NVSRAM/battery-backed SRAM and high-write serial EEPROM sockets where F-RAM/MRAM is a genuine candidate. Medtech accounts see a design-control warning. Your test adopted at m3: unless the 5 design-partner BOMs show >=1 NVM part per BOM with an EOL notice and a pin-compatible F-RAM/MRAM alternate within 3x the price, the feature is dropped (s8).

### RT-VET-DD2-06 — MINOR — VET — §1 / C-61 LTB sizer and LTB studies ($3,500)

> In 2026, suppliers took DDR4 LTB orders subject to allocation, so the sizer's optimal quantity is moot when the supplier caps the fill. Micron Manassas 1-alpha DDR4 (since 2026-05-22) and Micron's stated ongoing DDR4/LP4 commitment (01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 11) also blunt the EOL panic for the biggest DRAM line on SMB BOMs. Any LTB study that ignores storage is incomplete. Holding DRAM 5-10 years needs J-STD-033 dry/N2 storage and periodic solderability checks, or a GEIA-STD-0003/Rochester-style bank. Those costs and yield losses change the answer.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* 3 design partners with a 2026-27 DRAM LTB that the supplier filled at 90% or more of the requested quantity, plus an LTB study template that prices storage and solderability.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Accepted. In 2026 suppliers took DDR4 LTB orders subject to allocation, so an 'optimal' quantity is moot when the supplier caps the fill. Micron's continuing DDR4/LP4 commitment (Manassas 1-alpha) also blunts the EOL panic for the largest DRAM line on SMB BOMs. Storage and solderability are handled under RT-PHY-DD2-04. LTB studies are excluded from ARR. Together, the attach and COGS changes lower the revised cash low by about $1.1k.

*Action taken:* LTB studies re-scoped to allocation split across sources, redesign timing and storage cost, not just quantity. Model: ltb_attach 0.015->0.0125, ltb_cogs_share 0.30->0.35. Kill-test calls now ask whether 2026 DRAM LTBs were filled at >=90% of the requested quantity.

## DD3 — Drop-in Non-Volatile Continuity Modules (nv-continuity-modules)

**Final status after red team: KILLED.** After the red team, the revised BASE loses $29.8k over 36 months (Phase 4: +$79.1k). It breaks even only in m19, never pays the founder, and needs about $49k of peak cash (cash low -$9.2k), above the $40k deployable. The main cause is the market: once i-series controls (which take a $20 CF card) and the unfound DMSMS contracts are removed, addressable demand is about 2,591 cards/yr (~$0.62M/yr), and a 36-month cumulative breakeven needs 33 cards/month, 30% of revised U.S. demand. The F-RAM DIP leg (/CE-latch timing) and C-16 (hold-up energy still needed) are killed outright. The card leg is physically sound but unprofitable at any evidenced volume. ARR figures are run-rate (month revenue x 12); recurring ARR at m36 is only $2.7k.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: YES, conditionally: TRL-9 parts and no physical show-stopper, but the card now needs dual-supply translators, a mandatory CPLD for 8-bit steering and power-fail gating, and a magnetic shield. Magnetic immunity above 8,000 A/m (~10 mT), hot removal and acceptance by a pre-i Fanuc control are all untested. · Profitable: NO: BASE 36-month net -$29.8k (GLUT -$29.8k, SEVERE -$36.9k), breakeven m19, run-rate $29.4k at m18 and $39.9k at m36, recurring ARR $2.7k, founder never paid. Even a demand-validated 40 cards/month case nets only $13.3k over 36 months. · Doable by this founder: PARTLY: the work (contract EE, U.S. EMS, store, compatibility database) still fits a part-time software and operations founder, but the full plan's peak cash need of ~$49k exceeds the $40k deployable. Only a lean re-plan fits T0, and it still loses $22.4k.

### RT-HYP-DD3-01 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 pain / §10 Track B targets / INPUTS legacy_share 0.35

> The pain is weakest on the very controls the bench will validate. On i-series controls (16i/18i/21i/0i), boot-system SRAM backup works with a CF/ATA card in a PCMCIA adapter. A published 0i-Mate CF backup procedure exists, and an 18i-T note says flash cards work in boot mode while SRAM cards are needed only for normal-mode I/O (industrialmonitordirect; snippet grade, and forum reports are mixed). On those controls, the $249 card competes with a ~$20 CF card and adapter for the main job. The SRAM-only need sits on non-i Series 0/15/16/18 controls, which are older and retiring faster. legacy_share 0.35 lumps the two groups together.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Fanuc maintenance-manual citation, or a 20-shop survey, showing that i-series users need an SRAM card for backup and restore. Or a TAM split that sizes the non-i installed base separately.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct, and it hits the core of the TAM. The Phase 4 legacy_share of 0.35 mixed two groups: pre-i controls that need an SRAM card, and i-series controls where boot-menu SRAM backup to a CF card in an adapter is a documented routine (IMD 0i-Mate CF procedure; IMD calls it 'virtually identical' across 0i/16i/18i/21i). On i-series the residual need is real but partial. An 18i-T needs an SRAM card for normal-mode I/O, and one CNCzone 16iA user got an 'NMI occurred' alarm formatting a CF card. Memex documents the SRAM card as the backup medium for 16-B/16-C/18-B/18-C (M100741G manual, Phase 5 search). I could not find a Fanuc maintenance-manual excerpt showing i-series controls rejecting ATA cards for SRAM backup, so I cannot refute the objection. I re-split the TAM. Pre-i: 15% of cumulative units, 40% still running. i-series: 20% share, 60% running, 20% residual SRAM-card need. U.S. machines fall from 131,250 to 63,000 and U.S. card demand from 3,076 to 1,329 a year (model §[3]).

*Action taken:* Model: legacy_share 0.35->0.15, legacy_still_running 0.50->0.40, new iseries_share 0.20 / iseries_still_running 0.60 / iseries_card_need 0.20 (RED-TEAM block). Deep dive: pitch narrowed to pre-i controls (§0, §1); the bench target moves to a pre-i 16-C/18-C or 21-B (§10 step 2); the reopen probe records control model and caps i-series deposits at one-third of the count (§10).

### RT-HYP-DD3-02 — MAJOR — HYP — §2.5 TAM / INPUTS card_units_steady_month 40, card_replacement_years 8

> 40 cards a month is 16% of the DD's own U.S. demand of 3,076 cards a year. An MRAM card never wears out, so each sale also removes a future 8-year replacement, and the model applies no decay. At half the volume (volume_mult 0.5, my run), 36-month net is $17.7k. The demand chain rests on a single 2022 Fanuc production milestone multiplied by five unsourced factors: U.S. 15%, legacy 35%, still running 50%, 8 machines per shop, and ×1.5 for other families.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Sell-through data from Memex, Do Supply or MRO Electric showing at least 1,000 SRAM-card units a year in North America, or at least 30 paid deposits in track A.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. I reproduced the reviewer's run: volume x0.5 on the Phase 4 model gives $17.7k of 36-month net. With the TAM corrected (HYP-01), 40 cards a month would be 19% of the revised addressable market and 36% of U.S. demand, which is not credible for a part-time new entrant. Phase 4 also held demand flat while every battery-free card removes a future 8-year replacement. Revised BASE: 16 cards/month steady state (7% of SAM, 14% of U.S. demand), decaying 10%/yr after launch (about 6% retirement plus 4% self-depletion). Result: 36-month net -$29.8k (was +$79.1k), breakeven m19 (was m11), run-rate $29.4k at m18 and $39.9k at m36. Removing the decay alone helps only to -$25.9k. A 36-month cumulative breakeven needs 33 cards/month. I have no sell-through data from Memex, Do Supply or MRO Electric, so I cannot meet the reviewer's bar of 1,000 units a year.

*Action taken:* Model: card_units_steady_month 40->16; new card_demand_decay_yr 0->0.10; sensitivity rows added for volume x0.5, no decay, back to 40/mo, and demand-validated 40/mo. Deep dive §2.2-2.6 rewritten. The reopen probe requires >=30 full-list deposits plus a >=100-card/yr reseller LOI, or reseller sell-through >=1,000 cards/yr in North America (§10).

### RT-HYP-DD3-03 — MAJOR — HYP — §2.1 COGS / INPUTS card_frame_kit_connector_usd (source note: 'one 68-pos connector is Last Time Buy') / mr4a16b_lead_weeks 26

> The continuity product rests on parts that are obsolete themselves. The model's own source note says a 68-pin PC Card connector is on Last Time Buy. Frame kits have no small-quantity price, and MR4A16B is single-source with a 26-week lead time. As a supplier-qualification lead, I would fail a vendor whose lifecycle product needs an LTB on its own connector in year 1. Five years of connectors at 480 cards a year and ~$12 each is about $29k, nearly three times the $10.8k cash low, and inventory_cover_months 3 does not fund it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two current-production sources of 68-pin PC Card connectors and frames, shown as active at a franchised distributor, at MOQs of 1,000 or less.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid, and I could not refute it with evidence. One Phase 5 search found the following. Hirose IC1K-68RD-1.27SF(71) is Last Time Buy (Cytech). The FCI 90937 Type I/II part is reported obsolete. 3M MCH068 shows only broker stock (AERI). A TE page says 68-position receptacle assemblies with frames are offered, but gives no status or MOQ. NX1-series frame kits appear on an RS datasheet with no status. I found no franchised, current-production, card-side source with a stated MOQ, so the reviewer's would-change-my-mind bar is unmet. The reviewer's $29k figure assumed 480 cards a year for 5 years. At the revised volume the model needs 288 cards from m11 to m36, so a lifetime buy of 350 sets at $15 costs $5.2k. That sum is not trivial: it moves the BASE cash low by about $5.4k (the reverted case is -$3.8k). An MOQ-1,000 buy takes the cash low to -$18.9k.

*Action taken:* Model: card_frame_kit_connector_usd $12->$15; new ltb_connector_sets 350 bought at ltb_month 9 (section [0b]); sensitivity rows for an MOQ-1,000 LTB and for no LTB. Kill criterion 5 (after reopen): no two current-production card-side sources and no LTB quote of <=$20 landed at <=500 sets -> kill. The $299 reprice path is dropped.

### RT-HYP-DD3-04 — MAJOR — HYP — §4 pricing / INPUTS card2_price_direct $249

> The $249 anchor is a CDW-G federal list price (Synchrotech $239.39). MRO buyers anchor on $160 used cards and, on i-series, on $20 CF cards. The DD itself says price decides the business. At price ×0.8, 36-month net is $27.2k; at ×0.8 with no LTSA it is $13.2k (my runs), about $6 per part-time hour.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A deposits at $249 with no discount code, plus at least 2 reseller LOIs at $175 wholesale.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Partly valid. For the pre-i controls that are now the core target, the $20 CF anchor does not apply because those controls reject CF. The $160-200 used-battery-card anchor does apply, and $249 is a federal list-price anchor (CDW-G/Synchrotech $239.39). I re-ran the price stress on the revised model. Price x0.8 gives 36-month net -$42.7k with no breakeven, against -$29.8k at $249. Price x1.2 gives -$16.9k, so a higher price does not rescue the concept either. The plan now tests price before any hardware spend.

*Action taken:* Reopen probe: deposits count only at full list ($249/$349) with no discount codes. The reseller ask is >=$175 wholesale on a >=100-card/yr LOI. The deposit form records the buyer's alternative (used card price, Synchrotech, CF, RS-232). Sensitivity row 'RT price x0.8' added (model §[6]). Kill criterion 11: realized blended price <$175 for 3 months -> exit.

### RT-PHY-DD3-01 — MAJOR — PHY — §10 track B added magnetic test / §1 'reads/writes like SRAM' / §2.6 must-be-true #4

> Everspin rates 16 Mb parallel Toggle MRAM at Hmax_write = 8,000 A/m (≈100 Oe ≈ 10 mT) (MR4A16B datasheet Rev 11.7; Everspin AN 02049). Machine shops are full of permanent magnets: dial-indicator magnetic bases, magnetic tool strips and part trays, magnetic chucks. These reach roughly 0.1-1 T at contact (80-800 kA/m) [ESTIMATE: typical ferrite/NdFeB surface fields], 10-100× over the limit. Over-limit exposure can flip bits, which is permanent loss of the very parameters the card exists to protect, a failure battery SRAM cards do not have. The planned '72 h beside a spindle motor' test measures stray fields of a few mT and is the wrong test.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Calibrated exposures with a gaussmeter at 10, 30 and 100 mT, in standby and during write, with full-array CRC verify. Then data retained after a 1-minute contact with a dial-indicator base and 10 cycles on a magnetic chuck, with a ≤$5 Ni-Fe shield inside the 3.3 mm Type I envelope (or a warning label plus a returns allowance in the model).

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct on the numbers: mu0 x 8,000 A/m = 10.05 mT. Permanent-magnet contact fields of 0.1-1 T are 10-100x over the rating. A Toggle free layer is a synthetic antiferromagnet with near-zero net moment, which gives more standby margin than a single layer. But a field above the SAF's saturation field (tens of mT [ESTIMATE]) aligns both layers, and when the field is removed the bit can relax into either state. Contact with a magnetic base can plausibly scramble data. The datasheet guarantees nothing above 8,000 A/m, so only a test settles it. A thin Ni-Fe foil helps against fringing fields, but it can saturate near 0.7-0.8 T at contact, so its effect must be measured too. This is a failure mode the product introduces and the lithium card does not have, so it can kill the card line. I adopt the reviewer's protocol in full.

*Action taken:* Model: card_shield_usd $0->$3; one_time +$0.3k at m1 for the gaussmeter and test magnets; warranty_returns_pct raised (shared with VC-06). Deep dive §1 adds the magnetic limit; §10 step 2 tests calibrated 10/30/100 mT in standby and during write with a full-array CRC, then 60 s contact with a dial-indicator base and 10 magnetic-chuck cycles, shielded and unshielded, with an envelope check. Kill criterion 3: a shielded card that loses any bit at 30 mT or after 60 s on a 0.3 T base kills the card line. Failure only on chuck contact means a label plus a warranty exclusion.

### RT-VC-DD3-01 — MAJOR — VC — §0 pitch / §1 'How it works' / §6 'Can't clone Fanuc boards'

> The product does not replace the battery-backed memory that loses the machine's parameters; it replaces the backup card. The loss event in §1 ('If that battery dies during a shutdown, the settings are gone') happens in the control's internal SRAM and battery (for example the A20B-2900-0531 module), which §6 says cannot be cloned. Any working backup solves that pain: a battery card checked once a year, RS-232/DNC to a laptop, or a CF card where the control accepts one. The real comparison is a battery-free backup medium at $249 against a $160 used card, not 'save my machine'. The pitch overstates the value proposition and therefore the willingness to pay.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A deposits (at least 15) from buyers who, when asked, say they chose $249 battery-free over a $160–200 used battery card. Or a legal route to a drop-in replacement for the internal SRAM module itself.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. The loss event happens in the control's internal battery-backed SRAM (for example the A20B-2900-0531 module), which we cannot clone. Our card replaces the backup medium. Its value is that the backup itself does not silently die (Mitsubishi T30-0007). That is real, but it is a smaller claim than 'save my machine', and RS-232/DNC, a checked battery card, or CF (on i-series) all compete for the same job. This lowers confidence in willingness to pay at $249 against a $160 used card, and it is one reason BASE volume falls to 16 cards a month. No legal route to a drop-in for the internal SRAM module was found.

*Action taken:* Deep dive §0 pitch rewritten ('battery-free backup card; does not replace the internal SRAM or prevent the loss event'); §1 and §4 messaging changed. The reopen probe's deposit form asks what the buyer would otherwise buy; volume is cut (see HYP-02).

### RT-VC-DD3-02 — MAJOR — VC — §10 Track B validation target / §2.5 TAM INPUTS legacy_share=0.35

> The first validation target, Fanuc 16i/18i/21i or 0i-A, is the family where the cheap substitute already works. On i-series controls, boot-menu SRAM backup to CompactFlash in a PCMCIA adapter is a documented routine (IMD 'Fanuc 0i-Mate parameter backup to CompactFlash procedure'; dedicated CF-to-PCMCIA adapters recommended; snippets 2026-11-01). DD3's own CF-rejection source concerns the older Series 20/21, and Memex pairs SRAM cards with the 16/18-B/C. If only pre-i controls (0-C, 15, 16/18-A/B/C, 20/21, RJ-2) truly need SRAM cards, the '35% on SRAM-card-era controls' overstates demand. The kill test could then 'pass' by proving acceptance on controls whose owners don't need the product.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A compatibility matrix showing which families reject CF in a PCMCIA adapter. Validate first on a CF-rejecting pre-i control (16-C/18-C or 0-C), and re-run the TAM counting only CF-rejecting families.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. Track B would have validated on the family whose owners need the product least, so the test could 'pass' while proving nothing about demand. The evidence split is the one under HYP-01: Memex documents the SRAM card as the medium for 16-B/C and 18-B/C, IMD calls CF backup routine on i-series, and the IMD CF-rejection source concerns Series 20/21. The TAM is re-run counting pre-i controls plus an i-series residual (U.S. 1,329 cards/yr, SAM 2,591/yr). The bench target and the compatibility matrix now start with pre-i.

*Action taken:* §10 step 2: bench a pre-i 16-C/18-C or 21-B first, then i-A, then Mitsubishi QnH. Kill criterion 2 is now a pre-i Fanuc rejection, and Mitsubishi or i-A alone is not enough to continue. Model TAM inputs as under HYP-01.

### RT-VC-DD3-03 — MAJOR — VC — §2.5 TAM chain / INPUTS card_units_steady_month=40, global_over_us_mult=4.0, us_share_installed=0.15

> BASE sells 480 cards a year, 16% of the modeled U.S. demand (3,076/yr), within about two years of launch. The global $2.97M is not addressable in this plan: export classification is 'not assessed' (§7) and every channel named is North American, so the real market is about $0.74M/yr. It also depletes itself: each MRAM card removes an 8-year replacement while the installed base retires, yet the model holds demand flat. The TAM chain is internally inconsistent too: a 15% U.S. share implies global ≈ 6.7× U.S., not the 4.0× used. That tells me it is assembled from guesses.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The 20-shop MRO survey from 03_SCORING §8 giving card purchase frequency per shop, plus sell-through data from Memex, MRO Electric or Do Supply supporting at least 3,000 U.S. cards a year.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Mostly correct. (1) The global multiplier contradicted the model's own 15% U.S. share: 1/0.15 = 6.67, not 4.0. Corrected, for information only. (2) Self-depletion and retirement are now modeled as a 10%/yr decay. (3) Partial counter on addressability: a plain memory card with no cryptography is very likely EAR99 [ESTIMATE, unconfirmed], so eBay and a web store can reach some non-U.S. buyers without a licence. I credit only 15% of non-U.S. demand. Revised SAM = 1.1 x U.S. + 15% of non-U.S. = 2,591 cards/yr, about $0.62M/yr, not $2.97M. That is still well short of the reviewer's 3,000 U.S. cards a year. No 20-shop survey or reseller sell-through data exists.

*Action taken:* Model: global_over_us_mult 4.0->6.67; new sam_over_us_mult 1.10 and sam_intl_reach 0.15; card_demand_decay_yr 0.10. Section [3] prints SAM, U.S. and global separately, and the scale paths report share of SAM. The reopen probe asks each reseller for annual SRAM-card sell-through (§10).

### RT-VC-DD3-04 — MAJOR — VC — §2.2 recurring line / INPUTS ltsa_contracts=[(27, $2,000), (33, $2,000)]

> BASE includes two $24k/yr DMSMS blanket orders from m27 and m33: $48k of the $56.2k m36 recurring ARR and ~25% of m36 revenue. Yet §2.2 says 'no DLA demand for DS12xx-class parts was found', and §3 row 10 estimates 12–24 months to approved-source status after SAM/CAGE registration. A base case should not contain revenue from customers the research could not find. Without them, recurring ARR is $8.2k, m36 run-rate $147.8k and 36-month net $65.1k: about $28 per part-time hour, with no recurring revenue for a founder who asked for it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A DIBBS/NSN buy history showing at least $25k/yr of awards for DS1225/1230/1245-class NVSRAM or PC Card SRAM that a new CAGE could quote within 24 months. Otherwise, move the LTSAs to an upside case.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. A base case should not contain revenue from customers the research could not find. A Phase 5 search for NSN or DLA award history on DS1230/DS1225/DS1245 found only commercial distributor listings. Phase 4 had found no DS12xx NSN demand either, and approved-source status takes 12-24 months after CAGE registration. The LTSAs are removed from BASE. In the old model this alone cost $14.0k of 36-month net. BASE recurring ARR at m36 falls from $56.2k to $2.7k (cloud vault only), so the founder's recurring-revenue goal is not met by this concept.

*Action taken:* Model: ltsa_contracts [(27,$2k),(33,$2k)]->[] in BASE; LTSAs survive only in scale paths S2/S4 and a labeled upside sensitivity row ('+2 LTSAs', 36-month net -$15.8k). Deep dive §2.2 and §3 #10 marked upside-only.

### RT-VC-DD3-05 — MAJOR — VC — §5 'Where you lose' / §2.4 'Price −30%' / §3 customer #1 wholesale offer

> Moat is 2/5, and price is the whole P&L: a 30% price cut leaves $1.2k of 36-month net. The design is three buffers, an MRAM and an attribute EEPROM on a standard frame. The compatibility matrix, the claimed asset, is published as the sales tool (§4). Memex already sells Fanuc cards and is customer #1. Offering it wholesale at $175 shows it the cost structure, and once a card is in the field it or a Shenzhen house can copy it within a lot cycle.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* An exclusive white-label or supply agreement with Memex or MRO Electric (at least 200 cards a year over 2 years). Or evidence that Fanuc or Mitsubishi acceptance depends on non-obvious attribute or CIS behavior the founder can keep as a trade secret.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The moat is 2/5 and price drives the P&L. A Phase 5 search again found no battery-free PC Card SRAM product, so the gap is real, but a gap is not a moat. The design is a few buffers, a CPLD and an MRAM. A copycat entering at m24 at 75% of our price takes revised BASE from -$29.8k to -$39.2k with no breakeven. Offering Memex MOQ-10 wholesale at $175 reveals our price floor to the most likely copier and locks nothing. I can partly answer the 'non-obvious behaviour' question: the CPLD attribute/CIS configuration and the per-family compatibility results can stay trade secrets until the matrix is published. That is weak protection.

*Action taken:* Model: simulate() gains a copycat price step; new sensitivity row 'RT copycat from m24, price x0.75'. §3 and §10: the reseller ask becomes an exclusive white-label commitment of >=200 cards/yr x 2 yrs, which is also a reopen trigger. The compatibility matrix is published only to paying resellers until launch. Kill criterion 11 adds 'battery-free copy listed at <75% of our price -> exit'.

### RT-VET-DD3-01 — MAJOR — VET — §10 track B (validate on Fanuc 16i/18i/21i or 0i-A) / INPUTS legacy_share 0.35 ('0/15/16/18/20/21, i-A')

> The validation target is the wrong control. Fanuc i-series controls (16i/18i/21i, 0i) take boot-menu SRAM backup and parameter/program I/O on a CompactFlash card in a CF-to-PCMCIA adapter (IMD 0i-Mate CF procedure; IMD RJ-3/16i page). The DD's own IMD source says it is the older controls that reject CF. On exactly the controls track B tests, a $20 CF card already removes the battery. The base that genuinely needs an SRAM card is pre-i (Series 0-C, 15, 16/18-A/B/C, 20, 21-B) plus RJ-2, the oldest and fastest-retiring slice. If i-series is excluded (legacy share about 0.15 [ESTIMATE]), global card demand falls from 12,305/yr to about 5,300/yr, and BASE's 480 cards/yr becomes about 9% of global and 36% of U.S. demand.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Fanuc maintenance-manual (boot-system chapter) excerpt showing a common i-series configuration that rejects ATA cards for SRAM backup, or 10 or more of the 15 track A deposits from owners of i-series controls. Also: re-run the TAM without i-series, and move track B to a pre-i control.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. This is the same finding as HYP-01 and VC-02, and it is the single largest change in the red team. Excluding i-series (keeping a residual for i-A normal-mode I/O) cuts U.S. card demand from 3,076 to 1,329 a year. Phase 4's 480 cards a year would be 36% of that. The reviewer estimated about 0.15 for the pre-i share, and I use it. Reverting only the volume and decay changes would move revised BASE from -$29.8k to +$23.1k, so this objection accounts for about $53k of the $109k swing.

*Action taken:* Model: legacy_share 0.35->0.15, legacy_still_running 0.50->0.40, i-series residual rows, card_units_steady_month 40->16. Track B moved to a pre-i control; the reopen probe caps i-series deposits at one-third.

### RT-VET-DD3-02 — MAJOR — VET — §10 track B 'magnetic-field check' tagged [UNVERIFIED risk]

> The risk is not unverified; it is in the datasheet. MR4A16B's absolute-maximum field during write is 8,000 A/m, about 10 mT (Everspin datasheet Rev 11.7 mirror; Everspin app note: 8,000 A/m for 16Mb parallel parts). A magnetic dial-indicator base or tool-tray magnet is hundreds of mT at contact, and surface-grinder chucks and demagnetizers are worse. This product is a backup card that technicians keep in tool boxes and stick to machine panels, and the lithium SRAM card it replaces does not care about magnets. Seventy-two hours 'beside a spindle motor' does not test the real exposure.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A bench result: write a pattern, hold each face of 5 cards against a 0.3 T magnetic base for 60 s, and verify zero bit errors. Or a shielded (mu-metal) design that passes this, with the added BOM cost and thickness checked against the PC Card Type I envelope.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct. The risk is in the datasheet (Hmax_write 8,000 A/m), and the Phase 4 tag of [UNVERIFIED risk] was wrong. The reviewer's protocol is adopted verbatim: write a pattern, hold each face of 5 cards against a 0.3 T magnetic base for 60 s, and verify zero bit errors, shielded and unshielded, with the shield's thickness checked against the 3.3 mm Type I envelope. The physics and the shield are covered under RT-PHY-DD3-01.

*Action taken:* As RT-PHY-DD3-01: card_shield_usd $3, a gaussmeter and magnets at m1, §10 step 2 magnetic tests, and kill criterion 3 (a shielded card that fails at 30 mT or after 60 s on a 0.3 T base kills the card line).

### RT-VET-DD3-03 — MAJOR — VET — §1 'Second product: DIP modules' / INPUTS fm18w08_*, fm28v100_* (DIP line kept on F-RAM)

> The /CE-latch trap that moved the card to MRAM applies just as much to the DIP line, which still uses FM18W08 and FM28V100. These F-RAMs latch the address on /CE falling and need a /CE precharge between accesses (FM18W08: 70 ns access but about 130 ns cycle, per the datasheet as I recall it; verify). DS1225/DS1230-70 are true asynchronous SRAMs that tolerate /CE held low with address-only cycling at 70 ns. Host boards that tie /CE to a static decode, or cycle faster than about 130 ns, will read garbage. DS12xx parts also block writes internally below about 4.5 V, so F-RAM drop-ins need an added supervisor against brown-out corruption. With contribution already at −1% and 17%, a 10-20% host-compatibility return rate turns this line into a cash and reputation sink for the card brand.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A published compatibility list of 10 or more host boards passing, including one with /CE tied low and one cycling at 100 ns or less. Or move the DIPs to a true async NVM (nvSRAM or Toggle MRAM) and re-cost them.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct. The /CE-latch trap that moved the card to MRAM applies equally to the F-RAM DIP line. Hosts that tie /CE to a static decode, or cycle faster than about 130 ns, read garbage. The DS12xx parts also block writes below about 4.5 V, so an F-RAM drop-in would need its own supervisor. The leg's contribution was -1%/+17% in Phase 4 and -4%/+14% with revised returns. DS1230Y and DS1245Y are still in ADI production (Phase 5 search), which weakens the obsolescence premise. A true-async redesign costs more than a $34 price can carry. Restoring the leg makes revised BASE worse (-$32.7k vs -$29.8k).

*Action taken:* DIP leg killed (same actions as RT-PHY-DD3-02): dip_units_steady_month 0, first_lot_dip 0, DIP design cost removed, DIP marketing cut. Card launch becomes first revenue (m11, 2027-09). Kill criterion 13 states the revival conditions.

### RT-VET-DD3-04 — MAJOR — VET — INPUTS card_frame_kit_connector_usd $12 ('no small-qty price found ... one 68-pos connector is Last Time Buy') / §8 G2

> The one part that makes this a PC Card, the 68-position Type I/II connector and frame kit, is end-of-life across the industry. The model's own source note says Last Time Buy. The plan has no qualified source, no MOQ and no LTB stock plan, yet assumes a 36-month product. If the connector source dies, the card line dies. Buying an LTB for 36 months (~1,000+ sets) ties up cash the plan does not have: the cash low is $10.8k. Track B's 'reuse donor shells' is a prototype trick, not a production path.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written quote for 1,000 or more 68-pin PC Card connectors plus frame kits at $20 or less landed, with confirmed availability through 2029, or a funded LTB line in the model.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The one part that makes this a PC Card has no confirmed current-production source (see RT-HYP-DD3-03 for the search results). 'Reuse donor shells' is a prototype trick, as the reviewer says. At revised volume the funded lifetime buy is 350 sets x $15 = $5.2k at m9, not 1,000+ sets, but it still deepens the cash hole. An MOQ of 1,000 at $15 would take the cash low to -$18.9k. The reviewer's written-quote condition is unmet.

*Action taken:* Model: card_frame_kit_connector_usd $12->$15; ltb_connector_sets 350 at m9; sensitivity rows for MOQ 1,000 and for no LTB. §10 step 3 requires two current-production quotes, or an LTB quote of <=500 sets at <=$20 landed, before rev-B. Kill criterion 5.

### RT-VET-DD3-05 — MAJOR — VET — §8 Q2 validation protocol (100 power cycles, 72-h soak, format/restore) / §4 2-year warranty / INPUTS warranty_returns_pct 0.03

> That protocol is a functional check, not a qualification. A handheld card needs ESD testing (IEC 61000-4-2 ±8 kV contact on shell and pins; 74LVC buffers are typically about 2 kV HBM). It also needs connector insertion life, hot removal while a boot-menu write is in flight, cabinet temperature (0-55°C ambient, hotter at the slot), and drop/shock. A backup card that turns out dead on the day the CNC loses its battery is the worst failure this buyer can have. It ends the brand. An outside-lab pass is roughly $5-10k [ESTIMATE], which is material against a $10.8k cash low and is not in the model.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* An outside-lab ESD, temperature and insertion report on 10 rev-A cards before the first 100-card lot, with the cost added to the model and the cash low re-run.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. 100 power cycles, a soak and a format test are a functional check, not a qualification. A backup card that is dead on the day the CNC loses its battery is the worst failure this buyer can have. The plan now funds an outside-lab pass before the first lot: IEC 61000-4-2 +/-8 kV contact on shell and pins, insertion life, and 0-55 C (the reviewer's $5-10k range; $7.5k modeled). Hot removal is added under PHY-05. Together with PHY-06 and the rev-B respin, card launch moves from m9 to m11. The lab alone costs $7.5k of 36-month net, and the launch slip a further $1.6k.

*Action taken:* Model: one_time +$7.5k at m8 for the outside lab; card_launch_month 9->11; warranty_returns_pct raised (shared). §10 step 4 qualification gate; kill criterion 7 (an ESD failure at +/-8 kV contact, or >3% compatibility failure -> no first lot).

### RT-HYP-DD3-05 — MINOR — HYP — §9 team / INPUTS opex_from_launch_month $225 (product liability ~$125)

> MRO resellers and plant procurement onboard vendors on a certificate of insurance (typically $1-2M product liability), a quality manual or ISO 9001, and a formal RMA process. A card that corrupts a parameter restore on a $300k machine invites consequential-loss claims. $125 a month of product liability and no quality system will stall onboarding at Radwell and Do Supply, and the 40% reseller share of card volume depends on those accounts.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Completed vendor onboarding at one named reseller, with its insurance and quality requirements met inside the modeled opex.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. A $125/month policy will not produce a $1-2M product-liability certificate of insurance for MRO vendor onboarding. A card that corrupts a restore on a $300k machine invites consequential-loss claims. I did not budget ISO 9001 certification (about $5-10k plus annual audits [ESTIMATE]), which this volume cannot justify. That leaves a residual risk that Radwell or Do Supply will not onboard us. The model now carries insurance at about $250/month plus a short quality manual and an RMA process. Combined effect: -$5.6k of 36-month net (insurance -$4.1k, QMS -$1.5k).

*Action taken:* Model: opex_from_launch_month $225->$350/month; one_time +$1.5k at m7 for the QMS manual and RMA process. Deep dive §4: terms of sale limit liability to the purchase price, and the COI is obtained before reseller onboarding. Resellers that require ISO 9001 are treated as unreachable rather than funded.

### RT-HYP-DD3-06 — MINOR — HYP — §10 Track B 'added magnetic-field check'

> Everspin's Toggle MRAM datasheets set a finite limit on magnetic-field immunity [UNVERIFIED-training on the exact figure]. Magnetic chucks and lifting magnets on a shop floor produce far stronger fields at contact than a spindle motor. The worst case is a card left on a surface-grinder chuck, not 72 hours beside a spindle motor. One field failure that wipes a backup ends the 'no battery, no data loss' claim.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A test at the datasheet maximum field and at a chuck surface, plus a warranty exclusion or a shielding fix.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. A surface-grinder chuck or a dial-indicator base at contact, not a spindle motor at a distance, is the real exposure. The datasheet guarantees only Hmax_write 8,000 A/m, about 10 mT (MR4A16B Rev 11.7; Everspin AN02049, via reviewer snippets). The Phase 4 '72 h beside a spindle motor' check tested the wrong thing. This is answered in full under RT-PHY-DD3-01.

*Action taken:* Same as RT-PHY-DD3-01: calibrated 10/30/100 mT exposures, a 0.3 T base held 60 s on each face, 10 chuck cycles, a $3 shield in COGS, a $0.3k gaussmeter and magnets at m1, a keep-away label and a warranty exclusion for magnetic-chuck contact. New kill criterion 3.

### RT-PHY-DD3-02 — MINOR — PHY — §1 'Second product: DIP modules' / §2.1 NV-DIP rows / INPUTS: fm18w08_usd_our_qty, dip1m_cogs_parts_other_usd

> §1 drops FM18W08 from the card because of the /CE-latch trap, then builds the DS1225/DS1230 DIP on the same part. FM18W08 latches the address on /CE falling and has a 130 ns cycle with tPC = 60 ns precharge (datasheet Rev 2.0), versus a 70 ns read cycle on DS1230Y-70. Hosts that hold /CE low across consecutive accesses, or run back-to-back cycles under 130 ns, read stale data. FM28V100 (the DS1245 SKU) is a 2.0-3.6 V part: the model includes $2.50 of translators, but the two added translator delays push past the access time of DS1245Y-70 sockets. 'Tested + CoC + warranty' is the DIP line's only claim over free adapters, so compatibility must be proven per host type.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Bench passes on ≥5 host types (e.g., HP 3458A, a Z80 or 6809 arcade board, a PLC), or a DIP redesign on a part with true asynchronous-SRAM timing.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and it reverses §1's own logic. FM18W08 latches the address on /CE falling and has a ~130 ns cycle with a 60 ns precharge, against the DS1230Y-70's 70 ns asynchronous cycle. Hosts that hold /CE low, or that cycle in under 130 ns, read stale data. The FM28V100 version adds two translator delays against a 70 ns socket. A redesign on a true asynchronous core needs a 3.3V Toggle MRAM or nvSRAM plus dual-supply translators and an LDO. That is about $26-28 of COGS [ESTIMATE] against a $34 price, and it competes with DS1230Y/DS1245Y parts that ADI still lists in production (Phase 5 search). The leg already had -1% and +17% contribution. With revised returns, restoring it as in Phase 4 makes BASE worse (-$32.7k vs -$29.8k).

*Action taken:* DIP leg killed. Model: dip_units_steady_month 50->0, first_lot_dip 200->0, DIP design $1.5k removed from m2, DIP marketing $300->$150 (kept as pre-launch card marketing). Deep dive §0, §1, §2.1, §3 (#8 dropped), §4 and kill criterion 13: reopen only on a true-async core with >=10 hosts passing, including one with /CE tied low and one cycling at <=100 ns.

### RT-PHY-DD3-03 — MINOR — PHY — §1 DIP modules as working RAM / §4 2-year warranty

> Where DS1225/DS1230 parts serve as working RAM (stack and variables in arcade boards and instruments), F-RAM's destructive read consumes endurance on every access, row by row (FM18W08-class 1e14 read/write cycles). A hot row hit at ~1 M accesses/s reaches 1e14 in ~3.2 power-on years, and at 10 M/s in ~4 months. Long-held calibration constants at cabinet temperature also face ferroelectric imprint. A blanket warranty without screening the host's duty cycle is exposed.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Per-host access-rate measurement (logic analyser on /CE) showing hot-row rates ≤1e5/s, or warranty terms excluding working-RAM use.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct arithmetic: 1e14 / 1e6 accesses per second = 1e8 s, about 3.2 power-on years, and about 4 months at 10M/s. F-RAM restores after every read, so a stack row in an arcade or instrument board wears out, and a blanket 2-year warranty is exposed. On its own this is fixable with a duty-cycle screen or a working-RAM exclusion. It is moot for BASE because the F-RAM DIP leg is killed under RT-VET-DD3-03.

*Action taken:* No model change beyond the DIP kill. Kill criterion 13 adds a revival condition: any revived DIP leg must use a non-wearing core (Toggle MRAM, or nvSRAM whose STORE runs only at power-down) and must exclude or screen working-RAM use.

### RT-PHY-DD3-04 — MINOR — PHY — §1 card block diagram ('3x 74LVC16245-class 5V-tolerant buffers 5V <-> 3.3V') / INPUTS: card_logic_2mb_usd

> A single-supply 74LVC16245 at 3.3 V accepts 5 V inputs but drives only about 3 V onto the host data bus. That is fine for TTL-threshold hosts and out of spec for any 5 V CMOS-input host logic (VIH = 3.5 V). Dual-supply translators (74LVC16T245/74ALVC164245) with direction control from OE#/WE# are the correct parts. More importantly, PC Card 8-bit mode (CE1# low, CE2# high, A0 selecting the odd byte onto D0-D7) needs byte-lane steering from the x16 MR4A16B, and a '245 bank cannot do that without decode logic. The 'optional ≤$5 CPLD' is likely mandatory, and the $5 logic line understates it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A schematic with dual-supply translators and steering logic that passes 8-bit and 16-bit access tests on the bench control, with logic COGS re-quoted.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct on both points. First, a single-supply 74LVC16245 at 3.3 V gives VOH of about 3 V. The PC Card 5V signalling spec is TTL-level [UNVERIFIED], so many hosts would accept that, but the Fanuc host logic family is unknown and a 5V-CMOS VIH of 3.5 V would fail. Dual-supply 74LVC16T245-class parts powered from host VCC and the 3.3 V rail are the safe choice. Second, PC Card 8-bit mode (CE1# low, CE2# high, A0 selecting the odd byte onto D0-D7) needs byte-lane steering from the x16 MR4A16B. A '245 bank alone cannot do it, so the CPLD is mandatory, not optional. Re-costed logic: $5->$9 (2MB) and $6->$10 (4MB), plus a $1.5k rev-B respin. Combined effect about -$2.7k of 36-month net.

*Action taken:* Model: card_logic_2mb_usd $5->$9, card_logic_4mb_usd $6->$10; one_time +$1.5k rev-B respin at m8. Deep dive §1 block diagram and cost bullet updated. §10 step 2 adds 8-bit and 16-bit access tests on the bench control.

### RT-PHY-DD3-05 — MINOR — PHY — §10 track B tests (100 power cycles) / §11 kill criterion 7

> Technicians hot-remove PC Cards. Mid-write removal, when WE#/CE# glitch while VCC collapses through the regulator, is the real field abuse case, and the plan tests only 100 control power cycles. Everspin's datasheet requires E and W held high during power-up and power-down, so the supervisor must gate those lines on the MRAM side, with pull-ups to MRAM VDD and enough hold-up to finish an in-flight cycle. Hot removal is untested and unspecified.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Zero corruption outside the addressed word across ≥500 hot removals during active writes, with CRC checks of all non-addressed blocks.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. Technicians hot-remove cards, and Everspin requires E and W held high through power-up and power-down. The design now puts a supervisor on host VCC (trip at about 4.5 V). The CPLD forces E and W inactive, pull-ups go to MRAM VDD, and a hold-up capacitor on the 3.3 V rail lets an in-flight 35 ns cycle finish. The energy is trivial; the gating is what matters. Hot removal is now a test with a pass/fail line. The cost sits inside the $9 logic line.

*Action taken:* Model: included in card_logic_2mb_usd/4mb ($5->$9/$10). §10 step 2 and step 4: >=500 hot removals during active writes, with CRC of every non-addressed block. Kill criterion 4: corruption outside the addressed word that a rev-C does not fix -> kill.

### RT-PHY-DD3-06 — MINOR — PHY — §10 track B sample size / §2.6 must-be-true #4

> Three to five hand-built cards on one control instance cannot predict field compatibility. Zero failures in 100 cycles bounds the per-cycle failure rate only at ≤3% (95%, rule of three). One 16i/18i control does not represent the 0/15/16/18/20/21/i-A/0i family span or its firmware revisions, and testing is at room temperature while control cabinets run 40-55°C. The >10% compatibility-return kill (§11 #7) is thus discovered on customers, not on the bench.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* ≥10 cards across ≥3 control families or firmware revisions, ≥1,000 cycles at 0-55°C, before the first 100-card lot.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Correct. Zero failures in 100 cycles bounds the per-cycle failure rate at <=3% (95%, rule of three), and one control instance at room temperature says nothing about the family and firmware span. As planned, the >10% returns kill would be discovered on customers. The plan now qualifies before the first lot: >=10 rev-B cards on >=3 families or firmware revisions, and >=1,000 power cycles at 0-55 C. That costs $3k of extra bench time and, with VET-05, moves launch from m9 to m11. Combined effect about -$4.6k of 36-month net.

*Action taken:* Model: one_time +$3k at m6 for bench time on 2 more families; card_launch_month 9->11 (shared with VET-05). §10 step 4 qualification gate; kill criterion 7: >3% compatibility failure on the qualification bench -> no first lot.

### RT-PHY-DD3-07 — MINOR — PHY — §1 and §2.7 C-16 mechanism ('MRAM journal replaces the DRAM cache and its hold-up supercapacitors')

> Replacing the DRAM cache with MRAM removes the DRAM flush but not the hold-up energy needed to finish or safely abort in-flight NAND program and erase operations. In MLC, TLC and QLC, an interrupted upper-page program can corrupt previously written lower-page data (the paired-page problem), so either hold-up capacitance or a paired-page-aware FTL remains. The $12 supercap saving in INPUTS plp_supercap_bom_usd is therefore not fully realisable. The 1Gb pMTJ STT part's retention-at-temperature line should also be read before any claim (and that part is the ITC 337-TA-1487 target).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A controller vendor confirming that an MRAM-journal TLC design with no hold-up capacitors passes power-loss testing (JESD218-class) without data loss.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and fatal to C-16 as a kit. Replacing the DRAM cache with an MRAM journal removes the DRAM flush, but not the energy needed to finish or safely abort an in-flight MLC/TLC/QLC program. An interrupted upper-page program can corrupt paired lower-page data. Either hold-up capacitance stays, or the FTL becomes paired-page-aware. IBM's FlashCore can drop capacitors because IBM owns its FTL, which is consistent with the objection. A kit vendor that owns no FTL cannot capture the 'supercap-killer' value. With only about $4 of the $12 capacitor BOM removable, option EV is -$44.9k (BASE) to -$49.0k (SEVERE). The 1Gb STT part is also the ITC 337-TA-1487 target.

*Action taken:* C-16 killed. Model: plp_supercap_bom_usd $12->$4, and §[7] prints 'C-16 KILLED'. Deep dive §0, §1, §2.7 and kill criterion 13. The $0 email interest test is dropped from §10.

### RT-VC-DD3-06 — MINOR — VC — §9 team / INPUTS warranty_returns_pct=0.03

> A 3% return rate is optimistic for a card sold into 1990s controls with dozens of firmware and option combinations, when the kill test validates one or two families. Each 'won't format on my 18-B' ticket needs Fanuc service knowledge the founder lacks, and the contract EE is paid per design, not kept on call.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Returns under 5% on the first 100 cards (already kill criterion 7), and a paid support arrangement with a CNC service technician signed before launch.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The card goes into 1990s controls with many firmware and option combinations, and the founder has no Fanuc service knowledge. 3% returns with no support channel was optimistic. Returns are raised to 6%, and a CNC field-service technician is retained at $150/month from launch. Combined effect -$6.0k of 36-month net (returns -$2.1k, retainer -$3.9k).

*Action taken:* Model: warranty_returns_pct 0.03->0.06; new opex_support_retainer $150/month from card launch. §9 adds the support role. Kill criterion 10 tightened to >5% compatibility returns on the first 100 cards -> stop sales, and >10% -> kill (was 10%/20%).

### RT-VET-DD3-06 — MINOR — VET — §3 customer #7 (MELSEC-Q) / INPUTS other_families_mult 1.5

> Mitsubishi's bulletin FA-A-0184 discontinued the Q2MEM-8MBA ATA card and points High-speed Universal QCPU users to battery-free SD cards. The SRAM card's unique remaining role is runtime file registers and sampling trace on older QnH/QnUD CPUs. Mitsubishi's share of the 1.5x 'other families' multiplier is smaller than §3 implies.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* 5 or more Mitsubishi deposits in track A from owners of QnH/QnUD (non-V) CPUs who use SRAM-card file registers.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. Mitsubishi FA-A-0184 discontinued the Q2MEM-8MBA ATA card and pointed High-speed Universal QCPU users to SD cards. The SRAM card's remaining role is file registers and sampling trace on older QnH/QnUD CPUs. Mitsubishi's share of 'other families' is smaller than §3 implied. The other-families multiplier drops from 1.5 to 1.35, and Mitsubishi is demoted from co-primary validation target to third.

*Action taken:* Model: other_families_mult 1.5->1.35. §3 customer #7 narrowed to QnH/QnUD file-register users; §10 benches Mitsubishi QnH third and tracks Mitsubishi deposits separately; kill criterion 2 no longer lets Mitsubishi alone carry the card line.

## DD4 — Memory Efficiency for On-Prem & Self-Hosted Fleets (memory-efficiency)

**Final status after red team: KILLED.** After the accepted objections, the value-supported C-74 fee falls 77%, from $7.36 to $1.66 per node per month. The causes compound: Proxmox already runs KSM, fleets hold less DDR5, the saving is a rental equivalent rather than a purchase avoided, and the vendor's share of a counterfactual is smaller. Meanwhile credible 24/7 on-call plus E&O costs ~$2,900-3,400 a month. The re-run BASE never breaks even, burns through the $40k by M12 (2027-10), and ends at -$95.7k. Even a fully passed kill test ends at -$85.4k, so the ~$6k experiment is cancelled and C-74 is killed at $0. C-75 was already dropped, and C-73 is ~2 consulting audits a year, not a venture.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: Yes technically, but smaller and lumpier than claimed. Incremental reclaim over stock Proxmox (KSM already on) is ~5-7%, and ~0 on hugepage-backed or SEV-SNP/TDX hosts. On Kubernetes and HPC, schedulers allocate by request, so reclaim creates no usable capacity. · Profitable: No. The fee is $1.66/node/month at peak against $2,500-3,000/month on-call plus $400 E&O.
