### RT-PHY-DD5-06 — MINOR — PHY — §1 and §2.1 'banked 32-64 KB macro, 60% array efficiency, 316.5 kbit/mm²' (model §1)

> The 60% array efficiency has no OpenRAM evidence behind it. The free macros reach 43-57.6 kbit/mm², about 8-11% of the 527.4 kbit/mm² cell limit. The sky130 foundry array also needs even row and column counts (OpenRAM issue #137) plus end, strap and dummy cells, all of which cut efficiency. Density is the pitch to FABulous-class buyers (§3 #5), so a 5.5× claim that turns into 2× changes the sale against DFFRAM.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The area of an OpenRAM-generated, DRC/LVS-clean 32 KB sky130 macro from the day 1-7 run, replacing the 60% assumption.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. The free OpenRAM macros sit at 8-11% array efficiency. Going from the 1 KB to the 2 KB OpenRAM sky130 macro adds 11.5 um2 per added bit, 6.0x the bitcell (model section 1), so the periphery does not amortise like a commercial compiler's. The even row/column rule and the strap and dummy cells cut efficiency further. The assumption is cut from 60% to 30% until measured, so the claim falls from 316.5 to 158.2 kbit/mm2 (5.5x to 2.7x the 2 KB macro). The day 1-20 run must report the real area of a DRC/LVS-clean 32 KB macro. If it is under 2x the 2 KB density (~115 kbit/mm2), the density pitch to FABulous-class buyers is dropped.

*Action taken:* Model R14 (LARGE_MACRO_ARRAY_EFF 0.60 -> 0.30; context only, no revenue effect). Density claim revised in sections 1 and 2.1. Section 10 step 1 measures the 32 KB macro area.

### RT-PHY-DD5-07 — MINOR — PHY — §3 #7-8 (Sandia, DoD SBIR performers) / §10 PASS (ii) 'serious buyer'

> The DoD and SBIR buyers who would meet the $10k 'serious buyer' bar usually need single-event-effects data (heavy-ion SEU cross-section, latch-up LET threshold) and TID data, and Mobile Semiconductor's RH90 compilers already serve that segment. Bulk sky130 latch-up behaviour is uncharacterized in the plan. A heavy-ion campaign (TAMU or LBNL beam time plus boards and travel) runs ~$10-30k [ESTIMATE] and is absent from the model. Gain cells, with fF storage nodes, would have a far worse soft-error rate than 6T.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two of the targeted SBIR performers confirm they would buy macros without radiation data, or a funded SEE campaign is added to the model.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Accepted. A heavy-ion SEE campaign (~$10-30k) would use a quarter to three-quarters of the founder's capital, and Mobile Semiconductor's RH90 compilers already serve the rad-hard segment. Scope is narrowed instead: no radiation data and no rad claims. The DoD/SBIR licence channel is halved from 0.08 to 0.04 a month. Each SBIR performer contacted in the kill test is asked whether it needs radiation data, and (ii) counts only buyers who accept macros without it. Gain cells, the worst soft-error case, are killed anyway.

*Action taken:* Model R7 (LIC_DOD_MONTH 0.08 -> 0.04). Section 3 #7-8 annotated. Section 4 licence row says 'no radiation data'. Section 10 step 3 and pass rule (ii) updated.

> Open sky130 MPW turns from the Efabless era took far longer than 6 months from tapeout to packaged parts on a bench, and ChipFoundry runs only at 50% fill or better (eeNews, cited in DD). ## DD6 — Private MoE Inference Appliance (DRAM instead of HBM) (private-moe-appliance)

**Final status after red team: KILLED.** The FATAL objection holds. Four concurrent users touch 3.88x one user's expert bytes, so the 1-socket L box serves 1-2 users at >=10 tok/s (6.7-13.3 named), not 4 (27). At authorized-channel RDIMM prices the L box quotes $78,708. A $44.6k two-node Mac Studio cluster matches its single-stream speed with a ~10x faster first token, and U.S.-origin 1T models make it a one-user box for defense. So the L SKU, which carried the Phase 4 profit, leaves BASE. Without it, even on every other Phase 4 assumption, m36 ARR is $109,172 and the founder is never full-time. The accepted commercial corrections are a 6% close rate, a 7-month cycle, VAR paper, channel terms of 15%/20%, 30% churn, a $500 retainer, $900 insurance and $42.5/GB DRAM with a demand response to price. With them, BASE ARR is $15,814 at m18 and $63,873 at m36, the 36-month net is -$29,411 with a $25k external draw, and the founder never goes full-time. GLUT ($66,969) and SEVERE ($68,675) are no better. Even a passed kill test (8% close, 6-month cycle, channel at 3 a month) gives $86,763 of ARR and +$6,446 of net, with no full-time switch. Full-time by m36 needs a 21% close rate. The concept fails the brief's 36-month ceiling for a PRIMARY and pays the founder nothing.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: Yes for one user; no for a team. The software is TRL 8-9. The bandwidth model (576 GB/s platform peak, 50% sustained, routed bytes only) gives the L box (Kimi-K2.5 INT4) 16.3 tok/s single-stream and 9.8 / 7.1 / 5.5 tok/s per user at 2 / 3 / 4 concurrent. That is 1-2 users at >=10 tok/s and 6.7-13.3 named users, with ~22 s to the first token at 8k and ~178 s at 64k. U.S.-origin 1T models (Inkling, Nemotron 3 Ultra) run at 11-14 tok/s on L, so one user. The S box (MiniMax-M2.x FP8, RTX PRO 5000 48GB) gives 23.6 tok/s single, 3 users and ~20 named users. None of this has been measured on the exact box. · Profitable: No. BASE ARR is $15,814 at m18 and $63,873 at m36. The founder never goes full-time and salary-inclusive breakeven never comes; breakeven as run is m28, with the founder unpaid. The 36-month net is -$29,411, BASE draws $25k of external capital, and founder economic profit is -$228,311. GLUT gives $66,969 and SEVERE $68,675. A passed kill test gives $86,763 and +$6,446. Full-time by m36 needs a 21% close rate; $1M ARR is unreachable. · Doable by this founder: Build: yes; Linux, inference engines and RAG suit this founder. Sell and support part-time: no. Regulated buyers need supplier onboarding that a part-time single-member LLC struggles to pass, and the cycle runs ~7 months. Delivery and support hours cut selling to ~4.8 conversations a month by m36, and a business-hours SLA needs a paid U.S.-person backup technician.

### RT-VET-DD6-01 — FATAL — VET — §0 POSSIBLE (4 concurrent users, ~27 named users) / §1 'The CPU reads only the picked experts' / §2.3 $116 per user / INPUTS L_single_tps, conc_exponent, conc_saturation / §10 K4

> The L box's multi-user capacity breaks the memory-bandwidth arithmetic that §1 itself sets up. Kimi-K2.5 routes 8 of 384 experts per layer. Four independent users touch on average 384 x [1 - (376/384)^4] = 31.0 distinct experts per layer, 3.88x one token's 8. With the doc's own ~16 GB read per token, one batch-4 decode step therefore reads ~62 GB. Four users at 10 tok/s each means 10 steps/s, or ~620 GB/s. That is above the 614 GB/s theoretical peak of 12 x DDR5-6400, and AMD's 9005 architecture guide (58462) lists 6000 MT/s as the platform maximum (576 GB/s), with 6400 validated only on some customer boards. Using the more accurate routed-only bytes (~21B routed params x ~0.53 B = ~11.3 GB; attention and shared expert sit on the GPU), a step still reads ~44 GB, so 10 tok/s per user needs ~440 GB/s: ~71% of theoretical, or STREAM-class efficiency, against the 45% the doc assumes for one stream. conc_saturation = 2.4 comes from KTransformers balance-serve (17 -> 40 tok/s), measured on a Xeon 6 with 12-channel MRDIMM-8800. That is ~845 GB/s per socket, at least 1.38x the L box, and the doc says that run was GPU-bound; the L box would be DRAM-bound. L_single_tps also assumes a x2.0 gain from bandwidth, yet §1 reports that doubling bandwidth with a second socket gave only 1.29x (8.73 -> 11.26 tok/s). I re-ran capacity() with concurrency scaled by bandwidth (saturation 2.4 x 614/845 = 1.74). L max concurrency at >=10 tok/s falls from 4 to 2, named users from 27 to 13, and cost from $116 to $232 per user per month. Also scaling single-stream (17 -> 12.4 tok/s) gives 1 user at a time, 7 named users and $465. Both are worse than CoCounsel ($104) and Mac Studio ($124) in §2.3. The error never reaches §2.4, because simulate() never reads capacity(): ARR stays at $253,693 whatever the box can serve. K4's pass line (>=8 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent) needs ~350 GB/s (57% of peak) on routed bytes alone, so kill criterion 5 (drop L) is the likely outcome. That leaves the S box against the $22k Mac Studio and the $7k DGX Spark.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Track B measurement on the exact BOM configuration (one EPYC 9005 socket, 12 x 64 GB at the stated speed, RTX PRO 6000; CCD count and NPS mode logged) showing >=10 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent on Kimi-K2.5 INT4 with 8k-token contexts. Alternatively, a published KTransformers single-socket, 12-channel DDR5 concurrency curve showing >=2.3x aggregate at 4 users. And simulate() made to cap subscription seats and orders by measured capacity.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid, and my own re-derivation confirms it. Four independent users touch 384 x [1-(376/384)^4] = 31.0 distinct experts per layer, 3.88x one token's 8. On the routed-only bytes (~11.9 GB/token: 21.1B routed params at ~4.5 bits), 4 users at 10 tok/s need 10 steps/s x 3.88 x 11.9 GB = ~461 GB/s, which is 80% of the 576 GB/s DDR5-6000 platform maximum. That is STREAM-class efficiency, not what an engine that syncs a GPU every layer sustains. The conc_saturation 2.4 was measured on an ~845 GB/s Xeon 6 MRDIMM socket. I replaced the fitted concurrency curve with a bandwidth model: step = t_fix (20 ms) + distinct-expert multiple x routed GB / (576 GB/s x 0.50). It gives 16.3 tok/s single-stream (so the single-stream claim survives) and 9.8 / 7.1 / 5.5 tok/s per user at 2 / 3 / 4 concurrent. That is 1 user at >=10 tok/s and 6.7 named users. At 0.55 efficiency or on a DDR5-6400-validated board it is 2 users and 13.3 named. Cost per named user rises from $116 to $222-395 a month, worse than CoCounsel ($104) and Mac Studio ($124). At 4 concurrent the box sits at or below K4's 6 tok/s drop-L line. A 2-socket L would restore only 3 users (20 named) at a ~$87k quote. I also accept that the error never reached the P&L. simulate() now reads capacity through a $50-per-named-user cap on the subscription, which takes the L subscription from $1,250 to $333. One partial rebuttal: the 8.73 -> 11.26 2S figure the reviewer cites came from weight duplication, not a bandwidth limit (see RT-PHY-DD6-02). It does not change the conclusion.

*Action taken:* Model: capacity_model phase4 -> bandwidth (new bw_peak_gbs 576, bw_eff 0.50, routed GB, experts, t_fix, VRAM/KV term); sub_user_cap off -> $50/named user; mix_L 0.50 -> 0.00 in BASE (L is not quoted). Deep dive section 0, section 2.3 and section 1 corrected. K4-L re-admission bar: >=10 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent on a socket-pinned exact BOM. Kill criterion 5 is fired by default. Leg killed: the L 'Team-1T' box for a 10-40-person team.

### RT-HYP-DD6-01 — MAJOR — HYP — §3 buyer / §4 channels and sales motion / §7 / INPUTS conversion 0.08, channel_start_month 13, insurance_month $500 / §10 K1

> Regulated buyers do not issue a $31–63k PO to a new single-member LLC run part-time. My supplier-qualification program, and every regulated enterprise I sell GovCloud to, gates a new hardware vendor on five things: a security questionnaire (SIG/CAIQ), SOC 2 Type II or equivalent, a certificate of insurance at $1–5M cyber/E&O, financials, and a key-person/business-continuity plan. The model's $500/month insurance will not buy those limits (cf. RT-HYP-DD1-08). Hardware also normally moves on an existing vehicle: CDW-G, SHI, Carahsoft, GSA MAS, NASPO ValuePoint, E&I, or the university's own contract. The university targets (#3 VT ARC, #4 UD) buy on federal awards, so 2 CFR 200.320 requires competition above the micro-purchase threshold. That means a Dell/HPE/Supermicro quote for the same 768 GB/1-GPU configuration on their existing contract, and the open-source stack costs them nothing. DD6 books founder-direct closes from m3 at 8% and starts the channel only at m13. Model probe (run_with): if direct deals cannot close and only the MSP/RPO channel converts (conv_target_pt = conv_target_ft = 0), the first order slips to m17. m36 ARR is $53,844, 36-month net is −$54,855, and the founder is never full-time. Even with the channel signed from m4, m36 ARR is $75,202 and the founder is never full-time. K1 counts LOIs, which cost a buyer nothing and do not survive onboarding.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* In Track A, ≥2 buyers send a supplier-onboarding packet or security questionnaire and confirm in writing that they can PO a new single-member LLC directly. Alternatively, a DIB MSP/VAR (CyberSheath, Corsica, a CDW-class reseller) agrees in writing to carry the box on its paper from m3, with its 10% modelled from m1, and BASE still reaches full-time by m30. Either way, replace K1's '≥3 LOIs' with '≥1 supplier onboarding started, or a paid pilot'.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. A single-member LLC run part-time faces SIG/CAIQ questionnaires, COI limits, financials and key-person questions, and most hardware moves on an existing vehicle (CDW-G, SHI, Carahsoft, GSA, NASPO). Universities on federal awards must compete the buy under 2 CFR 200.320, against a Dell/HPE quote for the same configuration running the free open-source stack. I did not take the reviewer's extreme (zero direct closes); I took a middle path. The founder-direct close rate goes from 8% to 6%. Half of founder-sourced orders go on a VAR/MSP's paper at a 5% fee. Insurance rises from $500 to $900 a month for $1-2M cyber/E&O + GL + product limits. Universities are demoted to benchmark partners. Revised BASE: first order m10, m36 ARR $63,873, never full-time. With no channel at all it is $52,843 and net -$42,858. I did not model SOC 2 Type II (~$20-30k); a buyer that demands it is unreachable at this scale.

*Action taken:* Model: conversion 0.08 -> 0.06; new paper_share 0 -> 0.5 and paper_fee 0 -> 0.05; insurance_month $500 -> $900. K1 now requires cash plus a supplier-onboarding packet started (section 10). Kill criterion 3 treats universities as benchmark partners.

### RT-HYP-DD6-02 — MAJOR — HYP — §2.6 / §4 sales motion / INPUTS sales_cycle_m 4 / §8 gates / §11 kill criterion 8

> The model's 4-month lag from qualified conversation to order (sales_cycle_m) is shorter than the motion §4 itself prescribes. That motion runs discovery → a 60-day paid pilot → a 3–6-month security review (the C-48 verify_corrections note cited in the INPUT) → PO and deposit, all before vendor onboarding. On DD6's own numbers that is 5–8+ months. Re-running BASE with longer lags:
> - 6 months: first order m9, cash low −$166, m36 ARR $206,138, full-time m25.
> - 7 months: first order m10, cash low −$3,316 (the model draws a $25,000 tranche), ARR $182,462, full-time m26.
> - 9 months: first order m12, ARR $145,458, full-time m28.
> - 7 months plus conversion −30%: never full-time.
> Kill criterion 8 (no deposit by 2027-07-31, m9) is calibrated on the 4-month lag. A healthy funnel with a normal regulated-buyer cycle trips it, or the founder waives it.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A evidence from ≥3 regulated buyers that they bought comparable on-prem IT from a new vendor within 4 months, including security review and onboarding. Without that, re-run BASE with sales_cycle_m = 7, re-date the §8 gates, move kill criterion 8 to m12, and budget the bridge before m10.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid: section 4's own motion (discovery, 60-day paid pilot, 3-6-month security review, PO) is 5-8+ months, not 4. With sales_cycle_m = 7, the first order moves from m7 to m10 (2027-08), and expected-value cash falls to $3,450 at m9, which draws a $25k tranche. A 9-month cycle gives ARR $62,686 and net -$46,954. Kill criterion 8 (deposit by m9) was calibrated on the 4-month lag and would have fired on a healthy funnel. It now sits at m12.

*Action taken:* Model: sales_cycle_m 4 -> 7. Kill criterion 8 moved from m9 to m12 after reopening; criteria 9 and 11 re-based on the revised path. The bridge is treated as required, not optional (section 0).

### RT-HYP-DD6-03 — MAJOR — HYP — §5 competitors (Mac Studio row) / §2.3 cost per user / §11 kill criterion 14 / INPUTS mac512_users

> DD6 treats Apple only as one 512 GB box (5 users, 1T only at ~3-bit), and kill criterion 14 watches for 'a desktop at $25k or less'. The relevant substitute already ships and has been measured. Since macOS 26.2 (Dec 2025), Mac Studios cluster over RDMA-over-Thunderbolt-5 with exo. Four M3 Ultra nodes ran Kimi K2 Thinking (1T) at 28.3 tok/s (Jeff Geerling, Dec 2025). Creative Strategies reported ~25 tok/s, ~2 s time to first token and <450 W on a ~$40k four-node cluster. Those are measurements, and they beat the L box's estimated 17 tok/s and its estimated 19–22 s TTFT by about 10×. At DD6's own $22,299 price for the M5 Ultra 512 GB, two nodes give 1 TB of unified memory for ~$44.6k, below the $62,525 L box. KC14 can never fire on a cluster, so nothing monitors the L SKU's reason to exist. In DD6's favour: there is no ECC or BMC, and macOS as a server is awkward inside a CMMC boundary. That protects the DIB segment but not the law-firm and IP-boutique segments (#7, #8).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K4 run head-to-head: the L box matches or beats a 2-node M5 Ultra cluster on TTFT(8k) and on per-user tok/s at 4 concurrent, with the same model and quant. Or ≥60% of Track A legal/IP interviewees reject a Mac cluster for a stated reason (ECC, manageability, support). Either way, rewrite KC14 to 'any configuration at or below the L box price'.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid and verified by search. macOS 26.2 RDMA over Thunderbolt 5 with exo ran Kimi K2 Thinking at 28.3 tok/s on four M3 Ultras (Geerling), ~25 tok/s with ~2 s TTFT on a ~$40k cluster (Creative Strategies), and ~15 tok/s on two M3 Ultras (Awni Hannun, per Hardware Corner). Two M5 Ultra 512GB give 1 TB of unified memory for $44,598. That is 57% of the revised $78,708 L box. It roughly matches the L box's 16-17 tok/s single-stream with ~10x faster first token at under a third of the power. Multi-user behaviour on exo is unmeasured, but the L box is a 1-2-user box anyway (RT-VET-DD6-01). The L box's remaining edge (ECC, BMC, a Linux/FIPS image inside a CMMC boundary) protects only the DIB segment. For law firms and IP boutiques (#7, #8) the L box has no reason to exist, and the reviewer's condition (beat the cluster on TTFT(8k)) is physically out of reach: PCIe weight streaming sets a ~22 s floor.

*Action taken:* Model section 4 adds a 2x M5 Ultra cluster row ($185/named user). Deep dive section 5 adds a Mac-cluster row. Kill criterion 14 is rewritten to 'any configuration at or below the L price' and fires today. K4-M head-to-head is required before L is re-admitted. Leg killed: the L SKU for non-DIB buyers.

### RT-HYP-DD6-04 — MAJOR — HYP — §2 performance table / §2.3 / §5 win condition 3 / §10 K5 / INPUTS min_user_tps 10, peak_active_share 0.15

> I ran the model's own capacity() and per_user_tps() at the U.S.-origin speeds DD6 lists for the L box: Inkling 975B at ~13 tok/s and Nemotron 3 Ultra at ~10 tok/s single-stream. Per-user speed at 2 concurrent is 9.99 and 7.68 tok/s, both below the model's own 10 tok/s floor. Maximum concurrency is therefore 1, and named users fall to 6.7. L-box cost per named user becomes $465/month, against the $116 headline. That is 4.5× CoCounsel's entry seat ($104) and ~13× Copilot GCC High (~$35). The defense/ITAR segment is exactly the one that needs U.S.-origin weights; §5 cites the No Adversarial AI Act and agency DeepSeek bans. Yet K5 passes at '≥8 tok/s single', which in the model is a one-user box. KTransformers documents neither model (§5), so even 10–13 tok/s is unmeasured.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A U.S.-origin 500B+ MoE measured on the L box at ≥10 tok/s per user with 2+ concurrent (≈≥17 tok/s single). Or raise K5 to that threshold, and re-price and re-model the defense L SKU on ~7 named users.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid, and worse under the bandwidth model. Scaling routed bytes by active parameters, Inkling 975B-A41B runs at 13.7 tok/s single and 8.0 per user at 2. Nemotron 3 Ultra 550B-A55B runs at 11.0 single and 6.2 at 2. Both are 1 user at >=10 tok/s and 6.7 named users, ~$395 per named user per month against Copilot GCC High at ~$35 and CoCounsel at $104. The segment that needs U.S.-origin weights is the defense segment section 5 leads with, and KTransformers documents neither model. K5 passed at '>=8 tok/s single', which describes a one-user box. Defense can be served only on the S box with <=400B U.S.-origin models (gpt-oss-120b, Llama-class [UNVERIFIED-training for Maverick fit]). Those run on a single GPU workstation too, which removes the DRAM thesis for that segment.

*Action taken:* K5 raised to >=10 tok/s per user at 2 concurrent. Kill criterion 6 is fired for L. Defense moves to S with smaller U.S.-origin models. Model section 2 prints the U.S.-origin rows. Leg killed: the U.S.-origin 1T defense box.

### RT-HYP-DD6-05 — MAJOR — HYP — §1 'What it is bad at' / §2 capacity (TTFT columns) / §10 K4 / INPUTS L_prefill_2k 106, L_prefill_8k 368

> capacity() counts only decode concurrency, and its TTFT is for an idle box (8,192 / 368 tok/s = 22 s). On the L box, long-prompt prefill is a serialized resource: every RAG request streams expert weights over PCIe through the one GPU for ~19–22 s. With 4 users active, a new prompt waits for the pass in progress, so TTFT under load approaches 2× the floor (~44 s). That matches the vendor's own 41 s TTFT at ~23 concurrent on 8 GPUs (§1), and it breaches K4's own 40 s drop-L line. DD6's flagship use, 'draft this memo from these 40 documents', implies 50–150k-token prompts. K4 stops at 8k, so the use the pitch sells is never measured. The 27-named-user and $116/user figures hold only for light, short-prompt chat.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K4 extended and passed on real hardware: TTFT(8k) ≤40 s with 4 prompts arriving together, and a single 64k-token prompt answered in ≤90 s while 3 other streams hold ≥8 tok/s.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. capacity() counted only decode concurrency, and its TTFT was for an idle box. At the L box's 368 tok/s 8k prefill rate, a 32k prompt waits ~89 s and a 64k prompt ~178 s before the first token. The single GPU is busy for that whole time, so concurrent arrivals queue toward 2x. The flagship 'draft this memo from these 40 documents' use (50-150k tokens) is therefore not served by the L box at all, and the reviewer's 64k <=90 s bar cannot be met at that prefill rate. The S box is materially better: MiniMax-M2.1 prefilled 32k at 2,540 tok/s on the 2-socket benchmark (P2), ~13 s, and S prefill is GPU-bound so one socket should be similar [ESTIMATE]. The S named-user count is now computed at a 32k-token design context (20 users).

*Action taken:* Model: new design_ctx_tokens 32,000 with a KV-memory cap; section 2 prints 32k/64k TTFT. K4-S requires TTFT(32k) <=30 s with 2 other streams at >=8 tok/s; K4-L requires TTFT(8k) <=40 s with 4 arrivals and 64k <=90 s (expected to fail).

### RT-HYP-DD6-06 — MAJOR — HYP — §9 team (backup technician) / §2.2 subscription cost / INPUTS support_h_per_box_month 2.0, oncall_month 0, parts_reserve_pct_yr 0.03 / §2.5 24/7 row

> Buyers judge the subscription against Dell ProSupport and HPE Foundation Care, which give next-business-day or 4-hour onsite parts. A founder with a day job cannot even give business-hours response, and BASE uses the U.S.-person backup technician for only ~25 hours in 36 months. GPU field failures are not rare. Meta's Llama 3 report (arXiv 2407.21783, Table 5) logged 148 faulty-GPU and 72 HBM3 interruptions in 54 days on 16,384 H100s, about 10% a year under training load [UNVERIFIED-training on exact counts]. At a third of that rate, a 22-box fleet loses ~0.7 GPUs a year. Each is a $16k part that needs either a spare on the shelf or an RMA that takes weeks. The 3%/yr parts reserve ($1,463 per L box) cannot fund one spare RTX PRO 6000 until ~11 L boxes are under subscription. The model's own sensitivity puts full-time at m35 with a 24/7 retainer. Add sales_cycle_m = 7 and full-time slips to m36, with m36 ARR at $151,284.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A signed field-service agreement with an MSP or technician pricing NBD onsite coverage, plus written advance-replacement terms from PNY/NVIDIA or a funded spare pool. Both go into BASE, and full-time still lands at m30 or earlier.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Buyers will compare the subscription with Dell ProSupport and HPE Foundation Care, and a founder with a day job cannot give business-hours response. Note that the Llama 3 failure counts are under training load, and the reviewer flagged them [UNVERIFIED-training]. Inference duty cycles are lighter, so I use about a third of that rate (~3%/yr). Changes: a named U.S.-person backup technician on a $500/month business-hours retainer from first delivery. Next-business-day service covers DIMMs, NVMe and PSUs from the spare pool. A GPU failure is answered by installing a 48 GB loaner (RTX PRO 5000, bought at 5 boxes) within 2 business days. It runs either SKU degraded (Kimi-K2.5 needs >=48 GB of VRAM) while PNY handles the RMA. The parts reserve goes from 3% to 4%. The retainer alone costs ~$14.6k over 36 months: BASE net is -$29,411 with it and -$14,800 without. A 24/7 retainer gives -$70,990.

*Action taken:* Model: oncall_month $0 -> $500; parts_reserve_pct_yr 0.03 -> 0.04; new loaner_gpu_trigger 5. The SLA text now promises NBD on DIMM/NVMe/PSU and a 2-business-day loaner GPU, and is no longer equated with Dell ProSupport.

### RT-HYP-DD6-07 — MAJOR — HYP — §2.5 COGS row / §4 pricing ('quotes valid for 14 days') / §2.1 GPU prices

> DD6's answer to component risk is 14-day quotes, with parts bought in the order month. That clashes with how regulated buyers purchase. Public universities and most enterprise procurement want pricing held firm for 30–90 days, and they approve a specific quote. Here the PO comes after a 3–6-month security review, so the quote has expired and must be re-approved. Either the founder holds price through the review, or every re-quote restarts approval. The exposure is real. 4Q26 DRAM contract prices are still rising +12.5% QoQ (01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 4). DD6's own RTX 5090 evidence runs from $4,299 at Micro Center to $8.5–10k online. Model run: a 10% COGS drift after the quote moves full-time from m23 to m33 and cuts m36 ARR from $253,693 to $160,159. At 15%, full-time slips to m36.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two Track A buyers (one university, one DIB firm) accept in writing a price-adjustment clause indexed to a published DRAM/GPU reference. Or the distributor grants 60–90-day price protection on RDIMMs and GPUs.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Regulated buyers approve a specific quote, and the PO arrives after a 3-6-month review. A 14-day quote therefore either forces re-approval or makes the founder hold price through a rising DRAM market. Quotes are now valid 30 days with a price-adjustment clause applied at PO, indexed to a published DRAM contract index and the distributor GPU price. The integrator is modelled as absorbing a 3% expected drift. Buyers who refuse indexation (often universities) need a firm 60-90-day quote with a ~10% hold premium, which the new demand response charges for. A 30% COGS spike after quoting now gives BASE net -$95,730 and a second $25k draw.

*Action taken:* Model: new quote_drift 0 -> 0.03 on BOM at purchase. Section 4 pricing terms changed. K1b asks each buyer to accept the index clause; if >=50% refuse, price in a hold premium and re-run.

### RT-PHY-DD6-01 — MAJOR — PHY — §0 POSSIBLE (17 tok/s, 4 concurrent, ~27 users) / §1 'The CPU reads only the picked experts' / §2.3 L and S rows / INPUTS L_single_tps, S_single_tps / capacity()

> Both SKUs' user counts sit on rounding knife-edges, and the L input is scaled by parameters the CPU never reads. (1) Knife-edge. capacity() counts c users only if single x min(c^0.62, 2.4)/c >= 10 tok/s. For the L box that needs a single stream of >=16.93 tok/s for 4 users, >=15.18 for 3 and >=13.01 for 2 (my run of the model's own functions). L_single_tps = 17.0 clears 4 users by 0.4% (10.04 tok/s each). At 16.9 the L box serves 3 users, 20 named users, $155 per user per month. S_single_tps = 25.0 sits exactly on the c = 6 line (25 x 2.4/6 = 10.00). Its measured basis, 32.1/1.29 = 24.88, gives 5 users, 33 named users and $48 instead of 6, 40 and $40. (2) Wrong bytes. The input scales R1's 8.73 tok/s by '37/32 active params' (+16%). But the CPU holds only the routed experts, and those are almost the same size in both models. DeepSeek-R1 routes 8 experts of 3x7168x2048 = 44.0M params over 58 MoE layers: 20.4B params. Kimi-K2.x routes 8 over 60 MoE layers: 21.1B. Kimi's smaller active count comes from 64 attention heads instead of 128 and 1 dense layer instead of 3, all GPU-resident (public configs) [ESTIMATE: config arithmetic]. At ~4.5 bits (Kimi INT4, group 32) against ~4.5-4.8 bits (R1 Q4_K_M), the right factor is ~0.97-1.03, not 1.156. 8.73 x 2.0 x 1.03 x 0.85 = 15.3 tok/s, which gives 3 users and 20 named users. With the 6,000 MT/s platform limit in RT-VET-DD6-01 (x1.875) it is 14.3 tok/s: 2 users, 13 named users. (3) Wrong cross-check. The input says MiniMax-M2.1 reads '~0.8x Kimi bytes/token'. MiniMax-M2 routes 8 of 256 experts of 3x3072x1536 params over 62 layers: 7.0B params at FP8 = 7.0 GB. Kimi routes 21.1B params at 4.5 bits = 11.9 GB. The ratio is 0.59, not 0.8. So the cross-check gives 24.9 x 0.59 = 14.7 tok/s: 2 users, 13 named users, $232. No public single-socket EPYC 9005 measurement of any 1T MoE exists. The Level1Techs 'Kimi on Epyc' thread has only guesses of 10-20 tok/s (search 2026-11-01). Three independent corrections all land at 13-20 named users, not 27. The model never reads capacity() (RT-VET-DD6-01), so §2.4 does not move.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Track B single-socket run of Kimi-K2.5 INT4 on the exact BOM (EPYC 9455-class, 12 x 64 GB at the logged MT/s, RTX PRO 6000) measuring >=17.5 tok/s single-stream and >=10 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent with 8k contexts. Or L_single_tps re-derived on routed-expert bytes, the cross-check corrected to 0.59, and §2.3 re-run with capacity() at the corrected values.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid on all three counts. (1) Knife-edge: 17.0 tok/s cleared 4 users by 0.4%, and S at 25.0 sat exactly on the 6-user line. (2) The 37/32 active-parameter factor credited bytes the CPU never reads; routed bytes are ~21.1B (Kimi) vs ~20.4B (R1) params. (3) The MiniMax cross-check ratio is 7.0/11.9 = 0.59, not 0.8. The new bandwidth model works from routed bytes directly. It gives L 16.3 tok/s single (17.0-17.4 at 0.55 efficiency or a 6400 board) and S 23.6 (24.9 at 0.55). Knife-edge results are now printed as a bracket: L serves 1 user at 0.50 efficiency and 2 at 0.55 (6.7-13.3 named, not 27); S serves 3 users (20 named, $95 a user, not 40 and $40). Both are below the reviewer's 13-20 for L because concurrency now pays for distinct-expert bytes (RT-VET-DD6-01).

*Action taken:* Model: L_single_tps / S_single_tps superseded by bandwidth-computed values (new L_routed_gb 11.9, S_routed_gb 7.0, experts 384/256); section 2 prints efficiency variants. Deep dive section 2.3 rewritten.

### RT-PHY-DD6-02 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 'One socket, not two' / notes §2.2 ('Dual-socket is poor value') / INPUTS S_single_tps (÷1.29), L_single_tps cross-check / §10 Track B step 1 (rented host)

> The 1.29x dual-socket factor is stale, and the deep dive uses it in both directions. It comes from KTransformers v0.2 (Feb-2025), a build that copied every weight onto each NUMA node: 382 GB on one socket against 1 TB on two (notes P1). The engine the deep dive specifies, kt-kernel with SGLang, uses NUMA-aware tensor parallelism. That puts a slice of each expert in each node's local memory, with up to 63% more decode speed on dual-socket systems (LMSYS, 2025-10-22, snippet). (a) Rejecting 2S. With slicing, a 2S board with 24 x 32 GB still holds 768 GB and doubles peak bandwidth. It adds a second $5.5k CPU and ~$1k of board, so ~$8.3k (+13%) on the L box price at 22% GM. At a 1.29-1.63x decode gain, the model's own capacity() gives 5-6 users at >=10 tok/s and 33-40 named users. The 'one socket' design is not supported by current evidence, and the obvious fix for the bandwidth shortfall (RT-VET-DD6-01) is ruled out on a 2025 number. (b) Deriving 1S from the 2S benchmark. P2 (2x EPYC 9355 + RTX 5090, MiniMax-M2.1, 32.1 tok/s) ran on the current NUMA-aware engine. Dividing by 1.29 gives 24.9 tok/s. Dividing by 1.63 gives 19.7 tok/s: 4 users, 27 named users and $60 per user per month for the S box, instead of 6, 40 and $40. The Kimi cross-check then falls to 11.6 tok/s: 1 user, 6.7 named users, $465. (c) Track B. Most rentable 768 GB+ bare-metal hosts are dual-socket [ESTIMATE]. Unless the run is pinned to one socket and its local memory (numactl --cpunodebind=0 --membind=0, with NPS mode and CCD count logged), K4 measures a 2S machine and flatters the 1S SKU by up to 63%.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A current kt-kernel/SGLang run measuring the 1S-to-2S decode gain on the same host (one socket pinned, then both), at or below 1.35x, which would justify the 1S design and the ÷1.29 conversion. Or the L SKU re-specified as 2S with 24 x 32 GB, S_single_tps re-derived with the measured factor, and Track B written to require socket pinning.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The LMSYS post (2025-10-22, snippet) says NUMA-aware tensor parallelism gives up to 63% more decode on dual-socket systems; its baseline is not stated in the snippet. The 1.29x came from a build that duplicated weights on each socket. (a) I modelled a 2S L box (24 x 32 GB, 1,152 GB/s at 0.45 efficiency, 22 ms fixed time). It gives 22.2 tok/s single and 3 users at >=10 tok/s (20 named). That still falls short of 27, and at ~$87,041 it is DGX Station GB300 money ($87-95k) with far worse TTFT. So 2S is rejected on price, not on the 1.29 figure. (b) The S box's 1S speed is now calibrated from P2 through the bandwidth model: 23.6 tok/s, equivalent to dividing by ~1.36. On the reviewer's 1.63 reading it would be 19.7 tok/s and 2 users (13 named). The bracket is stated. (c) Accepted: rented 768 GB hosts are usually 2S, so Track B must pin one socket.

*Action taken:* Model section 2 prints the 2S L variant and section 1 its quote. Deep dive section 1 annotated. K4-S and K4-L require numactl --cpunodebind=0 --membind=0 with NPS mode and CCD count logged, and a 1S-vs-2S run on the same host.

### RT-VC-DD6-01 — MAJOR — VC — §2.5 'price +30%' and 'COGS' rows / §12 / model simulate(): elast = 1 + glut_elasticity x max(0, 1 - box_price_now/base_box_price)

> The model has no demand response to a price rise. Orders respond to the box price only when it falls (the max(0, .) term), so a component spike passed through cost-plus before quoting makes the business better, not worse. My re-run with DRAM x1.25 and GPUs at $20,000 (L) / $6,250 (S), same 22% GM target, gives m36 ARR $275,105 and full-time at m21, against BASE $253,693 and m23. The 'price +30%' row ($382,745 ARR, full-time m19) likewise assumes the same 8% close rate on an ~$81k L box; with an elasticity near -1 (price +30%, conversion -30%) the run gives $245,881 and m22, so the upside disappears. This is not academic: 01_LANDSCAPE §2.3 has contract DRAM still rising (+10-15% in 4Q26) with the peak called for 4Q26-2Q27, so the first quotes (m7 = 2027-05) land above the Nov-2026 deck the model prices from. §2.5's 'the COGS row is the GPU shortage in numbers' covers only a spike after the quote, which the 14-day quote validity already handles; the likelier pre-quote spike is scored as upside.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A re-run with a symmetric price elasticity (-0.5 to -1.0 on box price versus the Nov-2026 quote) that still reaches full-time by m30, or K1 evidence that the same prospects sign at the card price +20%.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid; this was a model bug. elast = 1 + e x max(0, 1 - p/p0) responded only to falling prices, so a pre-quote component spike passed through cost-plus raised profit. The model now measures every box price against the fixed Phase-4 price card ($62,525 L / $30,622 S), the price the close rate was assumed at. Orders fall 0.5% per 1% above it. I chose 0.5, the low end of the reviewer's -0.5 to -1.0, because Dell/HPE/Supermicro inflate with the same DRAM while Apple, NVIDIA and cloud seats do not. Results: the S box sits 34% above its card, cutting orders ~17%. 'Price +30%' now gives ARR $80,831 and net +$16,470, still never full-time. Elasticity 1.0 gives net -$43,966. One caution against reading 'price +30%' as upside: copy-cat integrators (RT-VC-DD6-06) would make real elasticity much higher than 0.5.

*Action taken:* Model: new elast_up 0 -> 0.5 and ref_mode 0 -> 1 (fixed Phase-4 card as reference); the sensitivity 'price +/-30%' rows now include the demand response. Phase 4 numbers still reproduce exactly via run_with(P_PHASE4).

### RT-VC-DD6-02 — MAJOR — VC — §2.6 / §10 K1 and K7 / INPUTS conversion 0.08 ('C-48 verify_corrections')

> The 8% qualified-conversation-to-order rate carries the verdict and nothing in the plan measures it. Its basis is the concept card's own correction note, not an observed funnel for $30-63k on-prem AI hardware. K1 passes on '>=3 LOIs at card price' from 20 conversations bought with $50 thank-you payments; a non-binding LOI costs an IT director nothing and says nothing about surviving a 3-6-month security review and a PO. On the model, a 6% close rate puts full-time at m36 (ARR $124,409) and 4% means never (ARR $91,287; 9.1 boxes in 36 months). K1 can therefore pass at a true close rate that leaves the founder never full-time. Round 1 hit the same pattern in DD1 (buy_frac) and DD2 (trial_conversion): an unmeasured conversion input deciding the call.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K1 tightened to cash: >=1 deposit or >=2 paid $2,500 pilots with written conversion terms by 2026-12-31; or a disclosed close rate >=8% from a comparable on-prem AI appliance seller (Lemony, Iternal, Petronella).

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The 8% came from the concept card's own correction note, not from an observed funnel, and K1 could pass on non-binding LOIs. I cut the central close rate to 6% (see RT-HYP-DD6-01) and replaced K1 with the reviewer's cash bar: >=1 deposit or >=2 paid $2,500 pilots with written conversion terms, plus a supplier-onboarding packet started. The decisive finding is what happens when K1 does pass. At 8% with a 6-month cycle and 3 channel conversations a month, m36 ARR is $86,763, net is +$6,446, and the founder is never full-time. Full-time by m36 needs a 21.0% close rate; a non-negative 36-month net needs 8.0%. The kill test therefore cannot confirm a viable business; at most it confirms a hobby.

*Action taken:* Model: conversion 0.08 -> 0.06. Section 10: K1 is cash-based. The November kill test is cancelled because even a pass is not viable (status KILLED). New kill criterion 15: exit if the trailing close rate is below 12% after 30 conversations.

### RT-VC-DD6-03 — MAJOR — VC — §9 backup technician / §10 effort / INPUTS founder_hours_pt 65, h_per_conversation 3, oncall_month 0; simulate() pays a contractor only for overflow hours

> The BASE path sits exactly on the founder's 65 hours a month, and the model hides what it costs to be available while they keep a day job. Support hours go to the founder first and a contractor is paid only for overflow (24.5 hours in 36 months, no retainer). Yet the product is sold with a business-hours, next-business-day SLA, 8-hour on-site installs and 3-6-month security reviews, all of which happen during the day job. Re-runs: founder_hours_pt 55 -> full-time m33 (ARR $145,428); 45 -> never ($122,847). A business-hours backup retainer of $500/month -> m29 ($191,337); $1,000/month -> m31 ($170,552). h_per_conversation is 3 hours for research, discovery, follow-up and proposal, with nothing for security questionnaires, SSP inserts or pilot support: 6 hours -> m35 ($131,361); 10 hours -> never ($112,990). A technician used 24.5 hours in three years will not be on call for regulated buyers.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A named U.S.-person technician signing business-hours availability for <=$300/month; a kill-test time log showing <=3 founder hours per qualified conversation including security-review work; written employer approval for daytime installs and meetings.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid, and the Phase 4 BASE was more fragile than the reviewer showed. In my decomposition, adding only the $400/month insurance change moves full-time from m23 to m28 and ARR from $253,693 to $201,730. The 65-hour budget was used exactly, with only 24.5 contractor hours in 36 months. Changes: a $500/month business-hours retainer from first delivery, and 4 hours per qualified conversation (security questionnaires, SSP inserts, pilot support) instead of 3. Under the revised inputs, delivery and support crowd out selling. Conversations fall from 7 a month (m13-m22) to ~4.8 by m36, and the model's 20% minimum sales share binds. At 55 h a month: ARR $56,202, net -$42,909. At 45 h: $45,291, -$57,263.

*Action taken:* Model: oncall_month $0 -> $500; h_per_conversation 3 -> 4. K7 now requires a time log showing <=4 h per conversation including security-review work. Written employer approval for daytime installs is folded into K0.

### RT-VC-DD6-04 — MAJOR — VC — §0 'a real recurring line' / §2.4 recurring ARR / §11 #12 / INPUTS sub_churn_annual 0.20 with a mandatory, prepaid year-1 subscription

> Most of the 'recurring' ARR is the compulsory first-year support sold with the box, so the PRIMARY gate 'a real recurring line' is not tested inside the model's window. In BASE, 15.6 of the 22.3 boxes under subscription at m36 (70%) were delivered in m25-m36 and are still inside their mandatory, prepaid first year; only 8.7 boxes delivered by m24 could have faced a renewal decision by m36. That is why churn +/-30% barely moves m36 ARR ($247,030-260,519). Subscriptions are 18.4% of 36-month revenue. For an air-gapped box the subscription buys patches and model refreshes through signed offline bundles, which is work the customer's own MSP can do, and the plan needs that same MSP as a channel partner taking 15%. Round 1 (DD4) found that buyers cancel once the purchase decision has passed.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two comparable appliance vendors showing >=70% year-2 renewal, or the first three customers signing non-cancellable 3-year subscriptions at card price.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. I added a 'renewal-tested ARR' metric: the ARR from boxes delivered by m24 that have already faced a renewal decision. In Phase 4 it was $75,334 of $253,693 (30%). In revised BASE it is $22,645 of $63,873 (35%). The rest is mandatory first-year support sold with the box. An air-gapped box's subscription buys signed offline patches and model refreshes, which the customer's own MSP can do, so I raised churn from 20% to 30%. Churn barely moves m36 (+/-30%: $60,863-66,947) precisely because the window cannot test it. 'A real recurring line' is therefore no longer counted as a PRIMARY gate that DD6 clears.

*Action taken:* Model: sub_churn_annual 0.20 -> 0.30; new renewal-tested ARR row in section 5. Kill criterion 12: the first three customers must renew at >=70% or take a non-cancellable 3-year prepay. Section 0 withdraws the 'real recurring line' claim.

### RT-VC-DD6-05 — MAJOR — VC — §0 PROFITABLE / §2.4 / §11 #11 / model §5 (36-mo net, founder salary, cash m36, shadow_rate $85)

> Valued at the model's own rates, 36 months of founder work buy roughly their own wage and no enterprise value. Before full-time the founder works 22 months x 65 h = 1,430 unpaid hours, $121,550 at the model's $85 shadow_rate; then 14 months at $10k loaded = $140,000. Founder labor is therefore ~$261.5k against a founder take of $140,000 salary + $129,441 retained after tax = ~$269k: an economic profit of ~$8k over three years. The $286,019 'cash at m36' exceeds the $40k capital plus $129,441 cumulative net by ~$117k, which is customers' money (annual subscriptions billed in advance and deposits on undelivered boxes). The exit is the one §11 #11 already names, 'offer the fleet to an MSP partner': at 1-2x recurring gross profit (~$14.1k/month at m36 per §2.4) that is ~$170-340k. The m60 ceiling is $559,144 ARR and $1M needs a 24.5% close rate. I have lost money on picks-and-shovels businesses that capped at $2M ARR; this one caps far below that, and asks the founder to leave a day job for a ~$120k loaded salary.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A comparable sale of a small on-prem AI integrator at >=3x ARR, or a revised model in which subscription gross profit exceeds 50% of total gross profit by m24 at unchanged conversion.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. I added the reviewer's metric to the model: 36-month net minus unpaid part-time hours x $85, with a full-time salary treated as equal to the forgone day-job wage. Phase 4 BASE gives +$7,891, matching the reviewer's ~$8k. Revised BASE gives -$228,311 (2,340 unpaid hours, net -$29,411). Customers' money (prepaid subscriptions plus deposits on undelivered boxes) was $163,592 of Phase 4's $286,019 m36 cash, and is $35,709 of revised BASE's $53,934. The subscription share of labor-valued gross profit in m13-m24 was 43% in Phase 4 and is 15% revised, against the reviewer's >50% bar. At 1-2x recurring gross profit the m36 fleet would sell for roughly $30-60k [ESTIMATE]. On the reviewer's own measure, the business never repaid the founder's time even before the other corrections.

*Action taken:* Model section 5 adds economic profit, customers' money in cash, and subscription share of GP rows. Section 2.4 reports them. This finding is part of the KILLED status reason.

### RT-VC-DD6-06 — MAJOR — VC — §4 channels ('load-bearing') / §3 #2 and #6 / §5 Petronella and DIY rows / INPUTS partner_hw_commission 0.10, partner_sub_commission 0.15

> The channel is load-bearing (without it m36 ARR falls 44% to $142,861 and full-time slips to m30) and it is made of the competitors. §3 #2 names Petronella as reseller and 'also a competitor'; §5 calls it the 'closest direct rival; already owns the CMMC buyer', with private LLM servers from $8-12k. KTransformers is Apache 2.0, and the veteran scorer called the SKU 'copyable by any integrator in a week'. The model has an MSP that owns the client, the assessment boundary and the Microsoft account taking 10% of hardware and 15% of subscription, leaving a part-time founder 85% of the recurring margin; the incentive runs the other way, toward the MSP building the box itself. At 10%, a channel L box keeps $5,853 of hardware gross profit (9.4% of $62,525), below Supermicro's 10.8%. With partner_hw_commission 0.20 full-time slips to m30 (ARR $180,944); with no channel and a 6% close rate the founder is never full-time ($96,602).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* One named MSP/RPO (Petronella, Daymark, CyberSheath, Summit 7 or Corsica) signing a co-sell at <=10%/15% with a 12-month undertaking not to build its own DRAM-resident SKU.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The load-bearing channel is made of firms that can build the same Apache-2.0 box in a week. At a 10% hardware share, an MSP keeps less margin by reselling than by building it, so its incentive runs the wrong way. I raised partner terms to 15% of hardware and 20% of subscription and cut channel-sourced conversations from 3 to 2 a month, since the partners compete and the CMMC rush has paused. Petronella is reclassified as a rival, not a channel. With no channel, revised BASE gives ARR $52,843 and net -$42,858. In Phase 4 a K3 failure meant 'side line only'. Now it means kill, because without the channel the side line loses money.

*Action taken:* Model: partner_hw_commission 0.10 -> 0.15; partner_sub_commission 0.15 -> 0.20; channel_conv_month 3 -> 2. K3 requires a written co-sell at <=15%/20% with a 12-month undertaking not to build a DRAM-resident SKU. Kill criterion 4 changed from 'side line' to 'kill'.

### RT-VC-DD6-07 — MAJOR — VC — §5 win condition 3 / §10 K4-K5 / model §2 U.S.-origin note / capacity(), per_user_tps(), conc_exponent 0.62

> For the lead segment (defense: 8 of the 20 kill-test calls, with CMMC Phase 2 as the 'why now'), the L box serves one or two users, not 27. Model §2 prints ~13 tok/s single-stream for Inkling 975B and ~10 tok/s for Nemotron 3 Ultra. Fed through the model's own per_user_tps, Nemotron gives 5.9 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent (below K4's 6 tok/s fail line) and 7.7 at 2; Inkling gives 7.7 at 4 and 10.0 at 2. capacity() returns 1 concurrent user at >=10 tok/s and ~6.7 named users for both. The L box then costs $462 per named user per month ($233 even at 13.3 users) against the $116 claimed, M365 Copilot in GCC High at ~$35 and CoCounsel from $104. K5 passes at '>=8 tok/s single', which is a one-user box, so the test can pass while the defense economics fail. KTransformers documents neither model (repo grep 2026-10-28).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B measures a U.S.-origin 500B+ MoE at >=8 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent on the L box, or >=40% of DIB prospects confirm in writing that PRC-origin weights (Kimi) are acceptable inside their enclave.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid; the same finding as RT-HYP-DD6-04, now under the bandwidth model. Nemotron 3 Ultra runs at 11.0 tok/s single, 6.2 per user at 2 concurrent and 3.4 at 4, which is below K4's 6 tok/s fail line. Inkling runs at 13.7 / 8.0 / 4.4. Both are 1 user and 6.7 named users, ~$395 a named user per month, about 11x Copilot GCC High (~$35) and ~4x CoCounsel. K5's '>=8 tok/s single' would have passed a one-user box. I have no evidence that >=40% of DIB prospects accept PRC-origin weights inside an enclave, and section 5's own policy evidence points the other way.

*Action taken:* K5 raised to >=10 tok/s per user at 2 concurrent; kill criterion 6 fired for L. Defense is addressed only on S with <=400B U.S.-origin models, which a single GPU workstation also runs. Leg killed: the U.S.-origin 1T defense box.

### RT-VC-DD6-08 — MAJOR — VC — §1 'what the customer buys' / §5 'where you lose' / §11 #14 / INPUTS L_dram_gb 768; subscription 'quarterly model refreshes'

> The product is pinned to a model generation that was superseded before the deep dive was written. Kimi K3 (2.8T parameters, ~104B active; open weights 2026-07-27) needs ~1.4-1.56 TB at MXFP4 (Tom's Hardware; Yotta Labs, 2026-07-31; SemiAnalysis via Implicator: it does not fit one DGX B200), and the DD6 notes (§2) list it as the top open-weight model. The L box's 768 GB (12 x 64 GB at 1 DPC) can hold it only as a ~594 GB, ~1.7-bit dynamic quant. Going to 1.5 TB (128 GB RDIMMs) roughly doubles DRAM to ~$45.5k (L box ~$92k at 22% GM), and at 104B versus 32B active parameters the model's own bandwidth scaling gives ~5 tok/s single-stream: one user. So 'native 1T-class quality' is last year's frontier on the day it ships, while Gemma 4 31B and gpt-oss-120b close in from below on one GPU (§5). The subscription promises quarterly model refreshes it cannot deliver at the frontier, and kill criterion 14 watches only a <=$25k desktop, not frontier drift.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Track B blind evaluation on the legal and engineering RAG set showing Kimi-K2.5/Inkling-class output materially beats Gemma 4 31B and is not materially behind K3, so a 768 GB box keeps its quality premium for a 3-year subscription; or a 1.5 TB configuration that passes K4.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid on the trend. I did not re-verify K3's size with my own search; DD6's notes already list Kimi K3 (2.8T) as the top open-weight model [REPORTED]. At ~104B active parameters, routed bytes scale ~3.25x Kimi-K2.5's. On the L platform the bandwidth model then gives ~6.5 tok/s single-stream: one user, before the ~1.5 TB of RDIMMs (~$65k at $42.5/GB) that holding it at MXFP4 would need. So the L box serves last year's frontier on the day it ships, the 'quarterly model refresh' promise holds only for models that fit, and Gemma 4 31B and gpt-oss-120b close in from below on one GPU. This also weakens the S box's premise. Its 200-300B models must beat single-GPU 30-120B models on the buyer's own documents, or the DRAM is wasted.

*Action taken:* New K8: a blind evaluation on the buyers' RAG set must show a clear preference for the 200B-1T model over Gemma 4 31B / gpt-oss-120b, else kill. Kill criterion 14 also fires on frontier drift. The subscription promise is narrowed to 'refreshes within the installed memory envelope'.

### RT-VC-DD6-09 — MAJOR — VC — §5 Mac Studio row / §2.3 cost per user / §11 #14

> A cheaper, faster 1T substitute already exists, and the kill criterion is drawn so that it misses it. Since macOS Tahoe 26.2 (Dec-2025), RDMA over Thunderbolt 5 lets Mac Studios pool memory. Jeff Geerling ran Kimi K2 Thinking on four M3 Ultras at 28.3 tok/s in exo, and Creative Strategies reported ~25 tok/s, ~2 s to first token and <450 W on a '$40K Mac Studio cluster'. With the M5 Ultra 512 GB at $22,299 (§5), two units give 1 TB of unified memory for ~$44.6k. That is 29% below the $62,525 L box and holds 1T at INT4 (~600 GB) with room for KV cache, against the L box's estimated 17 tok/s and ~20 s first token. §5 and §2.3 treat the Mac only as a single 512 GB box (1T at ~3-bit, 5 users). Kill criterion 14 fires only for 'a desktop at $25k or less', which excludes the existing ~$40-45k cluster. Multi-user concurrency on exo is unproven, and that is the L box's one remaining argument, but concurrency is unmeasured for the L box too.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B benchmarks a 2x M5 Ultra RDMA cluster beside the L box and the cluster fails K4 (per-user rate at 4 concurrent, or 8k TTFT); or criterion 14 is rewritten to 'any configuration <=$50k' and is not triggered.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid; the same finding as RT-HYP-DD6-03, verified by search. Two M5 Ultra 512GB cost $44,598 for 1 TB of unified memory, 43% below the revised $78,708 L box. Two M3 Ultras already ran Kimi K2 Thinking at ~15 tok/s and four at 28.3 tok/s, with ~2 s to first token, against the L box's ~16 tok/s and ~22 s. Kill criterion 14 watched only a <=$25k desktop, so it could never fire on a cluster. I agree with the reviewer that concurrency on exo is unproven. But the bandwidth model now says the L box is also a 1-2-user machine, so concurrency is not the L box's remaining argument. Its only remaining edge is ECC/BMC/Linux in a CMMC boundary.

*Action taken:* Kill criterion 14 rewritten to 'any configuration at or below the L price ... at or above the L box's measured per-user rate'; it fires today, so L is out of BASE. Mac-cluster rows added to model section 4 and deep dive section 5. Leg killed: the L SKU outside the DIB.

### RT-VET-DD6-02 — MAJOR — VET — §2.1 BOM (DRAM $22,770, 47% of L BOM) / §4 supply rules / §2.5 COGS row / §11 #13 / INPUTS dram_small_buyer_premium=1.15, glut_elasticity

> The DRAM line is priced at contract x 1.15 = $29.65 per GB, or $1,898 per 64 GB RDIMM, citing 'street ~$32/GB' from Aug-2026. The channel has moved well past that. Fusion Worldwide's July 2026 Greensheet reports 64 GB DDR5 RDIMMs trading at $3,000-3,700, with a June average of ~$2,972 for 64 GB 6400 ($46-58/GB). It also reports new RDIMM bookings averaging over 54 weeks, with allocations capped. ServerMonkey's October update has enterprise RDIMM spot up 35% in two months, with quotes expiring in as little as two hours. These sources are brokers with an interest, but even the doc's own $32/GB plus 35% is ~$43/GB. I re-ran the model at $2,972-3,700 per 64 GB. The L box quotes at $79,056-90,256 (not $62,525) and the S box at $38,887-44,487. That puts L inside the DGX Station GB300 band ($87-95k), which §5 credits with ~3x the users, so win condition #1 ('1T-class under $65k') fails. The model hides this. BASE conversion does not respond to price (glut_elasticity applies only after the turn), so a dearer RDIMM simply raises the cost-plus box price and the profit with it. 36-month net rises from $129k to $164-180k, and full-time comes earlier (m21). The model rewards the shortage. On supply, DD6's own DFARS 252.246-7008 rule rules out brokers, which leaves authorized allocation for an integrator with no purchase history. That cannot support 'parts bought in the order month, delivered month+1' or 14-day customer quotes when supplier quotes last hours. Kill criterion 13 watches GPU lead time only, although DRAM is the larger half of the BOM.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Three written authorized-channel quotes (franchised distributor or a Supermicro/ASRock Rack channel partner) for 12 x 64 GB DDR5-6400 RDIMMs from Samsung, SK hynix or Micron at <=$32/GB, with <=6-week delivery and a >=14-day price hold. Plus a BASE re-run in which conversion falls with box price, and an RDIMM lead-time trigger added to §11 #13.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid on direction; the size is partly verified. My searches (2026-11-01, snippets) found three things. SHI lists a 64 GB DDR5-6400 RDIMM at $7,862.50 MSRP (an undated list price). Origin Storage's 64 GB-6400 is GBP 2,516 on special, ~$52/GB. Used 64 GB DDR5 sold for $890-1,200 to Aug-2026. I could not see Fusion's prices, so the $2,972-3,700 Greensheet figure stays reviewer-cited. I set the authorized small-buyer price to $42.5/GB (contract $25.78 x 1.65), which is $2,722 per 64 GB, against $1,898. That is the doc's own $32 street price plus the cited +35%. The premium unwinds to 1.15 across 2028. The L box now quotes $78,708 (with 13 RDIMMs) and the S box $41,091, so win condition #1 ('1T-class under $65k') fails. I also accept the hidden-reward flaw. With the new demand response to price rises (RT-VC-DD6-01), dearer RDIMMs now hurt: BASE net is -$23,755 at premium 1.15, -$29,411 at 1.65 and -$36,496 at 2.0 ($52/GB). Authorized supply with no purchase history is a real lead-time risk, and DRAM is the larger half of the BOM.

*Action taken:* Model: dram_small_buyer_premium 1.15 -> 1.65 pre-turn; new dram_premium_post_turn 1.15 (unwinds across 2028); elasticity on rises (see RT-VC-DD6-01). Kill criterion 13 now covers RDIMM lead time >8 weeks and a missing >=14-day price hold. K1b shows buyers the $41k/$79k card. Three written authorized quotes are required before any price card is shown.

### RT-HYP-DD6-08 — MINOR — HYP — §7 'Dates' / §3 #5 trigger / §8 m13–m15 'CMMC Phase 3'

> DD6 dates its demand catalyst to 'Phase 2 starts 2026-11-10' (A-LIGN, Strike Graph). On 2026-07-13 the Department of War suspended Phase 2 (C3PAO Level 2 certification) pending a 60-day review (Clark Hill; McGuireWoods 2026-07-14; CSIS). My 2026-11-01 search found no published resumption. Phase 1 self-assessment and the NIST 800-171 obligations remain, so the need to keep CUI on-prem is unchanged. What is gone is the urgency, and with it the MSP/RPO pipeline rush that DD6 leans on: the 8 DIB interviews, the CMMC Con lead plan and the 'Phase 3 2027-11-10' gate. This is a factual error in a dated driver, not a change in what is mandated, so I rate it MINOR.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* An official DoW notice resuming Phase 2 with a dated C3PAO milestone. Or Track A K2 shows ≥40% of DIB prospects budgeting for on-prem AI regardless of Phase 2 timing.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid and verified by search (2026-11-01, snippets). The Department of War suspended CMMC Phase 2 on 2026-07-13 and paused later milestones pending a 60-day review due mid-September (Jenner, McGuireWoods 2026-07-14, HSToday, Redspin). I found no resumption. DFARS 7012 and NIST 800-171 duties remain, so the reason to keep CUI on-prem is unchanged, but the urgency and the MSP/RPO rush behind the channel plan are gone. This is part of why I cut the close rate (8% -> 6%) and channel conversations (3 -> 2 a month).

*Action taken:* Deep dive sections 3 (#5) and 7 re-dated with sources; the roadmap's 'Phase 3 2027-11-10' gate is superseded. Model effects come through conversion and channel_conv_month (RT-HYP-DD6-01, RT-VC-DD6-06).

### RT-HYP-DD6-09 — MINOR — HYP — §2.1 S BOM / §4 supply rules / INPUTS S_gpu $5,000

> The S SKU uses a GeForce RTX 5090 priced at Micro Center retail. NVIDIA's GeForce driver licence (§2.8) says the software is 'not licensed for datacenter deployment' and does not define the term. A regulated buyer's counsel or software-asset team will flag a GeForce card in a rack server serving 40 users, and ask for an indemnity a one-person vendor cannot give. Retail quantity limits and consumer AIB warranties also sit badly with DD6's 'OCMs and authorized distributors only' rule. The fix is cheap: re-spec the S SKU on an RTX PRO-class card bought through PNY. With S_gpu at $7,500, m36 ARR stays at $253,822, because pricing is cost-plus.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Written confirmation from NVIDIA or PNY that a GeForce card in a server at a customer site is licensed use. Or the S SKU re-specced on an RTX PRO card, with an authorized-channel quote, before any price card is shown in Track A.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid; same fix as RT-VET-DD6-03. The S box now uses an RTX PRO 5000 Blackwell 48GB at $8,216 (SHI listing; MSRP $8,999), not a GeForce RTX 5090. The reviewer's point that cost-plus pricing keeps ARR flat no longer holds, because orders now respond to price (RT-VC-DD6-01). The swap alone costs ~$5.6k of 36-month net (leave-one-out: -$23,840 vs -$29,411), which is small next to the other changes.

*Action taken:* Model: S_gpu $5,000 -> $8,216; S_vram_gb 32 -> 48. Supply rules: no GeForce parts in any SKU.

### RT-HYP-DD6-10 — MINOR — HYP — §5 competitors / §0 pitch ('1T-class open-weight model entirely inside a regulated firm's own network')

> Hyperscalers now run frontier closed models inside the customer's own network. Gemini on Google Distributed Cloud air-gapped went GA in Aug 2025 (SiliconANGLE). On 2026-10-30, Gemini 3.5 Flash went GA there, alongside HGX B300 systems (IT Brief). DD6's competitor table has no on-prem hyperscaler row. GDC's minimum footprint is far larger than a 20–40-person team needs. So this hits the top of DD6's range (50–300-attorney firms, university enclaves) and the 'only open weights give you frontier quality on-prem' story, not the core small-team buyer.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A published or quoted GDC air-gapped minimum configuration well above the L box's budget class (e.g. >$500k), confirming the overlap is limited to the largest prospects.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid as stated by the reviewer. I did not verify it with my own search: I spent my quota on DRAM, CMMC, GPU, Mac-cluster and NUMA facts, so it is tagged [REPORTED via red team]. Google Distributed Cloud air-gapped is a managed, rack-scale deployment, so it competes for 50-300-attorney firms and university enclaves, not 20-40-person teams. The 'only open weights give you frontier quality on-prem' line is withdrawn.

*Action taken:* Deep dive section 5 adds a GDC air-gapped row; the target range is capped at small enclaves and firms under ~300 staff. No model change.

### RT-PHY-DD6-03 — MINOR — PHY — §1 S SKU ('interactive') / §2.3 S row (40 users, $40/user) / capacity() (no GPU-memory term) / INPUTS S_prefill_*

> capacity() has no GPU-memory term, and the S box's 32 GB RTX 5090 runs out of KV cache before it runs out of decode speed. MiniMax-M2 uses grouped-query attention with 8 KV heads x 128 dims over 62 layers: 2 x 62 x 8 x 128 x 2 B = 248 KB per token at BF16, or 124 KB at FP8. The GPU must also hold: ~4.0B non-expert params (attention plus the 200k-vocabulary embeddings), ~4 GB at FP8; a layerwise-prefill double buffer for one layer's 256 experts, 2 x 256 x 14.2M x 1 B = 7.2 GB; ~2 GB of CUDA workspace [ESTIMATE]. That leaves ~19 GB, enough for ~74k tokens of BF16 KV or ~148k at FP8, shared by all users. Six concurrent users at the 32k prompts the S box's own source benchmarks (P2: 2,540 tok/s prefill at 32k) need 192k tokens before any decode, so only 2 users (BF16) to 4 users (FP8) fit. KTransformers' own DeepSeek-V4-Flash doc, for the other S-class model, defaults to MAX_RUNNING_REQUESTS = 2 (notes P8). On RAG-length contexts the S box is a 2-4-user machine. Its $40 per user, the venture's best per-user number, holds only up to ~12k tokens per user. The L box is not affected: its 96 GB card has room for a ~19 GB Kimi layer buffer and MLA KV of ~70 KB per token.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B logs peak VRAM and maximum sustained concurrent sessions on the S box at 8k and 32k contexts with FP8 KV, showing >=6 sessions at 32k with no KV eviction or host offload. Or capacity() gets a VRAM/KV constraint and §2.3 is re-run.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. On the 32 GB RTX 5090 the new kv_max_users() gives 4 users at 32k-token FP8 KV (the reviewer says 2-4), below Phase 4's 6. The re-spec to a 48 GB RTX PRO 5000 (RT-VET-DD6-03) leaves ~34.8 GB free after ~13.2 GB of fixed weights, buffers and workspace. That is ~280k tokens of FP8 KV, or 8 users at 32k. Decode bandwidth (3 users) now binds before VRAM does. The L box is unaffected (96 GB, MLA ~70 KB per token).

*Action taken:* Model: capacity() gains a VRAM/KV cap (new *_vram_gb, *_vram_fixed_gb, *_kv_kb_per_tok, design_ctx_tokens); S_vram_gb 32 -> 48. Track B logs peak VRAM and sustained sessions at 8k and 32k.

### RT-PHY-DD6-04 — MINOR — PHY — §0 pitch ('A quiet, U.S.-assembled server') / §3 #7 ('office power and noise bite') / INPUTS L_platform (2 kW PSU), install_h_per_box 8

> The L box is a sustained ~1.1-1.3 kW load, not a quiet office appliance, and making it quiet costs memory bandwidth. Power. Nameplate draw [ESTIMATE / UNVERIFIED-training]: EPYC 9455-class at 300 W TDP, RTX PRO 6000 Workstation at 600 W (300 W Max-Q), 12 DDR5 RDIMMs at ~10-13 W each while streaming, plus fans, NVMe and ~92% PSU efficiency. A 120 V/15 A office circuit is limited to 1.44 kW continuous (80% rule). The box leaves no headroom on its circuit and puts ~4,000 BTU/h into whatever closet holds it. Bandwidth. Twelve tightly packed RDIMMs next to a 600 W GPU need server-class airflow to stay below 85 °C. Above 85 °C, DDR5 halves tREFI from 3.9 to 1.95 µs [JESD79-5, UNVERIFIED-training]. With tRFC1 = 295 ns (16Gb die), all-bank refresh then takes 15.1% of device time instead of 7.6%; with 410 ns (24/32Gb die), 21.0% instead of 10.5%. BMC memory throttling acts even earlier. So a 'quiet tower' fan curve taxes exactly the bandwidth the whole capacity case rests on. The model has no line for an electrical survey, a dedicated circuit or cooling, and allows 8 install hours.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Track B log of wall power, DIMM SPD-hub temperatures and decode tok/s at 4 concurrent users, at a fan curve measured at <=45 dBA at 1 m, showing DIMMs below 85 °C with no throttle events. Plus an install checklist (dedicated 20 A circuit or colo) priced into the setup fee.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. An L box draws a sustained ~1.1-1.3 kW, which leaves no headroom on a 120 V/15 A circuit (1.44 kW continuous) and puts ~4,000 BTU/h into a room. A quiet fan curve risks DIMMs above 85 C, where refresh doubles and bandwidth falls; the JEDEC figures are the reviewer's [UNVERIFIED-training]. The 'quiet office appliance' claim is withdrawn. Both SKUs are server-closet or colo devices. The install checklist adds an electrical and thermal survey, a dedicated 20 A (or 208 V) circuit billed at cost, and SPD-hub DIMM temperatures logged during the soak with a throttle check. A 300 W Max-Q RTX PRO 6000 is specified if L is ever re-admitted. Install time rises from 8 h to 12 h. The law-firm segment loses its quiet-tower option, so colo only.

*Action taken:* Model: install_h_per_box 8 -> 12. Deep dive section 3 (#7) and the pitch no longer promise a quiet office tower; K4-L includes the DIMM-temperature and dBA test.

### RT-PHY-DD6-05 — MINOR — PHY — §1 L SKU (768 GB = 12 x 64 GB, 1 DPC) / §2.2 subscription (3%/yr spare-parts reserve, business-hours SLA) / §11 #13

> The L box has no degraded mode for the commonest hardware fault in a large DIMM population. Kimi-K2.5 needs >=600 GB of RAM (>=650 GB per the K2-Thinking native doc, notes P4), and the box has 768 GB in 12 DIMMs. EPYC one-DIMM-per-channel population rules allow balanced sets of 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 or 12 DIMMs per socket [UNVERIFIED-training: AMD memory population guide]. If one DIMM fails, the box has two options: 11 DIMMs, outside the balanced sets, with uneven interleave and lower bandwidth; or 10 DIMMs: 640 GB, below the 650 GB K2-Thinking floor and ~40 GB above K2.5's once OS and staging buffers are counted, with 17% less channel bandwidth (10/12). Since the capacity case is already on a knife-edge (RT-PHY-DD6-01), one failed RDIMM takes the 1T model out of service until an authorized replacement arrives. At BASE m36 the fleet is 22.3 boxes, so ~268 RDIMMs if they were all L boxes. A 0.5-1% per DIMM-year replacement rate [ESTIMATE] gives ~1-3 memory outages a year by m36, and ~8% of DIMMs a year logging correctable errors (Schroeder et al., SIGMETRICS 2009) [UNVERIFIED-training] gives the warning signs. The next-business-day SLA holds only with an on-site spare per L box ($1,898 per 64 GB RDIMM at the model's own price), which the 3%/yr reserve does not hold. This adds to RT-VET-DD6-06, which priced depot spares: the box has no degraded operating point at all.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A Track B test that pulls one RDIMM and shows the L box still serving Kimi-K2.5 INT4 at >=80% of the 12-DIMM decode rate on 11 (or 10) DIMMs. Or the subscription spec and cash model carry one on-site spare RDIMM per L box, plus correctable-error telemetry with a replace threshold.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. With 12 x 64 GB, one failed RDIMM leaves either an unbalanced 11-DIMM population or 640 GB, below the 650 GB Kimi floor. So the L box had no degraded operating point. Fix: the L SKU buys 13 RDIMMs (832 GB), one kept on-site as a cold spare, so an L swap needs no NBD shipment. The S box (384 GB for a ~230 GB FP8 model) can run on 10 DIMMs (320 GB) in a balanced population at ~17% less bandwidth [ESTIMATE], so it has a degraded mode. Correctable-error telemetry with a replace threshold (RT-VET-DD6-06) gives the early warning. The extra RDIMM adds ~$2.7k to the L BOM. That does not matter in BASE because L is not quoted.

*Action taken:* Model: L_dram_gb 768 -> 832 (12 installed + 1 on-site spare); the spare pool covers S. A pull-one-DIMM test is added to K4-L.

### RT-VC-DD6-10 — MINOR — VC — §10 effort and K7 / DD7 §10 and DD2 §10 dates

> The kill test is under-budgeted and triple-booked. K7 requires 20 qualified conversations in 28 days, 4x the model's conv_target_pt of 5 a month. At the model's own 3 hours each that is 60 hours, more than the ~55 hours budgeted for the whole test before K0, Track B and Track C. DD7 §10 schedules a ~50-hour test on the same dates (2026-11-02 -> 11-30) and DD2 §10 a ~55-hour test (2026-11-02 -> 12-01), against ~65 founder hours a month. Paying $50 per call also selects people who take paid calls over budget holders. Either the 5-a-month funnel is too conservative or K7 cannot be met; both cannot be right.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A dated calendar that runs DD6's test alone in November with a time log, and K7 restated against the model's funnel (for example >=10 unpaid qualified conversations, >=3 with budget holders).

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. K7 needed 20 conversations at 3 h each, 60 h, inside a ~55-hour test that shared its November dates with DD7's (~50 h) and DD2's (~55 h) tests, against ~65 founder hours a month. Paying $50 per call selects for people who take paid calls, not budget holders. Because the revised economics fail even when the test passes (RT-VC-DD6-02), the DD6 November test is cancelled outright, which removes the conflict. If a reopen trigger fires, the test runs alone over 6 weeks and ~80 hours with a time log, and K7 becomes >=10 unpaid qualified conversations with >=3 budget holders.

*Action taken:* Section 10 rewritten: test cancelled; a reopen-only version with K7 restated (>=10 unpaid, >=3 budget holders, 6 weeks, run alone, time-logged). No model change.

### RT-VC-DD6-11 — MINOR — VC — §1 'what it is bad at' / §2.3 named users / §10 K4 / INPUTS L_prefill_8k 368, peak_active_share 0.15

> Capacity is measured on chat-length prompts, but the stated use case is long-context. The ~27 named users rest on 4 concurrent streams at a 15% peak-active share, with TTFT checked at 2k and 8k tokens only. §1's own fit, 'draft this memo from these 40 documents', is a 30-100k-token prompt. At the model's 8k prefill rate (368 tok/s) that is ~89 s at 32k, ~178 s at 64k and ~272 s at 100k before the first token, with the single GPU busy prefilling while other users queue. K4 tests only 2k and 8k, so it cannot catch this. Fewer effective users push the L box's cost per user toward the $233-462 range in RT-VC-DD6-07.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B adds TTFT at 32k and 64k with 2 concurrent users and shows <=60 s at 32k, or the named-user count is re-derived from a measured RAG trace.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid; the same mechanism as RT-HYP-DD6-05. At the L box's 368 tok/s prefill rate, the first token takes ~89 s at 32k, ~178 s at 64k and ~272 s at 100k, and the single GPU is busy for that whole time, so other users queue. Named users are now derived at a 32k-token design context, with a GPU KV-memory cap. L falls to 6.7-13.3 named users, ~$222-395 per user per month, inside the reviewer's $233-462 range. S keeps 20 named users with ~13 s TTFT at 32k (the P2 measurement). A measured RAG trace remains the right test.

*Action taken:* Model: design_ctx_tokens 32,000 and a KV cap in capacity(); section 2 prints 32k/64k TTFT. K4-S and K4-L add 32k/64k TTFT tests under concurrent load.

### RT-VET-DD6-03 — MINOR — VET — §1 S SKU (RTX 5090) / §2.1 GPU prices / §4 supply rules (DFARS 252.246-7008, 'no gray-market GPUs') / INPUTS S_gpu

> The S box's $5,000 GPU is the retail Micro Center price for an RTX 5090. The model's own source gives the online price as $8.5-10k. A retail store is not an OCM or authorized distributor, which DD6's own supply rule requires. NVIDIA's GeForce software licence also carries a 'No Datacenter Deployment' clause, which NVIDIA enforced against a Japanese GPU host in 2017. A rack server in a client's server room, or in the colo cage §3 #7 offers, is the kind of deployment a regulated buyer's legal review will flag. GeForce board-partner warranties are consumer warranties. The S box is the fallback if L fails (§11 #5), so its BOM needs a PRO-class card (RTX PRO Blackwell, with ECC) or the authorized $8.5-10k price. That adds ~$3.5-5k of BOM, or $4.5-6.4k of quote at 22% GM.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Current NVIDIA licence text, or written NVIDIA/partner confirmation, that excludes customer-premises enterprise servers from 'datacenter deployment'. Or the S SKU re-priced with an authorized-channel PRO card and still quoting at <=$33k.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. A Micro Center GeForce card breaks DD6's own authorized-source rule, carries a consumer warranty, and invites a licence challenge over 'no datacenter deployment'. I re-specced the S box on an RTX PRO 5000 Blackwell 48GB (PNY), listed at $8,216 by SHI (MSRP $8,999; search snippet, undated). It has ECC, a professional driver licence and an authorized channel. Its 48 GB also fixes the KV ceiling in RT-PHY-DD6-03. The S quote rises from $30,622 to $41,091 together with the DRAM change, so the reviewer's '<=$33k' condition is not met. On its own the GPU swap costs ~$5.6k of 36-month net and $1.6k of m36 ARR (leave-one-out run: -$23,840 and $65,503 vs BASE -$29,411 and $63,873). It is a required compliance fix and small next to the L and close-rate changes.

*Action taken:* Model: S_gpu $5,000 -> $8,216; S_vram_gb 32 -> 48. Deep dive section 2.1 updated; supply rules now forbid GeForce parts.

### RT-VET-DD6-04 — MINOR — VET — §10 Track B and K4 thresholds / §11 #5 / INPUTS L_cpu ('9455-class' [UNVERIFIED]), min_user_tps=10

> The gate does not test the machine the model prices. (1) K4 passes L at >=8 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent, but capacity() counts a user only at >=10 tok/s. A box that just passes K4 serves at most 3 at a time: ~20 named users and ~$155 per user per month, not 27 and $116. (2) Track B allows 'EPYC 9005 or Xeon 6'. A Xeon 6 with MRDIMM is the 845 GB/s-per-socket machine behind conc_saturation, so measuring on it would validate the wrong box. (3) On EPYC, socket bandwidth depends on the CCD count and on whether the part is a 'wide' GMI OPN. AMD's 58462 guide gives each CCD one dedicated GMI link, or two on wide parts. The BOM names no SKU, only a recalled $5,500 '9455-class' price, so the ~614 GB/s the throughput rests on is assumed, not specified.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* K4's pass line raised to min_user_tps (10 tok/s per user at 4 concurrent), and Track B pinned to the exact BOM CPU SKU, DIMM population and speed, with STREAM triad recorded alongside tok/s.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. K4 passed at 8 tok/s per user, while capacity() counted a user only at >=10. Track B also allowed a Xeon 6 MRDIMM host, which is the 845 GB/s machine behind conc_saturation, and no CPU SKU was named. Fixes: K4 now uses the model's own 10 tok/s floor. Track B must run on the exact EPYC 9005 1S SKU priced in the BOM (a 48-core 9455/9455P-class part, with CCD/GMI width logged [UNVERIFIED-training on CCD count]) and 12 DIMMs at a logged MT/s. NPS mode and STREAM triad are recorded beside tok/s, and the run is pinned with numactl. A Xeon 6 MRDIMM result is not accepted as evidence for the EPYC box.

*Action taken:* Deep dive section 10: K4-S and K4-L rewritten (exact SKU, pinning, STREAM, >=10 tok/s per user). Kill criterion 5 references K4-L. No model change beyond the bandwidth model (RT-VET-DD6-01).

### RT-VET-DD6-05 — MINOR — VET — §4 supply rules ('Samsung, SK hynix or Micron RDIMMs, with a provenance sheet') / §5 win condition 3 (U.S.-origin curation) / §7 procurement

> 'Major-brand RDIMM' is not the same as non-PRC content. SK hynix makes a large share of its DRAM at Wuxi, China (~40% in 2022-23 reporting [UNVERIFIED-training]). DDR5 RDIMM register (RCD) and SPD-hub chips come from three suppliers, one of which is Montage Technology of Shanghai. None of this is prohibited: §5949 names CXMT, YMTC and SMIC. But the buyer DD6 courts with 'U.S.-origin model curation' will ask where the memory was made. Module makers do not publish die fab site or RCD vendor per lot to a small buyer, so the provenance sheet can show brand and lot, not origin. Selling a U.S.-origin model on possibly Wuxi-fabbed DRAM with a Shanghai RCD is an awkward discovery in a CMMC security review.
