*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Written per-lot declarations from the module maker (DRAM fab site plus RCD/PMIC vendor) obtained at small-buyer volume. Or a SKU rule that restricts L/S to module part numbers with a declared non-PRC fab and non-PRC RCD.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. A major-brand RDIMM is not proof of non-PRC content. SK hynix fabs DRAM at Wuxi, and Montage (Shanghai) is one of three RCD suppliers [UNVERIFIED-training]. A small buyer cannot get per-lot fab-site declarations. What it can get is each module's SPD record, which names the DRAM, RCD, PMIC and SPD-hub manufacturers [UNVERIFIED-training: JEDEC SPD field layout]. The provenance sheet will record those IDs plus lot and date codes, buy only through authorized channels, and reject modules with a Montage RCD where that is visible. It will state that the fab site is not disclosed at small-buyer volume. The 'U.S.-origin curation' pitch never claimed non-PRC hardware; it now says so explicitly. This changes paperwork, not economics.

*Action taken:* Deep dive section 4 supply rules: SPD-decoded component vendor IDs on the provenance sheet, a no-Montage-RCD rule, and no non-PRC claim. No model change.

### RT-VET-DD6-06 — MINOR — VET — §1 'a 48-hour burn-in' / §2.2 subscription cost (2 h/month, 3%/yr parts reserve) / INPUTS build_h_per_box, parts_reserve_pct_yr / §9 backup technician

> A 48-hour unattended memtest at room temperature is a system soak, not burn-in: there is no thermal or voltage acceleration. Samsung, SK hynix and Micron already test and burn in modules. Latent DIMM faults show up in the field as growing correctable-error counts. Google's fleet study (Schroeder et al., SIGMETRICS 2009 [UNVERIFIED-training]) found more than 8% of DIMMs logging correctable errors each year. The subscription has no correctable-error telemetry, no DDR5 post-package-repair runbook and no spare-RDIMM stock. With authorized bookings over 54 weeks (DD6-02), a next-business-day SLA needs spares on the shelf: about one 64 GB and one 32 GB RDIMM per ~5 boxes, or ~$6-12k of idle capital for the BASE m36 fleet of 22.3 boxes. That money is not in the cash model, and every swap is an escorted visit to a CUI enclave.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The model adds spare-RDIMM inventory to working capital, and the subscription spec adds correctable-error monitoring with a replace threshold. Or a module vendor's written advance-replacement SLA of <=5 business days at small-buyer volume.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. A 48-hour room-temperature memtest is a soak, not a burn-in, and the subscription had no correctable-error telemetry, no PPR runbook and no spares. Fixes: rename it to a 48-hour soak. Add rasdaemon/EDAC correctable-error monitoring with a replace threshold, plus a DDR5 post-package-repair runbook. Hold a spare pool as inventory: one spare RDIMM per 5 boxes under subscription and one 48 GB loaner GPU once 5 boxes are fielded. The parts reserve rises from 3% to 4% a year. At revised BASE m36 the pool holds $7,777. Every swap inside a CUI enclave is an escorted visit priced inside the existing 2 h/month. Spares and the higher reserve cost ~$4.1k of 36-month net (leave-one-out run: -$25,357 vs BASE -$29,411) and leave ARR unchanged.

*Action taken:* Model: new spare_kit_boxes 0 -> 5 and loaner_gpu_trigger 0 -> 5 (inventory cash); parts_reserve_pct_yr 0.03 -> 0.04. Deep dive section 1 now says 48-hour soak with CE logging.

## DD7 — Authorized-Aftermarket Legacy DRAM Die Bank (+ hi-rel MCMs) (legacy-dram-diebank)

**Final status after red team: KILLED.** No objection was refuted. All three FATALs were confirmed by the model, and they kill L1, the managed die-bank program and the only line this founder could run. Banking bare die costs a board OEM $186k-347k to manage ~$52k of die, 2.9-5.4x a packaged last-time buy plus dry storage. Board OEMs qualify packaged part numbers, and Micross sells the whole bundle direct (EDSP). L3, the title-taking bank, is killed by the completed pull-cost stack (RT-VET-DD7-03) plus bare-die storage yield, surveillance, whole-wafer pulls and a 2.0x blended multiple. Bought at m20 in BASE it has unit GM -12.8% and IRR -26.6%, and no bank is bought in any scenario; break-even needs <=$1.53/die all-in. Plan C is killed on founder constraints. L4, the MCM, survives only as a capital-heavy option. It needs a stacked/RDL redesign, a 125 C bin and per-lot requalification, and Plan B now returns 0.29x on $11.49M (GLUT 0.24x, SEVERE 0.19x). The moat (OCM authorized-aftermarket rights) was never tested, and the evidence says the OCMs that would talk are extending their lines. What remains, L2 rep commissions ($8.6k BASE m36 ARR, never breaking even at Phase 4 fixed costs), is not this concept. It also costs DD6 time.

*Revised verdicts* — Possible: Only for incumbents (revised after red team). Rochester, Micross and Teledyne prove the businesses at scale, but each leg fails for an entrant. 10-20-year bare-die DRAM storage is TRL 6-7: no published bondability or yield data past ~3 years, and Infineon caps unsawn wafers in N2 at 36 months. The 8 GB x72 MCM as described does not fit 15 x 20 mm (405-675 mm2 of die). It needs stacking with whole-wafer RDL, and JEDEC specifies no DDR4 refresh above 95 C, so a 125 C part needs a per-die hot bin and a refresh derate (23-72% refresh tax). An unauthorized re-packaged die is a new part number, so it cannot serve DO-254 or FDA locks. · Profitable: No (revised after red team). L1 costs the OEM 2.9-5.4x a packaged LTB, so it has no rational buyer. The revised bank loses money in every scenario: BASE m20 unit GM -12.8% and IRR -26.6%; GLUT -109% and -99.4%. IRR break-even needs <=$1.53/die all-in pull cost. Plan B returns 0.29x of $11.49M outside capital in BASE (0.24x GLUT, 0.19x SEVERE). Year-10 EBITDA is -$1.10M; MCM GM is 18.7/26.7/38.2% (m1/m36/m60); the BULL case is ~22%/yr gross before dilution over 10 years. The residual L2 rep line reaches $5.0k ARR at m18 and $8.6k at m36 in BASE ($4.4k GLUT, $928 SEVERE) and never breaks even at $680/month of fixed cost. · Doable by this founder: No (revised after red team). The founder-doable wedge (L1) has no rational buyer. DoD-facing study work needs NIST SP 800-171 compliance (~$35k + $500/month) and defense-OEM agency needs AS9120B (~$35k); each breaks the $20k cash floor by m6. The bank and the MCM need OCM authorization, a hi-rel co-founder and ~$11.5M. Every 10 hours a month diverted to DD7 moves DD6's full-time switch from m23 to m33, and 20 hours means never. The residual rep line also needs an employer carve-out.

### RT-HYP-DD7-01 — FATAL — HYP — §1 'The die bank' / §2.1 L1 row / §0 recommendation / INPUTS ltb_die_per_program 4000, kgd_factor 0.85, pull_margin_unit 1, bank_nre_first 120000

> L1 is the only line the founder can run and the 'optional SKU' DD7 keeps, and it has no buyer.
> - **No pull path.** An OEM can draw banked die only through a package and at-speed test program built for that die. DD7 prices that set-up at $120k for the first family (bank_nre_first) and books it to L3. L1, by contrast, assumes the OEM pulls die through an OSAT at ~$10 a unit (the pull_margin_unit note) with no set-up at all. One L1 program's die are worth ~$51.9k (4,000 × 0.75 GB × $20.37 × 0.85; the model's $2,077 agency fee is 4% of that), so the set-up costs 2.3× the die. A rational OEM instead buys ~4,000 packaged parts (~$61k) and stores them dry or under N2 to GEIA-STD-0003B/IEC 62435, or goes to Micross direct (§5).
> - **No die supply.** Micron sells no open KGD (01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 10), so an OEM whose board carries Micron or Samsung packaged DDR4 cannot buy that die at all. Winbond/ESMT KGD is a different die that forces requalification, which is the very lock DD7 wants to monetize. The parties that can use bare DRAM die (Teledyne, Mercury, Frontgrade, Micross, DDC) are the incumbents, and they have their own vaults.
> Programs make up ~$34k of Plan A's $42.6k m36 ARR, so without L1 only ~$8.6k of commissions remains.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B/C finds ≥3 mid-size OEMs that already own, or will fund, a qualified package-and-test flow for a bare DRAM die they can actually buy, at program sizes where die value exceeds the set-up. Or L1 is re-specified as packaged-part LTB storage management, with quotes showing that Micross, Rochester and franchised distributors do not already offer it at or below $750/month.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and the model confirms it. One L1 program's die are worth $51.9k as KGD at the 2026 peak ($61.1k as packaged parts). Phase 4 was internally inconsistent: it charged L3 a $40-120k package-and-test set-up per part family but let L1 pull die through an OSAT at ~$10 with no set-up at all. Model §10b prices the OEM's whole die-bank route: die + founder billing + U.S. package/test ($10-12.11/die) + vault (3%/yr, or a $5k/yr minimum) + set-up. That is $186k-289k if the die are drawn at 80/month (exhausted in 50 months), and $230k-347k over the model's 100-month program life. Against ~$64k for a packaged LTB plus dry or N2 storage under GEIA-STD-0003B/IEC 62435, it is 2.9-5.4x. The die-supply point also stands. Micron sells no open KGD, so a board carrying Micron or Samsung DDR4 cannot bank its own die. A Winbond/ESMT die is a different die and forces the requalification the LTB was meant to avoid. The bare-die users (Teledyne, Mercury, Frontgrade, Micross, DDC) run their own vaults, and Micross sells the whole bundle (EDSP; RT-VC-DD7-02). On the re-spec route: packaged-part LTB storage management is DD2's LTB study plus a storage recommendation. A $750/month fee on $61k of packaged parts is ~14.7% a year of inventory value. I have no quote showing Micross, Rochester or distributors don't already offer storage, so the would-change-my-mind test is not met. L1 is killed. Without it Plan A is L2 rep commissions only: revised BASE m36 recurring ARR is $8,606 ($4,389 GLUT, $928 SEVERE), versus Phase 4's $42.6k. It never breaks even at $680/month of fixed cost, and cash falls below the $20k floor at m22.

*Action taken:* Named leg L1 (managed die-bank programs) killed. Model: l1_enabled 1 -> 0 (RED-TEAM REVISIONS block); new l1_buyer_cost() and model §10b compare the OEM's die-bank cost with a packaged LTB. Plan A is now L2 only. Packaged-part LTB storage advice moves into DD2's LTB study as a free paragraph, not a DD7 SKU. Deep dive §0, §2, §10, §11 and §14 revised.

### RT-VC-DD7-01 — FATAL — VC — §2.1 L1 row / §1 'the die bank' / §6 FTO #1-2 / INPUTS ltb_die_per_program 4000, gb_per_die_l1 0.75, kgd_factor 0.85, program_fee 750, program_churn 0.01, pull_margin_unit 1; L3 inputs storage_pct_yr 0.03, storage_min_yr 5000, reprobe/pack/test, bank_nre_first 120k

> L1 bills the OEM more than the die it manages are worth, and the model leaves out the pull costs it charges L3. One program is 4,000 die x 0.75 GB x 0.85 x $20.37 = $51.9k of die at the 2026 peak (hence the $2,077 4% agency fee) and ~$7.7k at the $3/GB long-run price. Over the modelled 100-month life the founder bills $75,000 of program fees, ~$4k of pull margin, $2.1k of agency fee and the $4,000 study: ~$85k, more than the die are worth at peak and ~11x their long-run value. The OEM also pays the vault (on the model's own L3 terms, 3%/yr with a $5k/yr minimum, ~$40k over 8 years) and the pull costs the model applies only to L3: $12.11 per good die (~$48k for 4,000) plus the $120k first-family test set-up (bank_nre_first), where L1 books '$1/unit on ~$10 OSAT'. That is ~$170-290k to manage ~$52k of die. Worse, §6 FTO #1-2 say re-packaged die cannot carry the OCM part number without authorization and the test program belongs to the OCM. A board OEM (§3 #6-8: Trenton, CP Technologies, WINSYSTEMS, all on packaged FBGA parts) would therefore pull a different part number, which is the requalification the LTB was meant to avoid. The rational default is the one in the DSPO data: buy packaged parts at the last order date and dry-store them. L1 is ~$34k of Plan A's $42.6k m36 ARR.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Three OEMs with die-level (not packaged-part) LTB needs, each holding >=$500k of die so the fee is <=2%/yr of inventory, signing LOIs at $750/month; or a re-specified L1 that stores packaged parts and still clears C3 with written Micross or Rochester terms.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct. Model §10b reproduces it. Over the 100-month life the founder bills $85.1k: $75k of fees, $2.1k of agency fee, the $4k study and $4k of pull margin. The die are worth $51.9k at the 2026 peak and ~$7.7k at the $3/GB long-run price (4,000 x 0.75 GB x 0.85 x $3). Adding the vault, U.S. package/test and the $40-120k set-up gives the OEM $230k-347k to manage ~$52k of die ($186k-289k if drawn in 50 months), against ~$64k for a packaged LTB plus storage. Checking this also found a Phase 4 bug the reviewer implied: program_unit billed $1 pulls at 80/month for 100 months, which is 8,000 pulls from a 4,000-die program. Lifetime gross profit per program falls from $79.1k to $75.1k once pulls are capped, before the p_program change. The FTO point holds too. Without OCM authorization a re-packaged die cannot carry the OCM part number, and the test program belongs to the OCM. A board OEM would therefore pull a different part number, which is the requalification the LTB was meant to avoid.

*Action taken:* Named leg L1 killed (l1_enabled 1 -> 0). pull_cap_to_ltb False -> True fixes the program_unit pull bug. Model §10b documents the buyer economics. Deep dive §0, §2, §10, §11 and §14 revised.

### RT-VET-DD7-01 — FATAL — VET — §0 Recommendation (keep managed die-bank programs as a DD2 SKU) / §1 L1 / §2.1 L1 row / §3 #6-8 / INPUTS ltb_die_per_program=4000, kgd_factor=0.85, program_fee=750, pull_margin_unit=1 ('~10% on ~$10 OSAT package+test'), storage_pct_yr

> The one piece DD7 keeps, L1, asks the customer to bank the wrong form factor at a loss. The model's program is 4,000 die x 0.75 GB x $20.37 x 0.85 = $51,944 of KGD; the same parts packaged cost $61,110, so banking die saves the OEM $9,166. Against that, using the model's own inputs, the OEM pays over a 100-month program: $750/month ($75,000), the 4% agency fee ($2,077), the $4,000 study, the $1/unit pull margin ($4,000), ~$10/die of U.S. package and test ($40,000), and Micross storage at 3%/yr (~$13,000). That is ~$138k. It also needs a package-qual and test-program set-up per part family, which the model charges only to L3 ($40-120k: bank_nre_next/bank_nre_first), for a total of ~$178-258k to bank $52k of die. A 4,000-piece LTB of packaged parts, dry-packed in nitrogen at the contract manufacturer, costs the part price plus a few thousand dollars. The named L1 buyers (§3 #6 Trenton Systems, #7 CP Technologies, #8 WINSYSTEMS) build boards and servers that take packaged FBGA DRAM or DIMMs, never bare die. Their LTBs are module or component LTBs that the module vendor runs: Advantech's industrial DDR4 R-DIMM notice set EOL 2026-07-23 and LTB 2026-10-30. The only die consumers in §3 are NewSpace MCM makers (#9, 'no named buyer'). Without L1, Plan A is L2 commissions alone, $8.6k of the $42.6k BASE m36 ARR, so nothing founder-doable remains to keep as a DD2 SKU.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* At least 3 of the Track B interviewees are die-consuming hybrid, SiP or MCM makers without their own vault, and they state they would pay a third-party program manager >=$750/month. Or L1 is respecified as packaged-part/module LTB management, re-modelled without the OSAT and set-up costs, and shown to beat the free LTB handling that module vendors and distributors already provide.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Correct, and it reproduces. The program's die cost $51,944 as KGD against $61,110 packaged, a saving of $9,166. The OEM then pays ~$138k on the model's own inputs, plus a $40-120k set-up per part family that Phase 4 charged only to L3. Model §10b puts the OEM total at $186k-289k (50-month draw) or $230k-347k (100-month life): 2.9-5.4x a packaged LTB with dry or N2 storage. The named L1 buyers (#6 Trenton, #7 CP Technologies, #8 WINSYSTEMS) build boards and servers on packaged FBGA DRAM or DIMMs, and their LTBs are run by module or component vendors. The Advantech DDR4 R-DIMM notice the reviewer cites is consistent with this, though I did not verify it independently. The only die consumers in §3 are NewSpace MCM makers (#9), and §3 names no buyer. Without L1 nothing founder-doable remains to keep as a DD2 SKU, and I agree.

*Action taken:* Named leg L1 killed (see RT-HYP-DD7-01). The 'optional SKU of DD2' recommendation is withdrawn. DD2's LTB study may mention packaged-part dry storage at no charge. Re-spec of L1 as packaged-part or module LTB management is not pursued: it is DD2's study, and the founder has no evidence it beats free distributor or module-vendor LTB handling.

### RT-HYP-DD7-02 — MAJOR — HYP — §3 customers #1–#3, #5 / §7 rules that matter / §10 kill test / INPUTS fixed_pt $680, oneoff_m1 $8,500

> LTB studies for NAVSUP, Tobyhanna or NAWC DMSMS work, done under the awardee as prime, mean receiving weapon-system BOMs and part-level obsolescence data. That is normally Controlled Technical Information, so DFARS 252.204-7012 flows down to the founder's LLC. It brings NIST SP 800-171 (110 controls), an SPRS score under 252.204-7019/-7020, a FedRAMP-Moderate-equivalent mail and file tenant, and 72-hour incident reporting. The CMMC Phase 2 C3PAO requirement has been suspended since 2026-07-13, but Phase 1 self-assessment and the 800-171 obligations stay in force (McGuireWoods; Jenner). DoD's own regulatory cost model, as repeated by Secureframe, puts a small business's Level 2 self-assessment at $34,277 and the C3PAO path at $101,752. Plan A carries $680/month of fixed cost on a $40k bankroll whose cash low is $30.2k, and neither the model nor the kill test prices any of this. SAM/CAGE registration and a DD Form 2345 for export-controlled data are also missing.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A DMSMS prime or depot confirms in writing that the inputs it would share for an LTB study are not CUI/CTI. Or the model carries ~$35k one-off plus ~$500/month of enclave cost, and Plan A still stays above the $20k cash kill line (§11 #8).

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. An LTB study done for a NAVSUP, Tobyhanna or NAWC DMSMS awardee means receiving weapon-system BOMs and part-level obsolescence data. These are normally marked CUI (CTI or export-controlled), so DFARS 252.204-7012 flows down to the founder's LLC: the NIST SP 800-171 controls, an SPRS score under 252.204-7019/-7020, a compliant mail and file tenant, and 72-hour incident reporting. I could not verify the reported 2026-07-13 suspension of the CMMC Phase-2 C3PAO requirement (no search budget left), but the 800-171 obligation does not depend on it. Model §10c, on Phase 4 inputs: adding the reviewer's $35k one-off at m6 plus $500/month takes the Plan A cash low from $30.2k (m3) to -$3.7k (m6), through the $20k kill floor (§11 #8) at m6. Adding AS9120B as well (RT-HYP-DD7-03) takes it to -$40.5k. So the DoD DMSMS customers #1-3 were never reachable by a $40k part-time wedge. SAM/CAGE registration and DD Form 2345 were also missing from the plan.

*Action taken:* Customers #1-3 (DoD DMSMS work through primes) removed from every founder-funded plan. Model: variant hooks planA_extra_oneoff / planA_extra_month added, and the CUI case runs in §10c. Any reopening of DoD-facing study work needs either a prime's written statement that the inputs are not CUI/CTI, or ~$35k one-off + ~$500/month in the plan with cash still above $20k. SAM/CAGE and DD 2345 are listed as prerequisites in §11.

### RT-HYP-DD7-03 — MAJOR — HYP — §4 sales motion / §7 quality systems / §9 team / INPUTS as9120_cert $35k (Plan B only, m21–m25)

> In Plan A the founder acts as a procurement agent: they size the buy, arrange purchase of the die, store it at the vault and draw pulls through an OSAT. Defense and rugged OEMs (#6 Trenton, #7 CP Technologies, #8) flow DFARS 252.246-7007/-7008 counterfeit-prevention terms and AS5553/AS6174-style requirements to every supplier in the component chain of custody. My supplier-qualification programs, and primes', approve such an agent only with AS9120B, plus AS6081 or AS6496 depending on authorization status. DD7 puts AS9120B ($35k over ~5 months) in Plan B only, after a raise. So Plan A cannot close defense OEMs from m6 as modelled, and a $30k cash low cannot fund the certification.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Two of #6–#8 confirm in writing, or in contract language, that they would contract an uncertified program manager because custody sits entirely with Micross and the OSAT. Or Plan A is re-run with AS9120B in year 1 and still clears the §11 #8 cash floor.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. In Plan A the founder sizes the buy, arranges the purchase, places the die in a vault and draws pulls. That puts them in the component chain of custody, so defense OEMs flow down DFARS 252.246-7007/-7008 and AS5553/AS6174-type terms and would approve them only with AS9120B (plus AS6081 or AS6496). Phase 4 put AS9120B in Plan B only. Model §10c, on Phase 4 inputs: AS9120B in year 1 ($35k over m2-m6, then $8k/yr from m14) breaks the $20k cash floor at m3 and takes cash to -$3.2k at m6. I have no written statement from #6-#8 that they would contract an uncertified program manager because custody sits with Micross and the OSAT. With L1 already killed on buyer economics, this closes the last route to defense OEMs for any agent-type service in a founder-funded plan.

*Action taken:* Defense OEMs #6-#7 dropped from founder-funded plans. AS9120B (and AS6081/AS6496) is recorded as a precondition for any procurement-agent role. Model §10c shows the year-1 AS9120B variant and the combined CUI + AS9120B variant (cash low -$40.5k).

### RT-PHY-DD7-01 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 'The MCM' (nine 8Gb x8 die in a 15-20 mm BGA) / §6 FTO #4 ('conventional side-by-side 9-die substrate') / INPUTS mcm_die_per_unit 9, mcm_substrate $60, mcm_assembly $80, good_die_per_wafer (45 mm² basis)

> The MCM as described does not fit in its package. The model's own die basis is a 45 mm² DDR4 8Gb die (good_die_per_wafer = 1,251 'at 45 mm², 85% yield'; I reproduce 1,471 gross x 0.85 = 1,251). Nine of them are 405 mm² of bare silicon. That exceeds the whole 20 x 20 mm (400 mm²) envelope and is 135% of a 15 x 20 mm (300 mm²) footprint, before die spacing, bond fingers and the package-edge keep-out. Teledyne's DDR4T04G72, the deep dive's own benchmark, is a 15 x 20 x 1.92 mm, 391-ball, 0.8 mm-pitch multi-chip package, and its datasheet covers both 4 GB and 8 GB in that footprint (Teledyne DS 60S 219460 and Avnet, via search). An 8 GB x72 in 300 mm² therefore needs either 16Gb x16 die (five die, not nine) or die stacking. Stacking center-pad DDR4 die is not a 'conventional side-by-side' substrate. The upper die needs a wafer-level redistribution layer (RDL) to move its center-row pads to the edge, or a face-down window per die, and every die must be thinned to <=~100 µm [ESTIMATE: standard DRAM dual-die-package practice]. RDL is a whole-wafer step on a 300 mm bumping line. It cannot be done on singulated KGD bought from distributor stock (the §10 C4 lot), and on banked wafers it adds a process step. A side-by-side 3 x 3 array needs roughly 29 x 21 mm with spacing and bond fingers [ESTIMATE]. That is a larger part, not pin-compatible, with nine die on one command/address net instead of five for an x16 design: a signal-integrity penalty at DDR4-2400/3200. Cost. The $60 substrate and $80 assembly are side-by-side numbers. At $90 + $160 for a stacked/RDL build [ESTIMATE], my mcm_unit() re-run drops unit GM from 45.6/52.2/61.8% to 32.1/38.8/48.4% (m1/m36/m60), and to 27.3/34.5/44.7% at 85% module yield. §6's 'low risk' FTO reading no longer applies either: stacked-memory packaging (3D Plus, Teledyne, the Irvine Sensors lineage) is a patented field [UNVERIFIED].

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A substrate floorplan and quote from a U.S. hi-rel OSAT that shows an 8 GB x72 built from nine 8Gb x8 die in <=15 x 20 mm (or a larger footprint that named target buyers accept in writing). It must state the stacking and RDL steps and cost <=$140 for substrate plus assembly at 300 units/yr, with a die source that supplies whole wafers (for RDL) or pre-RDL'd KGD.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** The arithmetic is right: nine 45 mm2 die are 405 mm2 of silicon, more than a whole 20 x 20 mm package (400 mm2) and 135% of a 15 x 20 mm one. On the 60-90 mm2 die that the legacy OCMs actually make it is 180-270% (model §10f). Search confirms the benchmark: Teledyne's DDR4T04G72 is a 15 x 20 x 1.92 mm, 391-ball multi-chip package on Micron die, sold in 4 GB and 8 GB versions [REPORTED, search 2026-11-01]. An 8 GB x72 in that footprint therefore needs 16Gb die or stacking. I found no legacy OCM offering 16Gb x16 DDR4 KGD [UNVERIFIED]. Stacking center-pad DDR4 needs a wafer-level RDL or a face-down window per die plus thinning to ~100 um. RDL is a whole-wafer step, which rules out distributor KGD and needs whole wafers or pre-RDL'd die. Model §10e reproduces the reviewer's re-run exactly. Substrate $90 + assembly $160 cuts unit GM from 45.6/52.2/61.8% to 32.1/38.8/48.4% (m1/m36/m60), and to 27.3/34.5/44.7% at 85% module yield. §6's 'low risk' FTO reading is withdrawn: stacked-memory packaging (3D Plus, Teledyne, the Irvine Sensors lineage) has not been searched.

*Action taken:* mcm_substrate 60 -> 90 and mcm_assembly 80 -> 160 (stacked/RDL build) [ESTIMATE]. The §1 MCM description is corrected to a stacked or larger-footprint part needing wafer or pre-RDL die supply. §6 FTO #4 changed from 'low risk' to 'not cleared'. The L4 reopen condition requires the reviewer's OSAT floorplan and quote.

### RT-PHY-DD7-02 — MAJOR — PHY — §1 'screen it across -55 to 125 °C' / §2.1 L4 row / §5 'Where an entrant could win' #2 / INPUTS mcm_screen $150, mcm_yield 0.91

> The -55 to 125 °C claim is a refresh-physics problem, not only a screening-yield problem. JEDEC DDR4 (JESD79-4) defines tREFI = 7.8 µs up to 85 °C and 3.9 µs up to 95 °C Tcase, and nothing above [UNVERIFIED-training]. DRAM cell retention falls roughly exponentially with temperature (Hamamoto et al., IEEE TED 1998; Liu et al., ISCA 2013) [UNVERIFIED-training]. Refresh tax. Extending JEDEC's 2x-per-10 °C convention to 125 °C needs tREFI ≈ 0.49 µs. An 8Gb die's tRFC1 is 350 ns, so the device would spend ~72% of its time refreshing (18% at 105 °C, 36% at 115 °C). Even a gentler Arrhenius fit (Ea 0.5-0.6 eV) puts the 125 °C refresh tax at 23-32% of device time. So a 125 °C x72 part has two options. (a) Derate bandwidth and availability by roughly a quarter to three-quarters when hot, and require the host controller to issue non-JEDEC refresh intervals; many SoC DDR4 controllers stop at 2x [ESTIMATE]. (b) Bin every die for retention at 125 °C. That is a die-level test at temperature, which distributor KGD probed at commercial or industrial temperature has not had. Teledyne sells the same part in two grades, -40 to 105 °C and -55 to 125 °C (search, 2026-11-01), which is what binning looks like. Bin yield enters the module as a ninth power: 98% per die gives 83.4% module yield, 95% gives 63.0%, 90% gives 38.7%. The model has a single 91% yield and one $150 screen, with no temperature-grade split. This adds to RT-VET-DD7-04 (screen fallout) a spec problem that objection does not cover: even a perfect screen does not remove the refresh tax at 125 °C.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Retention-time distributions at 105 and 125 °C on >=100 Winbond or ESMT 8Gb DDR4 die (or a vendor's hot-retention bin data), showing >=98% of die hold data at <=2x JEDEC refresh at 125 °C. Plus an MCM datasheet target that states the 125 °C refresh interval and bandwidth derate, accepted in writing by >=1 NewSpace buyer.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid in substance. JEDEC DDR4 defines tREFI 7.8 us to 85 C and 3.9 us to 95 C Tcase, and nothing above that (SK hynix datasheets via search, 2026-11-01) [REPORTED]. Model §10e reproduces the refresh tax with tRFC1 = 350 ns. Extending 2x per 10 C past 95 C gives 18%, 36% and 72% of device time at 105, 115 and 125 C. An Arrhenius fit with Ea 0.5-0.6 eV gives 23-32% at 125 C. One partial pushback: automotive-grade DRAM vendors do ship parts rated above 95 C with derated refresh [UNVERIFIED-training], so 125 C is a bin-and-derate problem, not an impossibility. But that confirms the reviewer's main point: every die needs a hot-retention bin, which distributor KGD probed at commercial or industrial temperature has not had. Bin yield enters as a ninth power. On Phase 4 inputs, 98%/die gives 0.834 module yield (GM 40.6/47.9/58.3%); 95% gives 0.630 (21.4/31.0/44.8%); 90% gives 0.387 (GM -27.9/-12.2/+10.2%).

*Action taken:* mcm_yield 0.91 -> 0.76, carrying a 3%/die fallout from the 125 C bin and screen together with RT-VET-DD7-04. The MCM's lead grade becomes -40 to 105 C, with 125 C as a binned option whose datasheet must state the refresh interval and bandwidth derate. The L4 reopen condition requires hot-retention bin data on >=100 die.

### RT-PHY-DD7-03 — MAJOR — PHY — §0 POSSIBLE ('Yes (TRL 9)') / §1 'Buy and store' (10-20 years unsawn in nitrogen) / INPUTS pull_yield 0.95 / §11 #11 (>=20% unit-GM buy rule)

> The 10-20-year bare-die storage premise rests on evidence about a different object. pull_yield = 0.95 cites Rochester's study of packaged parts that still worked after up to 17 years (DigiKey blog); there, mold compound covers the bond pads. A banked DRAM wafer has exposed aluminium pads. They already carry probe scrub from two or three sort insertions (hot, cold, post-repair) [ESTIMATE: standard DRAM sort flow], and §1's plan adds a re-probe touchdown at pull. The bare-die numbers the deep dive itself cites point the other way. Infineon's own limit for unsawn wafers in nitrogen is 36 months. Micross's '20+ years' is a marketing claim with no published yield. IEC 62435-5 (die and wafer storage) has clauses on bare-die deterioration mechanisms, inert-atmosphere purity, wire bondability and storage time limits, and says compliant storage does not guarantee working parts (VDE and BSI previews, search 2026-11-01). I found no published wire-bond pull or yield data for DRAM die after >=10 years in nitrogen. The physical risks are halogen-residue pad corrosion, a thickened or contaminated aluminium surface, and probe-mark damage that lowers wire-bond pull strength, plus moisture uptake in polyimide overcoats. A 95% electrical pull yield captures none of these bondability risks. Sensitivity (my bank_standalone() re-run, BASE, bought m20): pull yield 0.90 gives unit GM 36.2% and IRR 0.8%; 0.85 gives 32.5%, -1.1% IRR and a 0.95x cash multiple; 0.80 gives 28.3% and -3.2%. Every one of those passes the >=20% unit-GM buy rule (§11 #11), so the rule cannot catch a storage-yield miss. 'TRL 9' holds for Rochester's packaged-part practice and for multi-year wafer storage. For 10-20-year bare DRAM die it is closer to TRL 6-7 [judgment]. The model also carries no surveillance cost: periodic sample assembly and test of each stored lot.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Bond-pull/shear and electrical-yield data on DRAM (or comparable aluminium-pad CMOS) wafers stored >=10 years in nitrogen, for example from Micross or Rochester, showing >=95% assembled yield and bond strength within MIL-STD-883 TM2011 limits. Plus a written vault quote that includes periodic surveillance sampling, carried in the model.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. pull_yield 0.95 cited Rochester's packaged-part study, where mold compound covers the pads. A banked wafer has exposed aluminium pads already carrying probe scrub from sort. Infineon's own limit for unsawn wafers in N2 is 36 months, and IEC 62435 has a whole part on deterioration mechanisms and says compliant storage does not guarantee working parts. I found no published bond-pull or yield data on DRAM die after >=10 years in nitrogen. Model §10d reproduces the reviewer's re-runs on Phase 4 inputs exactly: pull yield 0.90 gives GM 36.2% and IRR 0.8%; 0.85 gives 32.5%, -1.1% and 0.95x; 0.80 gives 28.3% and -3.2%. All of them pass the >=20% unit-GM rule, so that rule could not catch a storage miss. A $5k/yr surveillance programme (annual sample assembly, test, bond pull/shear) alone takes the Phase 4 IRR from 2.6% to -0.2%. 'TRL 9' holds for Rochester's packaged practice and for multi-year wafer storage. For 10-20-year bare DRAM die it is TRL 6-7.

*Action taken:* pull_yield 0.95 -> 0.90; surveillance_yr 0 -> $5,000 per bank per year. POSSIBLE verdict revised: 10-20-year bare-die DRAM storage is TRL 6-7. Bank buy rule (§11 #11) rewritten to need unit GM >=20% and a 10-year IRR above the hurdle at pull yield 0.85. That rule change is moot in the model because no bank passes even the old rule.

### RT-VC-DD7-02 — MAJOR — VC — §5 'Micross sells the vault direct' / §4 sales motion / §10 C3 / INPUTS p_program 0.35, program_churn 0.01

> The vault owner already sells the whole L1 bundle, not just storage. Micross's Program Sustainment Management covers BOM monitoring with EOL/PCN notifications, long-term die and wafer storage, and supplier-managed inventory. Its EOL Die Sustainment Program (EDSP) procures the LTB of die and wafers, charges an annual storage fee under a long-term agreement, holds die for 20+ years and ships fully processed die on demand (micross.com PSM and EDSP pages; EDSP flyer). That is the LTB study, procurement, vault and pulls end to end, from an IBAS-funded incumbent ($134.3M ceiling, §5). The model still books 35% study-to-program conversion and 1%/month program churn (100-month life) for a part-time intermediary adding a $750/month layer that §5 itself says 'the OEM can remove'. The exiting OCM routes EOL demand to established houses: Micron used ATP (2015, 2018), Alliance Memory and EDX Electronics' LTB program; Intelligent Memory chose Rochester (2024) and Sourceability and Flip (2026).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Micross (or Rochester) written terms under C3 that let a third-party program manager keep >=$500/month per program on customers it introduces, plus >=2 OEMs stating they would contract through the program manager rather than EDSP directly.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Confirmed by search (2026-11-01, Micross pages and EDSP flyer via snippets) [REPORTED]. Micross's EOL Die Sustainment Program procures the LTB of die and wafers for OEMs and contract manufacturers. It holds them in nitrogen for 20+ years for an annual storage fee under a long-term agreement, offers consignment or pay-on-demand billing, keeps the authorized chain of custody, and ships drop-in die or fully packaged parts from the original qualified die. Program Sustainment Management adds BOM monitoring and supplier-managed inventory. That is the whole L1 bundle, sold by an IBAS-funded incumbent with a $134.3M ceiling. A part-time $750/month layer adds nothing an OEM cannot buy direct. Model §10c, on Phase 4 inputs: p_program 0.35 -> 0.10 cuts Plan A m36 ARR from $42.6k to $18.3k. Conversations per program rise from 28.6 to 100 (~136 -> ~475 founder hours). The exiting OCM's routing of EOL demand to ATP, Alliance Memory and EDX (Micron) and to Rochester, Sourceability and Flip (Intelligent Memory) points the same way.

*Action taken:* p_program 0.35 -> 0.10 (used only in what-ifs, since L1 is off). Micross is recorded as a direct competitor for L1, not a white-label partner. Track C3 is moot. Sources logged in research/sources/p5r-legacy-dram-diebank.jsonl.

### RT-VC-DD7-03 — MAJOR — VC — §0 recommendation ('optional SKU of DD2's LTB study line'; 'run the 29-day kill test only alongside DD2') / §10 dates / DD6 model founder_hours_pt

> Keeping DD7 alive as a side line breaks the PRIMARY. DD7 Plan A uses ~20-30 founder hours a month (prospect_hours_pt 15, study_hours 10 per study, program_hours 2 per program, acct_hours 0.5 per account), and DD6's BASE uses all 65. Re-running DD6 with 10 hours diverted (founder_hours_pt 55) moves full-time from m23 to m33 (ARR $145,428); with 20 diverted (45 h) the founder is never full-time ($122,847). DD7's ~50-hour kill test (2026-11-02 -> 11-30) collides with DD6's ~55 hours on the same dates and DD2's ~55 hours to 12-01. The host it is to be attached to, DD2, was DOWNGRADED in round 1 ('a $249 feature distributors give away'). In return, Plan A never pays the founder ($42.6k ARR at m36).

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* DD6's model re-run with DD7's hours carved out still reaching full-time by m30, or an explicit decision to run DD7's test only after DD6's K1 result is in and DD6 has failed.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Reproduced exactly with DD6's own model (model §10h imports models/private-moe-appliance.py). At 65 h/month DD6 goes full-time at m23 with $253.7k m36 ARR. At 55 h it goes full-time at m33 ($145.4k). At 45 h it never goes full-time ($122.8k). DD7's Plan A used ~20-30 founder hours a month, and its ~50-hour kill test (2026-11-02 to 11-30) collides with DD6's and DD2's tests on the same dates. Keeping DD7 as a side line would cost the PRIMARY 10 months, or its full-time switch altogether, in exchange for a wedge that never pays the founder. DD2, the host DD7 was to be attached to, was itself downgraded in round 1.

*Action taken:* DD7 gets zero founder hours. The 29-day kill test is withdrawn (saving ~$6k and ~50 hours). Any residual L2 rep line may be considered only after DD6's K1 result is in and DD6 has failed, and only after the K0 employer carve-out. §0, §10 and §11 rewritten.

### RT-VC-DD7-04 — MAJOR — VC — §10 Track A pass test / §1 'who is allowed to sell to DoD' / §7 DFARS 252.246-7008

> Track A can pass without the moat. Its pass bar is '>=1 of 6 offers a rep or distribution agreement and >=1 quotes wafer or KGD terms'. A commission rep line (L2) or a KGD price list is not authorized-aftermarket status under DFARS 252.246-7008, which requires the OCM's permission to make or sell the part after EOL using transferred wafers, tooling or test data (§1, ERAI). So the only scored moat (C-91, moat 4) is not tested by the experiment meant to kill it. The OCMs most likely to reply are extending lines, not exiting: Winbond is sold out through 2027 with customers seeking LTAs to 2030, and ISSI supports DDR4/LPDDR4 to 2038. They have no EOL to authorize. The exiting OCM, Micron, has given its EOL continuity to ATP, Alliance Memory and EDX Electronics.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A's pass bar rewritten to require a written OCM statement of willingness to grant authorized-aftermarket (or authorized-distributor-for-EOL) status on a named part, and one of the six OCMs meeting it.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. As with RT-HYP-DD7-05 and RT-VET-DD7-05, Track A's pass bar ('a rep or distribution agreement' and 'wafer or KGD terms') does not test authorized-aftermarket status under DFARS 252.246-7008. That status needs the OCM's permission to make or sell the part after EOL using transferred wafers, tooling or test data. The reviewer adds evidence that the moat is unlikely to be granted at all. The OCMs likely to reply are extending lines, not exiting: Winbond is sold out through 2027 with customers asking for LTAs to 2030 (TrendForce, 2026-08-07), and ISSI supports DDR4/LPDDR4 to 2038. They have no EOL to authorize. The exiting OCM, Micron, has placed EOL continuity with established houses: e2v (2013), ATP (2015, 2018), Alliance Memory and EDX Electronics. The EDX item is reviewer-cited; I did not verify it. I put the chance that a new U.S. entity gets authorized-aftermarket rights on a DRAM part within 15 months at <=5% [ESTIMATE].

*Action taken:* Track A rewritten (reopen condition: a written OCM statement of willingness to grant authorized-aftermarket or authorized-distributor-for-EOL status on a named part). C-91's moat (score 4) is recorded as unsupported for an entrant. 'What must be true' #1 is restated.

### RT-VET-DD7-02 — MAJOR — VET — §1 'A minority cannot swap: the die is locked' / notes §0 finding 1 / §5 'Where an entrant could win' #1 / §10 Track B questions and pass line / §11 #1, #9

> 'Locked die' is wrong for two of its three cases. On a DO-254 avionics board or in an FDA-validated device, what is qualified is a packaged part number: package construction, substrate, mold compound, wire, MSL rating, marking and the OCM's final test. A banked die re-packaged in a new FBGA at a U.S. OSAT is a different part. Its construction change triggers the delta qualification or change-impact review the buyer was trying to avoid, unless the aftermarket maker holds OCM-transferred package designs and test programs (Rochester's model), which §6 says come only with an OCM authorization. So without authorization a die bank cannot serve the DO-254 or FDA lock at all. Die are truly locked only inside hybrids and MCMs, and those makers (Teledyne under its Micron life-extension program since 2013, Mercury, Frontgrade, and Micross with ~15M die at Apopka) already run their own banks. Track B asks 'how many DRAM obsolescence cases had no acceptable JEDEC substitute?' Every board-level DMSMS manager with a locked packaged part will answer yes, so the track can pass on demand a re-packaged die cannot serve.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B rewritten to ask: 'Would you accept a part built by a third party from banked die in a new package, without re-qualifying the board, and what delta qualification would you require?' Then >=3 of 10 interviewees answering in writing that they would accept it with no more than a paper delta qualification.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. On a DO-254 avionics board or an FDA-validated device, the qualified item is a packaged part number: package construction, substrate, mold compound, wire, MSL rating, marking and the OCM's final test. A banked die re-packaged in a new FBGA at a U.S. OSAT is a different part. Its construction change triggers the delta qualification or change-impact review the buyer wanted to avoid, unless the maker holds OCM-transferred package designs and test programs, which come only with authorization (§6). Unauthorized die banking can therefore serve only hybrids and MCMs. Those makers run their own banks: Teledyne under its Micron life-extension program since 2013, Mercury, Frontgrade, and Micross with ~15M die at Apopka. Track B's question would have scored demand that a re-packaged die cannot serve.

*Action taken:* The §1 'locked die' claim is corrected (via §14): for DO-254/FDA the lock is on the packaged part, so an unauthorized die bank cannot serve it. Track B rewritten as the reviewer proposes ('Would you accept a part built by a third party from banked die in a new package, without re-qualifying the board, and what delta qualification would you require?'). It needs >=3 of 10 written yes answers with no more than a paper delta, and it is now a reopen condition.

### RT-VET-DD7-03 — MAJOR — VET — §2.1 L3 row / §2.2 bank table / §10 Track C1 pass line (<=$15/die) / §11 #3 / INPUTS reprobe_unit=1.50, pack_unit=6, test_unit=4, bank_nre_first=120000

> The $11.50/die pull cost (re-probe $1.50, package $6, test $4, over 95% yield) prices a commercial re-package, not a part a DFARS buyer pays 2.5x for as authorized aftermarket. Meeting the OCM datasheet needs five things the stack leaves out. (a) Re-probe on the OCM's wafer-sort program with a product-specific probe card; DRAM test modes and fuse/repair readout are vendor-confidential. (b) Window-BGA (board-on-chip) assembly, because DDR3/DDR4 die are center-pad designs; few U.S. low-volume OSATs run that flow. (c) At-speed test at hot and cold, not a single insertion. (d) Burn-in, which the L3 stack omits although the MCM stack and Mercury's datasheet (100% burn-in) include it. (e) A JESD47 qualification (HTOL, temperature cycling, HAST, ESD and latch-up) on the new package. I put (c) and (d) at another ~$10-20/die at 2,000 units [ESTIMATE]. I re-ran bank_standalone() (BASE, bought m20) with total pull cost raised. At $15/die, exactly C1's pass line, unit GM is 30.8%, 10-year IRR -0.5% and cash multiple 0.98x. At ~$21.50 it is 14.5% GM and -7.2% IRR; at $30, -6.8% GM and -19.9% IRR. So C1 can pass a bank the model itself says loses money, and the bank-line decision rests on an incomplete cost stack.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Written quotes from a U.S. OSAT and test house (e.g. QP Technologies, Integra) for window-BGA assembly, 3-temperature at-speed test and burn-in of a DDR4 x16 at <=$11.50/die all-in at 2,000 units, plus an OCM letter transferring probe and test programs. And C1's pass line reset to the model's break-even (~$11-12/die all-in).

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid, and model §10d reproduces the reviewer's re-runs on Phase 4 inputs exactly. All-in pull cost of $15/die gives unit GM 30.8%, IRR -0.5% and 0.98x. $21.50 gives 14.5% and -7.2%. $30 gives -6.8% and -19.9%. On Phase 4 inputs the IRR break-even is $14.48/die all-in (unit GM is zero at $27.30), so C1's $15 pass line would have passed a bank that the model itself says loses money. The stack also omitted wafer-sort-program re-probe with a product probe card, window-BGA (board-on-chip) assembly for center-pad die, hot and cold at-speed insertions, burn-in and JESD47 qualification. I carry (c)+(d) at $10/die, the low end of the reviewer's $10-20. With all Phase 5 revisions together (pull $21.50, yield 0.90, 2.0x multiple, $5k/yr surveillance, whole-wafer opening), the bank bought at m20 in BASE has unit GM -12.8%, IRR -26.6% and a 0.10x cash multiple. GLUT is -109% GM and -99.4% IRR. The IRR break-even pull cost on revised inputs is $1.53/die, below even the Rochester-scale $2. No bank is bought in any scenario. A title-taking bank at U.S. low-volume pull costs is not viable for an entrant.

*Action taken:* Named leg L3 (title-taking bank) killed. burnin_hotcold_unit 0 -> $10/die. Track C1 is withdrawn, and the reopen condition is an all-in quote at or below the revised break-even. Plan B carries no bank in any scenario (model §5 and §10d). New pull_breakeven() helper.

### RT-VET-DD7-04 — MAJOR — VET — §1 'The MCM' / §2.1 L4 row / §2.3 design-in grid / §0 POSSIBLE (no radiation or -55 to 125 C data) / INPUTS mcm_yield=0.91 ('99% KGD -> ~91% module'), kgd_factor=0.85, mcm_screen=150, mcm_nre_total

> A 91% module yield assumes each of 9 die is 99% good after a -55 to 125 C screen. Commercial Winbond/ESMT KGD bought from distributor stock (the Suntsu lot of 175 die in §10) are wafer-probed to commercial or industrial temperature. They are not burned in, and they are not screened at 125 C, where DDR4 already needs 2x refresh above 85 C and JEDEC specifies nothing above 95 C Tcase. The hot screen therefore happens after assembly, on a sealed 9-die module that cannot be reworked. Per-die fallout of 3-5% [ESTIMATE, typical of upscreened commercial DRAM] gives 0.97^9 = 76% to 0.95^9 = 63% module yield. I re-ran mcm_unit(). At 76% yield, unit cost is $587/$515/$412 at m1/m36/m60 (GM 34.8/42.8/54.2%). At 63% it is $708/$621/$497 (GM 21.4/31.0/44.8%), against the model's 45.6/52.2/61.8%. If KGD prices at parity with packaged parts rather than 0.85x, the m1 GM at 76% is 30.8%. The heritage bar is also per lot, not one-off. Teledyne's DDR4 qualifies up to NASA Level 1 under the EEE-INST-002 PEM section, with SEL >60 MeV.cm2/mg and 100 krad TID, on Micron die under a life-extension agreement that controls die revision. Distributor KGD carries no revision lock, so radiation and temperature data must be repeated for each die revision and wafer lot, a recurring cost the one-time $1.4M NRE does not carry.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written Winbond or ESMT KGD supply agreement with die-revision change notification and wafer-lot traceability. Plus 125 C screen data on a commercial KGD lot showing <=1% per-die fallout, gathered when the MCM is revisited with the hi-rel co-founder.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. A 91% module yield assumed each of nine die is 99% good after a -55 to 125 C screen. Distributor KGD is wafer-probed at commercial or industrial temperature, without burn-in or a 125 C screen, so the hot screen happens after sealing, on a 9-die part that cannot be reworked. Model §10e reproduces the reviewer's re-runs on Phase 4 inputs. Module yield 0.76 gives unit cost $586/$515/$412 and GM 34.8/42.8/54.3% (the reviewer's 54.2% is rounding). Yield 0.63 gives 21.4/31.0/44.8%. KGD at parity at 0.76 yield gives 30.8% at m1. The heritage point also holds. Teledyne's die-revision control comes from a Micron life-extension agreement. Distributor KGD has no revision lock, so TID, SEL and hot-retention data recur per die revision and wafer lot, a cost the one-time NRE did not carry. Combining this with RT-PHY-DD7-01 and -02, revised MCM GM is 18.7/26.7/38.2% (m1/m36/m60).

*Action taken:* mcm_yield 0.91 -> 0.76 (0.97^9); new mcm_lot_requal_yr $75k/yr from the evaluation lot onward [ESTIMATE: one TID + SEL + hot-retention campaign a year]. The L4 reopen condition adds a KGD supply agreement with die-revision change notification and wafer-lot traceability, plus <=1% per-die fallout at a 125 C screen.

### RT-VET-DD7-05 — MAJOR — VET — §10 Track A pass line / §11 #2, #4, #9 (m15 'DRAM OCM letter') / §2.7 #1 / §1 'That permission is the moat'

> Track A is C-91's only test of the moat, and it can pass without anyone granting the moat. It passes if '>=1 of 6 offers a rep or distribution agreement and >=1 quotes wafer or KGD terms'. Intelligent Memory and Alliance Memory sell through reps and franchised distributors as a matter of course: IM added Sourceability and Flip in 2026 (§4). ESMT and Winbond KGD are already listed at distributors (Suntsu; 01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 10). A rep agreement makes the founder a sales agent, and a KGD quote makes them a customer. Neither is what DFARS 252.246-7008 ranks ahead of independents: an authorized aftermarket manufacturer, holding OCM permission to make and mark the part after EOL with transferred wafers and test programs (§1, §6 #1). The m15 gate's 'DRAM OCM letter' has the same ambiguity, and it is the gate that unlocks a $1.5M raise. §5 also concedes that the six named OCMs are extending their legacy lines, not exiting them, so none has a reason to appoint an aftermarket maker now.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track A and the m15 gate both require a written statement from >=1 OCM that it would license EOL wafers or die, test programs and part-number marking rights to a new U.S. entity as an authorized aftermarket manufacturer, with indicative terms. A rep or distribution offer is scored only as L2.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. This is the third reviewer to make the point (with RT-HYP-DD7-05 and RT-VC-DD7-04). Track A and the m15 'DRAM OCM letter' gate, which would have unlocked a $1.5M raise, can both pass on a rep agreement or a KGD quote, and neither is the moat DFARS ranks ahead of independents. §5 already conceded that the six named OCMs are extending their legacy lines, so none has a reason to appoint an aftermarket maker now. The test design would have let the most important assumption pass untested.

*Action taken:* Track A and the m15 gate (now reopen conditions) both require a written statement from >=1 OCM that it would license EOL wafers or die, test programs and part-number marking rights to a new U.S. entity as an authorized aftermarket manufacturer, with indicative terms. A rep or distribution offer is scored only as L2 (§10, §11).

### RT-HYP-DD7-04 — MINOR — HYP — §3 demand / §12 cycle test / INPUTS aftermarket_mult 2.5, mult_compress, sell_rate_yr, demand_mult

> The model leaves out the supply swing that hyperscalers control. Round 1 established that we are keeping DDR4 now (Meta's Vistara CXL reuse, RT-HYP-DD1-04), which tightens 2026–27. But the 2019–22 Cascade Lake, Ice Lake and Milan fleets retire into ITAD in 2028–30, exactly the bank's m38+ sell window in every scenario, BASE included. Recovered packaged DDR4 caps the price for every buyer that DFARS does not bind. Those buyers are rugged, industrial-SBC, medical and rail (#6–#10): 5 of the 10 named customers, and the only ones reachable without an OCM letter (#4 is 'unreachable'). The 2.5× authorized multiple therefore lives mostly in the DoD slice DD7 cannot reach. Yet the model applies it to all sales and compresses it only ×0.9/×0.8 in a glut. I rate it MINOR because DD7 already concludes the bank fails. It also removes the BASE m20 bank: per §2.2, ~1.5× is break-even, not 39.6% GM.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Track B shows ≥3 non-DFARS buyers whose quality agreements exclude recovered or refurbished DRAM and who would pay ≥2.5× for authorized supply.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The bank sells from m38 (sell_lag 18 months after an m20 purchase), which is exactly when the 2019-22 Cascade Lake, Ice Lake and Milan fleets retire through ITAD (2028-30). Recovered packaged DDR4 caps the price for every buyer DFARS does not bind: rugged, industrial SBC, medical and rail (#6-#10). The 2.5x authorized multiple therefore applies mainly to the DoD slice, and that slice needs an OCM letter. Model §10d, on Phase 4 inputs (bank bought m20, BASE): at 1.5x, unit GM is -0.7% and IRR -15.3% (no buy). At a 2.0x blend, GM is 24.5%, which still passes the >=20% rule, but IRR is -5.0% and the cash multiple 0.77x. So the BASE bank's 2.6% IRR depended wholly on 2.5x across all sales.

*Action taken:* aftermarket_mult 2.5 -> 2.0 [ESTIMATE: 50/50 blend of 2.5x DFARS-bound and ~1.5x non-DFARS buyers]. The BASE m20 bank is no longer bought (revised model §5 and §10d). §12 cycle text notes ITAD supply in the sell window.

### RT-HYP-DD7-05 — MINOR — HYP — §10 Track A pass rule / §8 m15 Stage-1 gate ('a DRAM OCM letter') / §2.7 #1

> Track A passes if ≥1 of 6 makers offers 'a rep or distribution agreement' and ≥1 quotes wafer or KGD terms. Both are easy to get, and neither says anything about the moat. Intelligent Memory and Alliance appoint distributors and reps routinely; IM added Sourceability and Flip in 2026 (§4). KGD is already listed at distributors (Suntsu, §10). DFARS priority comes only with authorized-aftermarket status, where the OCM transfers wafers, test programs and marking rights for a named EOL part (ERAI definition, §1). The m15 'OCM letter' gate is just as loose. So the test can pass while C-91 is false, and the Stage-1 file would then rest on a rep agreement.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* Rewrite Track A and the m15 gate to require written willingness to grant aftermarket or continuity rights (die plus test-program transfer, and permission to mark) for at least one named EOL DDR3/DDR4 part.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. A rep agreement makes the founder a sales agent, and a KGD quote makes them a customer. Neither is authorized-aftermarket status, where the OCM transfers wafers, test programs and marking rights for a named EOL part (ERAI definition, §1). Intelligent Memory and Alliance appoint reps and distributors routinely (IM added Sourceability and Flip in 2026), and ESMT KGD is already listed at Suntsu. So Track A and the m15 'OCM letter' gate could both pass while C-91's moat is false. With DD7 killed, the 29-day test is not run, but the rewritten Track A becomes the reopen condition. It requires a written OCM statement of willingness to grant aftermarket or continuity rights (die plus test-program transfer, and permission to mark) on at least one named EOL DDR3/DDR4 part to a new U.S. entity, with indicative terms. A rep or distribution offer scores only as L2. The rewritten bar is far less likely to be met, because the six named OCMs are extending their legacy lines rather than exiting (§5). That lowers the moat's probability, not just the test's rigor.

*Action taken:* Track A pass bar and the m15 Stage-1 gate rewritten as reopen conditions (§10, §11). A rep or distribution offer counts only toward L2. C-91's moat score of 4 is recorded as unsupported for an entrant (§14).

### RT-HYP-DD7-06 — MINOR — HYP — §10 kill test / §11 kill criteria / §9 team (no employment-agreement review)

> DD6 makes an employer carve-out its first gate (K0), as round 1 did for DD2 (RT-VC-DD2-05). DD7 has no such gate, although the founder's employer is a design/manufacturing company, the same OEM type as targets #6–#8. L2 earns commissions on DRAM sold to OEMs, and L1 manages their LTB buys. Under a standard invention-assignment and conflict-of-interest agreement, a rep commission on parts the employer might buy, or pitching its suppliers or competitors, is a textbook conflict.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A ~$500 employment-agreement review and a written carve-out covering component representation and LTB services, added as Step 0b with kill-on-refusal.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. The founder's day job is product development and operations at a design/manufacturing company, the same OEM type as targets #6-#8. A rep commission on DRAM sold to OEMs (L2), or managing an OEM's LTB buys (L1), is a textbook conflict under a standard invention-assignment and conflict-of-interest agreement. That holds especially if the employer buys from the represented maker or competes with the prospects. DD2 (RT-VC-DD2-05) and DD6 already gate on this; DD7 did not.

*Action taken:* New Step 0b / K0: a ~$500 employment-agreement review and a written employer carve-out covering component representation and LTB services, before any rep or OEM conversation; kill on refusal (§10, §11). Model: oneoff_m1 8,500 -> 3,900 ($500 employer review + $900 rep-agreement review + $2,500 LLC/rep legal; the $6,000 kill test is withdrawn). Economic effect ~$500.

### RT-HYP-DD7-07 — MINOR — HYP — §3 'sanity ceiling' / model §6 DMSMS sanity check

> The $6.3–25.4M/yr of 'DoD-tracked memory life-of-need demand' rests on an unsourced '~10% of DSPO cases are memory' and an assumed $50–200k per buy. Even taken as given, those life-of-need buys are packaged parts from franchised distributors or Rochester stock, not a die-bank market. The same DSPO table also shows simple substitution resolving 2.2× as many cases as life-of-need buys (2,809 vs 1,268). The figure overstates what a die bank can address and should not anchor the C-91 ceiling.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A DSPO or GIDEP breakdown showing the memory share of cases, and the share of memory life-of-need buys met from die or wafers rather than packaged stock.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The $6.3-25.4M a year rests on an unsourced '~10% of DSPO cases are memory' and an assumed $50-200k per buy. It also measures the value of life-of-need buys, which are mostly packaged parts from franchised distributors or Rochester stock, not die-bank demand. DSPO's own table shows simple substitution resolving 2.2x as many cases as life-of-need buys (2,809 vs 1,268). Model §10i restates it as the founder's fee pool. ~127 memory life-of-need cases a year x $9.0k of program ARR = $1.14M of new ARR a year at 100% national share, plus $0.25-1.01M a year of one-off 4% agency fees. A 3-5% share gives $34-57k of new ARR a year. Because die-bank programs are now killed, even that pool is not addressable by this concept.

*Action taken:* §3's 'sanity ceiling' is withdrawn as a C-91 anchor and restated as a fee-pool ceiling (model §10i). DSPO substitution share added to §14.

### RT-PHY-DD7-04 — MINOR — PHY — §1 'Pull. On an order, re-probe the wafer, saw it, package the die' / INPUTS sell_rate_yr 0.15, pull_units_per_program 80, bank_wafers 10, reprobe/pack/test per unit

> 'Pull on demand' cannot be done one order at a time from unsawn wafers, so most banked die spend most of their life in exactly the state the storage case avoids. Thinning, sawing and (for stacks) RDL are whole-wafer steps, so opening a wafer releases ~1,251 die. The model sells 15% a year of 12,510 die (~156 a month in BASE), and an L1 program draws 80 die a month. Each opened wafer therefore sits for ~8 months (L3) to ~16 months (L1) as singulated die on dicing tape, in waffle packs or in gel-paks. Dicing and UV tape adhesion and residue change with age; tape makers typically state shelf life in months, not years [ESTIMATE/UNVERIFIED]. Singulated die also face handling, edge-chipping and contamination risks that unsawn wafers do not. Each alternative has a cost the model leaves out: (a) package the whole wafer when it is opened and store packaged parts, the mode Rochester's 17-year data actually supports. That front-loads ~1,251 x $11.50 = ~$14.4k of pull cost per wafer, plus dry-pack and MSL handling. (b) accept a shorter-life singulated-die inventory. The model books pull cost per unit sold instead. It is also unverified that the small U.S. houses named (QP Technologies, Integra) can backgrind and saw 300 mm DRAM wafers; thinning 300 mm wafers to <=100 µm needs a large-OSAT tool set.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written vault or OSAT process for partial-wafer pulls, for example sawing and re-sealing the remaining die in nitrogen with a stated shelf life of >=24 months. Or the model changed to package a whole wafer at each opening. Plus a U.S. OSAT quote for 300 mm DRAM backgrind and saw.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid on the process point. Thinning and sawing (and RDL for stacks) are whole-wafer steps, so opening a wafer releases ~1,251 die (Phase 4 basis) or ~736 (revised basis), which then sit for 8-16 months singulated on tape or in waffle packs. I adopted the reviewer's option (a): package the whole wafer when it is opened and store packaged parts, the mode Rochester's 17-year data supports. The model now books each wafer's full pull cost when it is opened. The money effect is small: on Phase 4 inputs the BASE IRR moves from 2.64% to 2.57%, and the cash multiple stays 1.13x. Whether QP Technologies or Integra can backgrind and saw 300 mm DRAM wafers is still unverified, and it is now a reopen condition.

*Action taken:* pull_whole_wafer False -> True; new pull_charge() books a wafer's pull cost at opening in both simulate() and bank_standalone(). A U.S. 300 mm backgrind/saw quote is added to the L3 reopen conditions.

### RT-PHY-DD7-05 — MINOR — PHY — §10 Track C4 (Suntsu ESMT KGD lot) / §2.7 #1 (OCM list) / INPUTS good_die_per_wafer (45 mm²), mcm_die_per_unit 9, bank_wafers 10

> The die the kill test would buy is not the die the model uses, and the model's die is not one the plausible OCM partners make. (1) The Suntsu listing cited in §10 C4 is 'KGD 256Mbx16 DDR4' (URL slug): a 4Gb x16 die. An 8 GB x72 built from 4Gb x16 die needs at least 18 die, and 20 in a practical four-rank build (four data die plus one ECC die per rank), against the MCM's nine. Buying that lot tests the availability of a die the design cannot use, and Track C2's '$400 unit build before die' gate means nothing for it. (2) 1,251 good die per 300 mm wafer assumes a 45 mm² 8Gb die at 85% yield. That is a big-three 1y/1z-class die, from the makers §5 says will not authorize a startup. The OCMs in §2.7 (Winbond, ESMT, Etron, Nanya, Alliance, Intelligent Memory) sell older-node or foundry-made die. At 60-90 mm² [ESTIMATE], a 300 mm wafer yields ~610-930 good die. A 12,510-die bank is then 13.5-20.6 wafers, not 10, and the nine-die MCM silicon is 540-810 mm², which makes RT-PHY-DD7-01 worse. The model prices die per GB, so bank IRR barely moves. Every wafer-count, vault and MCM-area figure in §1, §2.1 and §8 does.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* An OCM or distributor quote naming the exact 8Gb x8 DDR4 die that would be banked and used in the MCM (die size, node, gross and good die per wafer). And a §10 C4 purchase of that die instead of the 4Gb x16 lot.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid on both counts. (1) The Suntsu lot named in §10 C4 is 'KGD 256Mbx16 DDR4', a 4Gb x16 die. An 8 GB x72 needs 18-20 of those, so buying it tests a die the design cannot use. (2) 1,251 good die per wafer assumes a 45 mm2 big-three die. Model §10f reproduces the reviewer's range for 60-90 mm2 legacy-OCM die: 928-608 good die per 300 mm wafer, so a 12,510-die bank is 13.5-20.6 wafers. As the reviewer predicted, bank economics do not move, because die are priced per GB: on Phase 4 inputs unit GM stays 39.6% and IRR 2.6%. The wafer counts and MCM silicon area do move, and they feed RT-PHY-DD7-01.

*Action taken:* good_die_per_wafer 1,251 -> 736 (75 mm2 midpoint) and bank_wafers 10 -> 17 (keeps ~12,500 die). The C4 KGD purchase is withdrawn with the kill test. Any reopened MCM work must name the exact 8Gb x8 die (size, node, die per wafer) in an OCM or distributor quote.

### RT-VC-DD7-05 — MINOR — VC — §3 'sanity ceiling' / model §6 DMSMS sanity check

> The demand ceiling is stated in die value, not in the founder's fee pool, and its memory share is unsourced. The $6.3-25.4M a year assumes ~10% of DSPO's 1,268 life-of-need cases are memory ('if ~10% are memory'), and the DSPO case mix covers all commodities, not DRAM. Even granting it, the founder earns $750/month plus 4%: winning every one of the ~127 memory life-of-need cases a year would add ~$1.14M of program ARR (127 x $9,000) plus $0.25-1.0M of agency fees. A realistic 3-5% share is ~$35-60k of new ARR a year, which is the model's own answer. L1 + L2 is capped near $1-2M a year even at 100% national share.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A DSPO or GIDEP breakdown showing memory life-of-need cases >=3x the assumed ~127 a year, or evidence that program fees scale with program size.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid, and the same finding as RT-HYP-DD7-07. The $6.3-25.4M is die or part value with an unsourced 10% memory share, not the founder's fee pool. Model §10i: winning all ~127 memory life-of-need cases a year would add $1.14M of program ARR plus $0.25-1.01M of agency fees. A 3-5% share is $34-57k of new ARR a year, which is the Phase 4 model's own answer. Program fees do not scale with program size, so L1 + L2 were capped near $1-2M a year even at 100% national share. With L1 killed, the addressable pool for this founder is zero.

*Action taken:* §3 ceiling restated as a fee pool (model §10i). The 'Rochester-scale ceiling' selection reason for DD7 is recorded as unsupported in §14.

### RT-VC-DD7-06 — MINOR — VC — §0 'revisit the MCM' / §2.3 BULL / §5 MCM rows / INPUTS ev_rev_multiple 1.5, mcm_asp 900

> The door left open for the MCM leads to a sub-venture return even in the BULL case. BULL needs a $1,500 ASP (base $900), 4 design-ins a year of 1,000 units from m54 and a 4.0x aftermarket multiple to reach 7.31x, undiscounted, at 1.5x year-10 revenue, ten years out. That is ~22% a year gross if all capital went in at t0; staging helps, but dilution, an option pool and a hi-rel co-founder's equity hurt, and ten years runs past a normal fund life. Year-10 EBITDA is negative in every non-BULL case, so 1.5x revenue is generous. The competitor list is short: 3D Plus appears in §6 FTO but not in §5. Micron Manassas (1-alpha DDR4 for defense) and ISSI (DDR4/LPDDR4 to 2038) give a 2028 NewSpace designer a supported part, which removes the locked-die premise for new designs.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* >=3 named NewSpace or rugged buyers issuing evaluation LOIs at >=$1,200 for an unflown x72 ECC MCM, or a hi-rel co-founder who brings an existing design-in.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. Model §10g: the BULL case returns 7.31x on Phase 4 inputs and 7.22x revised, at 1.5x year-10 revenue. That is ~22.0% (21.9%) a year gross if all capital went in at t0, before dilution, an option pool and a hi-rel co-founder's equity, over a horizon longer than a normal fund life. Revised BASE Plan B is worse than Phase 4: 0.29x on $11.49M of outside capital (was 0.46x on $8.11M), GLUT 0.24x, SEVERE 0.19x, year-10 EBITDA -$1.10M, and EBITDA negative in every year. The 4 design-ins x 1,000 units grid cell falls from 4.04x to 2.73x, so not even the 'venture case' clears 3x. The competitor list was short: 3D Plus is added. Micron Manassas (U.S. 1-alpha DDR4/LP4 for defense, qualified output expected end-2026) and ISSI (to 2038) give a 2028 NewSpace designer a supported part, which erodes the locked-die premise for new designs.

*Action taken:* The open door to the MCM is narrowed. Reopen C-65 only if a hi-rel co-founder brings an existing x72 design-in, and >=3 NewSpace or rugged buyers issue evaluation LOIs at >=$1,200 for an unflown part. 3D Plus added to §5 (via §14). BULL and grid recomputed on revised inputs (model §6, §10g).

### RT-VC-DD7-07 — MINOR — VC — §2.5 Plan C / model §7 / INPUTS sba_share 0.8, acq_multiple 5, equity_share 0.1

> Plan C cannot be executed by this founder. It needs the founder full-time (the day job ends), a personal guarantee on a $3.6M SBA loan from someone with under $50k of capital, $410k of outside equity nobody has been asked for, and a target nobody has named. Resurgent, the model's template, was bought by Flip in 2023, and Lansdale makes no DRAM. The only asset that matters, the OCM licence, may carry change-of-control clauses (notes gap 8, unchecked). DSCR is 0.85 in GLUT and 0.45 in SEVERE, and the $5M SBA cap is marked 'prior knowledge, not verified'.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A named for-sale licensed manufacturer whose DRAM OCM licences survive a change of control, and a lender term sheet that accepts this founder.

**Response — ACCEPTED-KILL:** Valid. Plan C needs a full-time founder (the day job ends), a personal guarantee on a ~$3.6M SBA loan from someone with under $50k of capital, $410k of outside equity nobody has been asked for, and a target nobody has named. Resurgent, the template, went to Flip in 2023, and Lansdale makes no DRAM. The only asset that matters, the OCM licence, may carry change-of-control clauses (unchecked). Its DSCR is 0.85 in GLUT and 0.45 in SEVERE against a ~1.25 lender bar, and the $5M SBA cap is unverified. No input changes rescue this. It fails on founder constraints, not on arithmetic.

### RT-VC-DD7-08 — MINOR — VC — §2.1 L2 row / §4 channel / INPUTS commission 0.05 ('unsourced'), acct_rate_pt 0.3

> The rep line's own evidence points the other way. The only channel news cited is Intelligent Memory signing Sourceability as a global distributor and Flip as a partner (Evertiq, 2026-10-06). The maker filled its EOL channel with scaled distributors, which lowers, not raises, the odds of a solo part-time rep. The 5% commission is unsourced, accounts arrive at 0.3 a month, and commission scales with the DRAM price: $2,000/yr per account at 2026 prices, $295 at $3/GB, and $928/yr in total in SEVERE at m36.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A written rep offer from Intelligent Memory, Alliance Memory or ISSI at >=5%, including house accounts in a named territory.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Valid. The only channel evidence cited (Intelligent Memory signing Sourceability as a global distributor and Flip as a partner, Evertiq 2026-10-06) shows the maker filling its EOL channel with scaled distributors, which lowers the odds for a part-time solo rep. I found no semiconductor-rep commission benchmark: search turned up only generic 5-15% ranges for technology and manufacturing reps, so 5% stays [UNVERIFIED]. Model §10j, revised inputs, L2 only. BASE m36 recurring ARR is $8.6k (GLUT $4.4k, SEVERE $928). It never breaks even at $680/month of fixed cost and falls below the $20k cash floor at m22. A 3% commission gives $5.2k; 0.20 accounts a month gives $5.7k. A lean $300/month set-up breaks even (founder unpaid) at m15 in BASE but still earns only ~$8.6k a year. Probability-weighted at p(rep offer) = 0.25 [ESTIMATE], BASE m36 ARR is ~$2.2k.

*Action taken:* L2 is no longer part of DD7's plan. It is recorded as a contingent residual, allowed only after DD6 fails and after K0, and only with a written rep offer at >=5% that includes house accounts in a named territory. Model §10j carries the commission, account-rate and lean-cost variants.

### RT-VET-DD7-06 — MINOR — VET — §7 Quality systems ('MIL-PRF-38535 QML for hi-rel re-manufacture'; 'the model carries MCM qualification inside the $1.4M NRE') / §2.1 L4 / INPUTS mcm_assembly=80, mcm_nre_total

> A 9-die x72 module sold into QML programs is a hybrid (multichip) microcircuit under MIL-PRF-38534 (Class H/K), not MIL-PRF-38535, which covers monolithic parts. Otherwise it is a plastic part screened to NASA EEE-INST-002, as Teledyne's is. 38534 certification attaches to a DLA-audited assembly line, so a fabless entrant must build at a house that already holds it. The U.S. holders largely overlap with the competitors in §5 (e.g. Micross, Teledyne, Frontgrade, DDC; check DLA's QML-38534 listing). That hands a competitor control of cost, schedule and the substrate design. The $1.4M NRE and the $80 assembly line carry no line-certification, audit or competitor-margin item.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* A quote from an independent QML-38534 or EEE-INST-002-capable assembly house that does not sell x72 DDR4 modules, with NRE <=$2M and unit assembly plus screening <=$290.

**Response — ACCEPTED-DOWNGRADE:** Correct. A 9-die x72 module sold into QML programs is a hybrid (multichip) microcircuit under MIL-PRF-38534 (Class H/K), not MIL-PRF-38535, which covers monolithic parts. The alternative is a plastic part screened to NASA EEE-INST-002, as Teledyne's is. 38534 certification attaches to a DLA-audited assembly line, so a fabless entrant must build at a house that holds it. Per the reviewer, the U.S. holders largely overlap with the §5 competitors. I did not check DLA's QML-38534 listing, so that is [UNVERIFIED]. The $1.4M NRE and $80 assembly carried no line-access, audit or competitor-margin item. With RT-PHY-DD7-01's stacked build, assembly plus screening is now $310/unit, above the reviewer's $290 bar.

*Action taken:* §7 corrected to MIL-PRF-38534 / EEE-INST-002 (via §14). mcm_nre_total $1.4M -> $1.6M for QML-38534 line access [ESTIMATE]. The L4 reopen condition adds a quote from an independent 38534 or EEE-INST-002-capable house that does not sell x72 DDR4, with NRE <=$2M and assembly plus screening <=$290.

### RT-VET-DD7-07 — MINOR — VET — §10 budget and Track C2-C4 (MCM quote; ~$2,500 KGD lot) / §0 Recommendation / §11 #10, #12

> The 29-day test spends ~$2,500 of its ~$6,000 (about 42%), plus founder hours, on L4 evidence: an MCM substrate quote and a distributor KGD lot. Yet §0 shelves L4 until a hi-rel co-founder and ~$5M appear (m16 at the earliest). The KGD lot tests only distributor availability, which 01_LANDSCAPE §0 item 10 already confirmed ('legacy 4Gb DDR4 KGD can be bought from distributors in lots of a few hundred die'). It tests nothing about die quality at 125 C, because no screen is budgeted. For a part-time founder this money should buy more Track B interviews, aimed at die-consuming hybrid makers, and the re-packaging question in RT-VET-DD7-02.

*What would change the reviewer's mind:* The doc commits to revisiting L4 within 12 months with a named co-founder candidate, and budgets a 125 C screen of the KGD lot so the purchase produces yield data rather than an availability check.

**Response — ACCEPTED-MITIGATED:** Valid. ~$2,500 of the ~$6,000 test (~42%) bought L4 evidence, an MCM substrate quote and a distributor KGD lot, for a line §0 shelved until a hi-rel co-founder and ~$5M appear. The KGD lot tested only availability, which 01_LANDSCAPE had already confirmed, and with no 125 C screen budgeted it would have produced no yield data. It was also the wrong die (RT-PHY-DD7-05). With DD7 killed, the whole 29-day test is withdrawn, so no money goes to L4 evidence. If C-65 is ever reopened, the first spend is a 125 C screen of a KGD lot of the right die, which produces yield data rather than an availability check.

*Action taken:* Kill test withdrawn (~$6,000 and ~50 founder hours not spent). The L4 reopen sequence starts with a budgeted 125 C screen of a KGD lot of the named 8Gb x8 die (§10, §11).
